Review · Chapters 4–9

Chapters 4–9 in Review

Chapters 4 to 9 of the notes in one place: the argument that connects them, the facts and lists each chapter gives, every problem-and-fix chain and process flow, the equations with every board problem and its numbers, the places where the course and the literature differ, and a self-check at the end of each chapter.

Prerequisites M07–M15, with M07b Read time ~2 h with the self-checks Sources Class notes pp. 49–146 (Chapters 4–9); Lectures 11–24; modules M07–M15

Chapters 4 to 9 of the notes take the transistor of Chapters 1–3 below the point where its gate oxide, its channel doping, its lithography, its interconnect and its isolation each stop working. Chapter 4 replaces the gate and capacitor dielectric; Chapter 5 rebuilds the wells, the channel profile and the source/drain; Chapters 6 and 7 follow the lithography that prints the shrinking features; Chapter 8 flattens the wafer that lithography needs and builds the copper interconnect; Chapter 9 replaces LOCOS isolation with the shallow trench. Everything on this page is taught on a module page first. This page gathers it, keeps the notes' own values and wording, and links every item back to the section that explains it.

Sections 2 to 7 take the chapters in turn, Chapter 5 in two parts, and each has the same five parts. Facts and lists sets out what the notes enumerate or quantify, with the notes' values and any module flag in the same cell. Problems and fixes, and the process flows gives each chapter's chains — the structure, what goes wrong with it as the device shrinks, and what replaced it — and every process sequence as numbered steps with their conditions. Equations and board problems lists each equation with its units and links it to the formula sheet, then every numerical example worked or set in class, with its numbers and answer. Where the course and the literature differ collects the flagged conflicts; the course's version is the one to use in an answer, and the literature's is given beside it. The self-check closes each chapter with multiple-choice items, short answers and problems with changed numbers, each solved in full beneath its prompt.

1 · The argument of Chapters 4–9

Chapters 4 to 9 of the notes ask one question six times: the gate length shrinks, so what stops working, and what replaced it? Chapter 4 begins where Chapter 3 ended. Silicon dioxide cannot be thinned below about 1.5 nm because direct tunnelling then carries too much gate current, so the capacitance per unit area can rise only through the dielectric constant: a high-k film 10 nm thick at k = 20 has the capacitance of 1.95 nm of SiO₂ (M07 §3), and the same move to high k keeps a DRAM cell near 30 fF as its area shrinks (M07b §2). Chapter 5 rebuilds the silicon beneath the gate. Diffused wells spread sideways about 0.7 times their depth and need 12–24 h of drive-in above 1100 °C, so they gave way to retrograde wells implanted at high energy (M08 §4); below about 0.13 µm the channel needs a mean doping above 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ against punch-through and a lighter surface for mobility, which only a retrograde profile and pocket implants provide (M09 §1); and the source/drain splits into a shallow extension and a deeper silicided contact whose series resistance must stay within about 10 % of the channel's own (M10 §3). Chapters 6 and 7 follow the lithography: with F = 0.5λ/NA and DOF = 0.6λ/NA², printing smaller features meant shorter wavelengths, new resists, phase-shift masks and, below the optical limit, EUV and electron beams (M11 §3, M12 §4). The shrinking depth of focus is Chapter 8's starting point: the steps of an unplanarized interconnect stack reach 2.2 µm against a planarity requirement of ±0.5 µm, and chemical mechanical polishing removes them and makes copper damascene wiring possible (M13 §1, M14 §4). Chapter 9 replaces LOCOS, whose bird's beak and surface step it could not shrink, with the shallow trench, which CMP made possible (M15 §1).

The table below is an index by topic. Each chapter section has the same five parts, so the second column names the part where an item sits; the numbered board problems P25–P72 are in the third part of each section, and every flagged conflict in the fourth.

2 · Chapter 4: high-k dielectrics

Chapter 4 of the notes (pp. 49–67), taught in Lectures 11–15, replaces the gate oxide that Chapter 3 thinned to its limit. Its problem and fix rest on one relation, C = kε0/t: once direct tunnelling stopped SiO₂ at about 1.5 nm, only the dielectric constant could rise, and a replacement then had to meet everything else SiO₂ had met — a barrier to electrons, stability on silicon, an amorphous film, a clean interface. M07 takes the gate: the equivalent oxide thickness, the stack rule, the candidates Ta₂O₅, Al₂O₃, ZrO₂, HfO₂, HfSiON and Pr₂O₃, deposition by ALD, and the metal gate. M07b takes the memory capacitor: a DRAM cell that must keep about 30 fF as it shrinks, the trench, stack and HSG approaches, then Ta₂O₅ and barium strontium titanate capacitors with their electrodes, and the ferroelectric memory. The same series-capacitance arithmetic runs through both halves: the lowest-k layer of a stack sets the floor, whether it is the interlayer under a gate or the oxide on a polysilicon storage node. Chapter 5 (§3–§4) returns to the silicon under the gate.

2.1 Facts and lists

What high-k means, where it is used, and when SiO₂ runs out (pp. 49–50)

ItemThe course's version
Definition
M07 §1		High-k means k > 7, above Si₃N₄ (notes, after Wolf). Other conventions: any k above SiO₂'s 3.9; Robertson's k > 10, preferably 25–30. Nitrided oxides and oxynitrides (k 3.9–7) are not high-k (Lecture 11 called nitride and the oxynitrides, k ≈ 4–7, the early high-k films, used in EEPROMs; the notes' threshold is the course's)
Three uses
M07 §1		Gate dielectric replacing SiO₂ in MOSFETs; storage-capacitor dielectric of DRAM; capacitor dielectric of non-volatile ferroelectric memory (FeRAM)
Candidates named
M07 §1, §8		Si₃N₄, Al₂O₃, Ta₂O₅, ZrO₂, HfO₂, BST, PLZT (p. 49); HfSiON and Pr₂O₃ (p. 67). CeO₂ and Hf–Zr oxide from the lectures only
Why only k is left
M07 §1		C = (kε0/t). Thinning supplied every increase up to the 90 nm generation; direct tunnelling rises six to seven decades per nanometre, and a good thermal oxide reaches 1 A/cm² at about 1.4 nm (M06). Notes p. 50: SiO₂ thinner than 1.5 nm may not be feasible. A higher k allows a thicker film, less tunnelling, the same C and the same drive current
Dating (p. 49)
M07 §1		2001, 0.13 µm device: gate oxide ≈ 3.0 nm; 0.1 µm device: under 1.5 nm needed (1999 ITRS, via Wolf) — below the 1.5 nm floor (Lecture 12 said 3–4 nm and 1.5–2 nm; leading-edge 130 nm logic of 2000 already used 1.5 nm at 1.3 V, and SiO₂-based films reached ≈ 1.2 nm at 90 nm; the notes' values stand)

The notes' table of dielectrics (p. 49), with the values the chapter gives later

MaterialkEg (eV)StructureElsewhere in the notes, and flags
PLZT
M07 §1		1000——PbLaZrTiO₃; a memory-capacitor material
BST
M07b §7		300——BaSrTiO₃. 90 for DRAM-thickness films (p. 60); 250 as the roadmap requirement for 2005 (p. 56) (Lecture 11 dictated 100–400)
Ta₂O₅
M07 §8		264.5orthorhombic25 on pp. 54, 58; 22 in the roadmap (p. 56) — all three inherited from Wolf (Robertson gives 22)
ZrO₂
M07 §8		257.8monoclinick 20–25, Eg ≈ 5.1 eV (p. 66) (Robertson: 5.8 eV, the same as HfO₂; 7.8 is an early value)
HfO₂
M07 §8		245.7monoclinick 24–40 (p. 66) (measured films 20–25; the upper end belongs to tetragonal and cubic phases; in a calculation use the stated k, 24 if none)
Al₂O₃
M07 §8		98.7amorphousEg ≈ 8.3 eV on p. 66 (bulk 8.8; ALD films ≈ 6.8) (Lectures 11 and 15: k 9–11)
Si₃N₄
M07 §1		75.1amorphousThe threshold of the definition
SiO₂
M07 §1		3.98.9amorphousCB offset to Si 3.1 eV, VB offset 4.6 eV (course values)

The band-gap column carries no unit in the notes; it is eV. Lecture 11 dictated ranges (20–25 for Ta₂O₅, ZrO₂, HfO₂; 9–11 for Al₂O₃); the notes' values are the course's.

Equivalent thickness, the stack and the interlayer (pp. 50–52, 67)

ItemThe course's version
EOT (p. 50)
M07 §3		The replacement must be thicker, to cut tunnelling, yet keep the same drive current: tox,eq = thigh-k(3.9/k). "Effective oxide thickness" in Lectures 12 and 15
The notes' example
M07 §3		10 nm at k = 20 behaves like ≈ 2 nm of SiO₂: 1.95 nm (Lecture 12 rounded 3.9 to 4 and got 2.0 nm; 3.9 is the course value). Lecture 12's illustration: k = 39 allows a film ten times thicker
What the thickness buys
M07 §3		Tunnelling falls exponentially with the physical thickness, and also with √(m*φB), both smaller in high-k films; Taur and Ning rank candidates by k√(m*φB). At fixed EOT a very large k gives strong fringing fields from the drain (beyond the notes)
Stack (pp. 50–51)
M07 §4		An interfacial SiO₂ layer "will limit the highest possible gate stack capacitance"; in a stack the layer with the lowest k dominates and sets the minimum EOT. EOT = tSiO₂ + thigh-k(3.9/k)
HfO₂ capacitor (p. 67)
M07 §4, §6, §8		EOT 12 Å, leakage four orders of magnitude below SiO₂ at the same 12 Å. Wolf: 38 Å of HfO₂ (6.2 Å of EOT at k = 24) on ≈ 6 Å of interlayer seen in micrographs — half the EOT. Wolf credits the four decades to the physical thickness (Lecture 15: to the absence of the top interlayer)
Ta₂O₅ on polysilicon
M07 §4		Wolf: 10 nm of Ta₂O₅ measures EOT 2.5 nm; (7.3) at k = 25 gives 1.56 nm, so ≈ 0.9 nm sits in lower-k layers (nitrided poly surface; oxide grown in the O₂ anneal)
Passivating layer (p. 51)
M07 §5		One to two atomic layers of SiO₂ — a few ångströms (0.17–0.4 nm on the notes' count of 10–12 layers in 2 nm; 0.35–0.7 nm counting 0.35 nm SiO₂ units) (Lecture 12 said 1–2 nm, once 15–20 Å: that alone would use most or all of the < 1.5 nm EOT target)
The ideal interface (p. 52)
M07 §6		A few atomic layers of Si–O, possibly containing N, forming a pseudo-binary oxide; higher k than SiO₂ with SiO₂'s interface quality. Realisation: SiON/Ta₂O₅/TiN, EOT 1.8 nm (Park et al., via Wolf). ZrO₂ by ALD forms a 9 Å interfacial silicate (p. 66)
Why the layer is kept
M07 §6		A thermally grown interface is clean, a deposited one "is not clean" (Lecture 3); the layer also starts ALD nucleation and spaces the channel from the high-k film. Taur and Ning: composite EOT hard to push much below 1.0 nm

The notes' device points and drawback (p. 51)

ItemThe course's version
Threshold roll-off
M07 §5		Below 0.3 µm, VT decreases as the gate length decreases, at constant doping and dielectric thickness. Ta₂O₅ shows less roll-off than SiO₂ (Wolf Fig. 4-2a, 2-D simulation of SOI devices): at equal physical thickness its larger C strengthens the gate's control
Subthreshold swing St
M07 §5		How effectively a MOSFET turns off below VT; smaller is better; 80 mV/decade achievable with Ta₂O₅. Ideal ln 10 × (kT/q) = 59.5 mV/decade (≈ 60) at 300 K. Wolf: below 100 very good; Taur and Ning: 70–100 typical. The 80 is the Ta₂O₅ device at a 0.05 µm channel in Wolf's Fig. 4-2b, where SiO₂ gives ≈ 123; at 0.1 µm, 65 and 75; from 0.2 µm both ≈ 60
Optimum k
M07 §5		Wolf: k = 25 is optimum for a 5 nm dielectric (circuit simulation at fixed physical thickness; inverter delay into 1 mm of interconnect barely improves beyond it)
The drawback
M07 §5		Deposited directly on Si, high-k films have a high Dit; growing 1–2 atomic layers of SiO₂ passivates the surface — at the price of an EOT floor

What decided the candidates — beyond the notes (M07 §2, §5)

ItemThe modules' version
Origin of k
M07 §2		k = n² + klat: HfO₂ ≈ 4 electronic + ≈ 20 ionic. A large ionic part needs a large Born charge (Hf 5.85 against +4) and a soft polar vibration; ionic character Si–O 45%, Hf–O 68%. The metal d states that make the conduction band narrow the gap: k and Eg run inversely
Band offsets
M07 §5		Both offsets > ≈ 1 eV against Schottky emission, so Eg ≳ 5 eV. CB offsets: SiO₂ 3.1 (Robertson 3.2), Al₂O₃ 2.8 (ALD films ≈ 2.2), Si₃N₄ 2.4, HfSiO₄ 1.8, ZrO₂ 1.5, HfO₂ 1.4, Ta₂O₅ 0.35 (measured 0.3) — Ta₂O₅ fails; perovskite titanates ≈ 0
Stability on Si
M07 §5		Avoid MO₂ + Si → M + SiO₂ and MO₂ + 2Si → MSi + SiO₂. Pass: Al₂O₃, ZrO₂, HfO₂, Y₂O₃, La₂O₃, lanthanide oxides (Pr₂O₃), alkaline-earth oxides. Fail: Ta₂O₅ (up to 2 nm of SiO₂ grows beneath it), TiO₂, SrTiO₃, BaTiO₃. Hf forms the most stable oxide of Ti, Zr, Hf; HfO₂ density 9.68 g/cm³. Notes p. 66: ZrO₂ "thermodynamically stable with Si", Al₂O₃ "excellent", HfO₂ "good" thermal stability (ZrO₂ later found slightly unstable, forming ZrSi₂)
Crystallisation
M07 §5		HfO₂ crystallises in a 1000 °C anneal (p. 67); a polycrystalline film leaks (Wolf). Hf and Zr have 7 O neighbours (monoclinic), 8 in cubic and tetragonal forms: poor glass formers. Al₂O₃ the best glass former after SiO₂. Silicates and aluminates stay amorphous to ≈ 1000 °C, nitrogen raises it further; cost in k: HfSiO₄ ≈ 11 against 25

Deposition methods and ALD (pp. 59–60, 65–67)

ItemThe course's version
Methods by candidate
M07 §7		Reactive sputtering in Ar + O₂: ZrO₂, HfO₂, Al-doped ZrO₂ by co-sputtering an Al target. Zr film oxidised by RTA: ZrO₂. CVD, MOCVD, RTCVD: Ta₂O₅; HfO₂ by in-situ RTCVD. MBE: Pr₂O₃ at 625–725 °C, 0.1 Å/s. ALD: ZrO₂ from ZrCl₄ + H₂O; Al₂O₃ from TMA + O₃ at 450 °C (the notes name the precursors, Wolf the method)
CVD (p. 65)
M07 §7		Two or more gases mixed in the chamber react in the gas phase and deposit on the wafer; the reactants are present together (Wolf gives both paths, gas-phase or on the wafer surface; Plummer defines CVD by the surface reaction; what matters for ALD is that the reactants are present together)
ALD (p. 65)
M07 §7		One reactant gas at a time; a monolayer of the first chemisorbs on the surface; precursors pulsed sequentially without gas-phase mixing; the surface reaction goes to completion and permits only one layer at a time (self-limiting). Thickness set by counting cycles
ALD advantages (p. 65)
M07 §7		Very thin films; low temperature, < 400 °C (200–300 °C in Lecture 14; the notes' own Al₂O₃ runs at 450 °C); 100% step coverage and excellent conformality; wide choice of precursors; low impurity levels (relative to CVD; Robertson rates ALD purity equal to MOCVD); composition controlled at the atomic level
ALD rate (p. 65)
M07 §7		A few ångströms per cycle at most, "as determined by lattice constant" (the bound holds; the cause is sub-monolayer adsorption by steric hindrance — TMA/H₂O gives 1.1–1.2 Å per cycle); switching in < 1 s gives 50–200 Å/min (Wolf: up to 200)
Nucleation
M07 §7		ALD HfO₂ and ZrO₂ nucleate poorly on H-terminated Si after an HF clean; production starts on a thin chemical oxide from an ozone clean, which joins the interlayer

The gate candidates, in the notes' order (pp. 59–60, 66–67)

CandidateThe course's version
Ta₂O₅, first route (p. 59)
M07 §8		2 nm of oxide grown on Si, annealed in N₂ at 800 °C for 30 (min, in Wolf) as a nitridation, so the nitrided SiO₂ bars oxygen and no further oxide grows; TiN gate instead of poly. Leakage like an SiO₂ MOSFET of identical thickness (Wolf) (N₂ is nearly inert at 800 °C; Lecture 9: "Nitric oxide. Not nitrogen.")
Ta₂O₅, second route (p. 60)
M07 §8		Interlayer grown in NO at 800 °C for 20 s, then CVD Ta₂O₅: EOT 2.3 nm, 1000× smaller leakage (than the same stack with an SiO₂ interlayer, Wolf). Variant: NH₃ at 800 °C for 30 s (Wolf)
Ta₂O₅ gate electrode (p. 60)
M07 §8		A barrier, usually TiN or WN, stops silicide forming with a poly gate; the drawing has TiN as the gate itself. A metal gate raises VT by ≈ 0.6 V, so VT must be adjusted, which produces buried-channel MOSFETs with degraded short-channel behaviour
Ta₂O₅, its fate
M07 §8		CB offset 0.35 eV and unstable on Si: eliminated as a gate dielectric (Robertson: "eliminated because it is reactive"); it stayed a capacitor dielectric (Lecture 2: Ta₂O₅ first, then HfO₂, then hafnium silicate)
Al₂O₃ (p. 66)
M07 §8		TMA + O₃ at 450 °C; k = 9; Eg ≈ 8.3 eV; excellent thermal stability on Si. Wolf: no barrier needed under poly; 2–3 orders less leakage than SiO₂ at equal EOT; effective k of the stack 5.5–6.0 (interlayer); perhaps one node only; lower mobility from electron trapping
ZrO₂ (p. 66)
M07 §8		k 20–25; thermodynamically stable with Si; Eg ≈ 5.1 eV; lattice mismatch 2.1%; reactive sputtering in O₂ + Ar or Zr film oxidised by RTA; etches in HF; Al doping by co-sputtering gives better uniformity (amorphous, slightly lower k, Wolf). ALD from ZrCl₄ + H₂O; 9 Å interfacial silicate; post-deposition anneal in O₂ or inert gas at 400–600 °C to cut leakage. Zr-Al-Si-O: Al slows O diffusion. Yttria-stabilised ZrO₂ usable for MOSFETs
HfO₂ (pp. 66–67)
M07 §8		k 24–40; good thermal stability with Si; Eg ≈ 5.7 eV; PVD (reactive sputtering) or in-situ RTCVD; no interfacial layer with poly if the poly deposition temperature is kept below 600 °C and RTA anneals run at 1000 °C; crystallises; 10⁴ less leakage than SiO₂ at 12 Å EOT. Electron trapping, lower mobility (Wolf)
HfSiON (p. 67)
M07 §8		A candidate with smaller leakage than SiO₂ at the same EOT. Hafnium silicate, (HfO₂)x(SiO₂)1−x, named in Lectures 2, 3, 6, 11, 15; Lecture 2 called it "more stable": amorphous, higher offset (HfSiO₄ 1.8 eV), N blocks boron; k falls with SiO₂ content (Lecture 11's 20–25 is the Hf-rich end; HfSiO₄ ≈ 11)
Hf–Zr oxide
M07 §8		Lecture 15, not in the notes: HfxZr1−xO₂, finer grains, more tetragonal phase, lower electrical thickness and higher mobility than HfO₂ (Triyoso 2007)
Pr₂O₃ (p. 67)
M07 §8		5 nA/cm² at 1 V with EOT 14 Å; k ≈ 31; no C–V hysteresis; MBE at 625–725 °C, 0.1 Å/s; anneal in a furnace at 600 °C in dry N₂ (5 min, Wolf) or RTA at 1000 °C for 15 s in N₂
CeO₂
M07 §8		Lectures 2, 11, 15, not in the notes: close lattice match, 5.411 Å against Si 5.431 Å, 0.37% (cubic ZrO₂: 6.6%), so epitaxial gate insulators; k 20–25 (Lecture 11)

The gate electrode (pp. 60, 67)

ItemThe course's version
Poly on high-k
M07 §9		Chemical: reacts with an unstable oxide (Ta₂O₅ needs a TiN or WN barrier); HfO₂ needs poly deposited below 600 °C. Electrical: poly depletion adds a few ångströms of EOT; HfO₂ with a TaN gate reached 8 Å EOT (Wolf)
Work functions
M07 §9		n⁺ poly ≈ 4.05 eV (electron affinity of Si); p⁺ poly ≈ 5.17 eV; Si midgap 4.61 eV (Robertson 4.6); TiN is a midgap metal
The 0.6 V (p. 60)
M07 §9		n⁺ poly → midgap metal on an n-channel device: +0.55 V, the notes' "about 0.6 V"; p⁺ poly → midgap on a p-channel device: −0.57 V, also a larger |VT|. Thresholds near 0.5 V are too large for supplies at or below 1 V (Wilk)
The fixes
M07 §9		Counter-doping the channel surface lowers VT but makes a buried channel (roll-off, swing, DIBL degrade). Two band-edge metals instead: ≈ 4.0 eV for n-channel, 5.1 eV for p-channel (Robertson)
Production
M07 §9		Beyond the notes: Intel, January 2007, 45 nm (Mistry et al., IEDM 2007) — hafnium-based high-k at 1.0 nm EOT with dual band-edge metal gates. Wolf (2002) forecast about five years

The DRAM cell and why it keeps 30 fF (pp. 52–53, 55–56)

ItemThe course's version
1T1C cell (p. 56)
M07b §1		The MOS capacitor connects to the bit line through the pass transistor (n-channel switch) whose gate is the word line. Storage node: the electrode on the transistor (bottom electrode in a stacked cell); plate electrode or cell plate: common to the array (top electrode) (p. 55). DRAM is dynamic because the charge leaks and must be refreshed
Read, retention
M07b §1		Beyond the notes (Taur and Ning): read by charge sharing from a bit line precharged to half the supply; the sense amplifier writes the value back. Worst-case retention ≈ 100 ms; 30 fC losing 10% in 100 ms allows ≈ 30 fA of total leakage
The squeeze (p. 52)
M07b §2		Supply 5 V → 3 V → may drop to 1 V; charge storage capacity stays ≈ 30 fF per cell, "about 1–2×10⁶ electrons/cell" (by Q = CV, 30 fF holds 1.9×10⁵ at 1 V, 5.6×10⁵ at 3 V, 9.4×10⁵ at 5 V; 1–2×10⁶ would need 5.3–10.7 V)
Reason 1: soft errors (pp. 52–53)
M07b §2		α-particles from U and Th in the packaging and cosmic rays (at ground level, secondary neutrons) generate minority carriers that alter the stored state; ≈ 30 fF keeps the soft-error rate low
Reasons 2 and 3 (p. 53)
M07b §2		Sense-amplifier detection capability unchanged; data retention time unchanged — each requires about the same storage capacity per cell

Keeping the capacitance as the cell shrinks (pp. 53–54, 57, 61)

ItemThe course's version
Three levers
M07b §3		C = (ε0kA/t): raise A, thin t, raise k. A planar 30 fF on 10 nm SiO₂ needs 8.7 µm² (≈ 3 µm square); since the 4 Mb generation (1.2 µm), trenches or stacks (Taur and Ning)
1. Three-dimensional cells (pp. 53, 57, 61)
M07b §3		Trench: etched into the Si beside the pass transistor; the n⁺ wall is the storage electrode ("charge storage"), a poly plate fills it; ≈ 5 µm deep (Lecture 13). Stacked: storage node on a plug above the transistor, area from its height
2. Thinner dielectric (p. 53)
M07b §3		SiO₂ (3.9), Si₃N₄ (≈ 7) or ONO, oxide–nitride–oxide; ONO 50 Å — Wolf's minimum: below ≈ 5 nm, tunnelling gives excessive leakage
3. Rough polysilicon (pp. 53–54)
M07b §3		Hemispherical silicon grain (HSG) or rough surface poly (RSP): bottom-electrode poly grown with larger grains, area × ≈ 2. Only the bottom electrode is roughened (Lecture 12)
Their limit (p. 54)
M07b §3		ONO 50 Å with HSG served 0.18 and 0.15 µm DRAMs; "not good for memories of more than 256 MB" because of leakage (read Mb). Wolf adds: 3-D structures could not be scaled further; premature breakdown even at reduced voltage. Fix: a high-k film, thick enough for low leakage
What followed (p. 54)
M07b §3		Ta₂O₅, k = 25, good success for memories up to 4 GB (read Gb), by CVD, MOCVD, sputtering or anodization; then ferroelectrics or near-ferroelectrics — BST, PZT, other perovskites — with k from a few hundred to a few thousand, strongly dependent on composition and crystal structure
Wolf's 0.15 µm stack
M07b §3		Pedestal 0.15 × 0.45 µm, 0.3 µm high: 0.43 µm²; ≈ 80 fF/µm² needed, EOT ≈ 0.43 nm: Ta₂O₅ 2.8 nm, BST (k 90) 9.9 nm. Wolf: minimum practical Ta₂O₅ EOT ≈ 1.5 nm

Electrodes (pp. 55–56, 59, 63–64)

ItemThe course's version
SIS, MIS, MIM (p. 55)
M07b §4		Poly–insulator–poly (the ONO capacitors); poly storage node with a metal plate, MIS, read top-down as metal–insulator–silicon (Lecture 12 said "SIM"); metal–insulator–metal
Poly's fault (p. 55)
M07b §4		Forms SiO₂ at the poly/high-k interface, natively or in later anneals and the ILD CVD; it adds to the EOT and cuts the cell capacitance. 1 nm of SiO₂ on 10 nm at k = 90 (0.43 nm): EOT 1.43 nm, capacitance 30% of intended
Materials considered (p. 55)
M07b §4		Al, Ir, Pt, Ru, TaN, TiN, W — "metals or near metallic".
Pt, Ir, Ru (pp. 55–56)
M07b §4		Good chemical corrosion properties, thermal stability, diffusion-barrier properties (Wolf adds low resistivity). Problems: Pt — adhesion, etch residue, cost; Ru — toxic RuO₄ from the etch; pinholes (Ru's, in Wolf). RuO₂ and IrO₂ conduct
Conducting oxides
M07b §4		RuO₂ bottom electrode, ρ = 35 µΩ·cm (p. 59; ≈ 13× Al); IrO₂, SrRuO₃, (La,Sr)CoO₃ as top electrodes (p. 64). Already oxidised, so no dielectric oxide grows at the interface
RuO₄ (p. 64)
M07b §4		By-product of the easy RuO₂ etch; MP 25.4 °C; "flammable and toxic" (toxic, yes; it is an oxidiser, not a fuel)
Barrier (p. 63)
M07b §4		Pt forms silicides: a Ti/TiN diffusion barrier separates it from the poly plug and also serves as its glue layer

The k values are requirements, not material properties: Ta₂O₅ at 50 is double its bulk value. Each node is about 0.7 times the last, so the cell area falls by 0.49 per node and, at 30 fF, the capacitance per unit footprint doubles, 35 times over the table's five steps. Lecture 13 dictated Ta₂O₅ as 25, 25 and filled the question marks with SBT at 1500; the notes' entries are the course's (M07b §4).

Ta₂O₅ capacitors (pp. 54, 58–59)

ItemThe course's version
k and standing
M07b §5		k = 25 (pp. 54, 58; 26 in the p. 49 table, 22 in the roadmap). Developed since the early 1980s; replaces ONO without major integration problems; biggest drawback for DRAM a relatively high leakage (Wolf)
Four methods (p. 58)
M07b §5		LPCVD, MOCVD, sputtering of Ta followed by oxidation, anodic oxidation. LPCVD most common (Wolf)
CVD process (p. 58)
M07b §5		Oxygen-assisted thermal decomposition of tantalum ethylate Ta(OC₂H₅)₅ (TAETO) or Ta(OC₂H₅)₄(OCH₂CH₂N(CH₃)₂) (TATDMAE); 1–4 Torr; 450–485 °C (Lecture 13: 480–500 °C, and called it MOCVD; at 1–4 Torr it is both)
Regime
M07b §5		Wolf: 450–485 °C reaction-rate limited, Ea = 1.3 eV, rate doubles every 25 °C; above 485 °C mass-transport limited, thick at a trench mouth and thin below — conformal deposition needs the low window
Anodic oxidation
M07b §5		Lecture 13: the sputtered Ta film is the anode (+), Pt the cathode (−), in dilute sulphuric or boric acid; 2Ta + 5H₂O → Ta₂O₅ + 5H₂; ≈ 1.7 nm of oxide per volt (Gill); ≈ 40% of the oxide lies below the original metal surface
Interlayer (p. 58)
M07b §5		"Deposited on to an interfacial layer of SiO₂ that is achieved by rapid thermal (polysi) nitridation": oxidise the poly, then RTA in N₂O (Lecture 13); or nitride the poly directly in NH₃, e.g. 900 °C for 60 s (Wolf). Its nitrogen slows further oxidation; it costs some capacitance
Densification (p. 58)
M07b §5		As deposited: oxygen deficient and amorphous, poor leakage. Densify by O₂ anneal or RTA in O₂ at 600–700 °C, which also crystallises it (Lecture 13 said 900–1000 °C; Wolf's ceiling ≈ 800 °C, above which Si crosses the nitride and SiO₂ grows)
Electrodes (pp. 58–59)
M07b §5		TiN or WN barrier under a poly top electrode, or TiN as the top electrode (MIS; TiN sputtered). Bottom electrode Pt or RuO₂ (ρ = 35 µΩ·cm) (Wolf's reports: TiN or RuO₂ bottom, Pt top). Ru/Ta₂O₅/Ru with sputtered Ru: k = 65; less leakage after a 400 °C forming-gas anneal (Wolf)
MOSD (p. 59)
M07b §5		Metalorganic solution deposition of 0.9Ta₂O₅–0.1Al₂O₃: k = 45 (Wolf 42; Lecture 13 45–50), 6×10⁻⁸ A/cm² (up to 1 MV/cm, Wolf)
Etch gases (p. 59)
M07b §5		C₂F₆, CH₄, CF₃Cl, CHF₃, "CCl₃F₂" (an impossible formula carried from Wolf: CCl₂F₂ or CCl₃F)
The limit (p. 59)
M07b §5		HSG poly with Ta₂O₅ for the 1 Gb and 4 Gb generations (0.15 µm node) (Wolf's next sentence: minimum EOT ≈ 1.5 nm, the limit "may be reached at the 1-Gb integration level")

Perovskites, BST and FeRAM (pp. 54, 57, 60–64)

ItemThe course's version
Perovskite
M07b §6		Beyond the notes: ABO₃; A = Ba²⁺, Sr²⁺, Pb²⁺; B = Ti⁴⁺, Zr⁴⁺; corner-sharing TiO₆ octahedra. A titanate is not TiO₂ (Lectures 11 and 14 called BST's titanium part TiO₂). High k from ionic polarisation (Wolf)
Ferroelectric, paraelectric
M07b §6		Below the Curie point Tc the Ti sits off-centre: domains, hysteresis, remanent ±Pr, coercive field. Above it: paraelectric, no spontaneous polarisation, still high k (Curie–Weiss). BaTiO₃ Tc ≈ 120 °C; Sr brings it below room temperature, so BST is paraelectric for DRAM (Wolf); PZT and SBT are ferroelectric for FeRAM (Lectures 13 and 15 called BST ferroelectric)
BST films (p. 60)
M07b §7		BaxSr1−xTiO₃, 0.5 < x < 0.7; polycrystalline; k = 90 for DRAM-thickness films (Wolf 88–90); 300 in the p. 49 table, 250 in the roadmap (lectures: 100–400, and ≈ 1500). No band gap in the notes (Lecture 11 said 5–6 eV; titanates ≈ 3 eV, SrTiO₃ 3.2, CB offset ≈ 0)
Dispersion (p. 60)
M07b §7		MOCVD BST: k falls 2% from 100 Hz to 10 MHz and 7% from 200 MHz to 2 GHz (Wolf: < 2%, < 7%) (the notes make the small dispersion a result of the high k; Wolf gives two separate reasons)
For high k (pp. 61, 63)
M07b §7		Free of impurities, correct stoichiometry, crystalline. Ti as a percentage of all cations best at ≈ 51%, tightly controlled (Wolf: k falls ≈ 40% from 51% to 53%). Rougher surface → lower k, higher leakage (hazy films; less haze above 600 °C, Wolf)
Deposition (p. 61)
M07b §8		RF sputtering, thermal MOCVD, sol-gel, pulsed laser ablation. MOCVD 80% conformality against 60% for sputtering and sol-gel; a composite sputter target has too short a life because the elements sputter at different rates — so MOCVD is most common
Precursors (pp. 61–62)
M07b §8		Solids, dissolved in an organic solvent, flash vaporised to minimise their decomposition, carried as gas; adducts of Ba and Sr β-diketonates with organometallic Ti (Lecture 14 named chlorides in water)
Temperature (p. 62)
M07b §8		Low temperature → conformal, high → crystalline perovskite; precursors used at 480–550 °C for 80–100% conformality (two regimes: adducted at ≈ 550 °C, good films, poor conformality; non-adducted at 480 °C, 100%, > 100 fF/µm², 10⁻⁸ A/cm²; crossover ≈ 480 °C)
Anneal (p. 62)
M07b §8		After the top electrode, 500–700 °C in N₂O, O₂ or N₂ (Lecture 14 gave 500–700 °C as the deposition temperature); heals sputter damage, cuts leakage, raises C by ≈ 25% (Wolf)
Excluded electrodes (p. 63)
M07b §9		Al: integration steps above 600 °C (Wolf: > 500 °C). TiN: not very stable against oxidising species above 500 °C. Poly: oxidises (Wolf)
Electrodes used (pp. 63–64)
M07b §9		Pt, Ir, Ru and the conducting oxides of Ir and Ru, hard to dry-etch; Ti/TiN barrier under Pt. Top: Pt, Ru, RuO₂, IrO₂, SrRuO₃, (La,Sr)CoO₃ (Wolf omits Pt: it catalyses BST reduction in the forming-gas anneal)
Etching (p. 64)
M07b §9		BST has no volatile by-products: etched physically, like ion milling, which small features hinder (TiF₄ volatile; BaF₂, SrF₂ refractory) (Lecture 14 described CF₄ RIE). Ru and RuO₂ etch in O₂/Cl₂ as volatile RuO₄, anisotropically. Tools: sputtering system, MOCVD reactor, reactive etching system
FeRAM (p. 57)
M07b §10		Non-volatile, "an emerging area": the bit is a remanent polarisation, no refresh. PZT (lead zirconium titanate) and SBT, k 1000–1500 (Wolf gives it for PZT; SBT is SrBi₂Ta₂O₉, a tantalate, films k ≈ 180–300). Stacked cell: poly plug, barrier, Pt, ferroelectric, Pt. Considerations: interfacial reactions, electrode thermal stability, plasma etching

2.2 Problems and fixes, and the process flows

Ta₂O₅ gate stack (notes pp. 59–60; Lecture 13's sequence; M07 §8)

  1. Oxidise the Si: a thin interfacial SiO₂ (2 nm in the notes' first route) — the clean interface.
  2. Nitride the oxide (notes: N₂ at 800 °C for 30 — minutes in Wolf; Lecture 12 named N₂O for nitriding the interlayer) — so it bars oxygen from the Si and no further oxide grows. Second route: one step, NO at 800 °C for 20 s.
  3. Deposit Ta₂O₅ by CVD — the high-k film (EOT 2.3 nm on the NO interlayer).
  4. Deposit TiN — the barrier against silicide; made thick, it is the gate itself (the notes' drawing) and the sequence stops here.
  5. Deposit the polysilicon gate on the thin TiN barrier (Lecture 13's arrangement).

One ALD cycle (notes p. 65; M07 §7)

  1. Metal-precursor pulse, e.g. TMA (or ZrCl₄) — chemisorbs on the surface –OH until every site has reacted (self-limiting); CH₄ (or HCl) released.
  2. Inert-gas purge — removes unreacted precursor and by-product, so the two reactants never meet in the gas (otherwise it is CVD).
  3. Oxidant pulse, O₃ (the notes' Al₂O₃, 450 °C) or H₂O — converts the adsorbed layer to oxide and restores the –OH surface.
  4. Purge, then repeat: thickness = cycles × growth per cycle; each switch < 1 s, 50–200 Å/min, < 400 °C typical.

Lecture 14 taught the cycle with SiH₄ and O₂ (Wolf's example) without the pump-out between pulses, and with silane decomposing to Si — that is CVD, not ALD.

Trench cell (notes p. 57; Lecture 13's four steps; M07b §3)

  1. Oxidise, open a window by masking, etch the trench by RIE (Si leaves as SiF₄) — the depth supplies the area (≈ 5 µm, Lecture 13).
  2. Diffuse the n⁺ source/drain — the same diffusion dopes the trench wall n⁺, the storage electrode joined to the drain.
  3. Oxidise — one oxidation grows the gate oxide and the capacitor dielectric on the wall.
  4. Fill with polysilicon — the cell plate, spread over the surface to be contacted.

The lecture's order (S/D before the gate) is a teaching sequence; trench cells belong to self-aligned processes.

SIS capacitor (Lecture 13's five steps; notes p. 55; M07b §4)

  1. LPCVD poly storage node from SiH₄ → Si + 2H₂ (575–650 °C, Plummer; the lecture said 600–800 °C).
  2. Oxidise the poly in O₂ — a thin SiO₂ (the notes treat it as forming anyway; it adds to the EOT).
  3. Deposit the high-k film.
  4. Deposit a TiN barrier (or WN) — stops the hot poly plate reducing the high-k film.
  5. Deposit the poly plate by the same CVD. An MIS capacitor uses a metal plate, deposited cooler, which may need no barrier.

Ta₂O₅ DRAM capacitor (notes p. 58; Lecture 13's order; M07b §5)

  1. Poly storage node (HSG-roughened for 1–4 Gb).
  2. Oxidise it — thin SiO₂.
  3. RTN in N₂O (or NH₃ directly, 900 °C, 60 s, Wolf) — nitrogen to slow further oxidation of the poly.
  4. CVD Ta₂O₅: TAETO or TATDMAE + O₂, 1–4 Torr, 450–485 °C — reaction-limited, conformal.
  5. Densify in O₂ or O-RTA at 600–700 °C, below ≈ 800 °C — thought to fill the oxygen vacancies; crystallises the film; cuts leakage.
  6. TiN (or WN) barrier — against silicide formation with the plate.
  7. Poly plate (or a TiN plate instead of steps 6–7: MIS).

Ru/BST/Ru capacitor (notes p. 64, after Wolf Fig. 4-14; M07b §9)

  1. Polysilicon plug formation — connects each storage node to its pass transistor.
  2. Barrier TiN/Ti deposition — keeps Ru from reacting with the plug's poly in the BST steps.
  3. Ru deposition (sputtered) — the storage-node metal.
  4. SiO₂ deposition — the future hard mask.
  5. Lithography — the storage-node pattern in resist.
  6. SiO₂ etch — the hard mask (resist would burn in the O₂/Cl₂ Ru etch, M07b's reading).
  7. Ru and barrier etch — one node per plug.
  8. SiO₂ removal.
  9. BST deposition, low-temperature two-step CVD: thin buffer layer, anneal, main layer, anneal — conformal, Ru kept unoxidised.
  10. Ru deposition — the top electrode, the common cell plate.

Lecture 14 drew it with four differences: a 1 µm resist mask for the 0.5 µm Ru etch, an SiO₂ window after the Ru etch, one BST step at 500–550 °C with one 600–700 °C anneal, and the top Ru as a word line.

2.4 Where the course and the literature differ

2.5 Self-check

Choose one. Which of these is a high-k dielectric by the notes' definition — (a) Si₃N₄ (b) an oxynitride with k = 5 (c) Al₂O₃ (d) SiO₂

(c). The notes define high-k as k > 7, above Si₃N₄; Al₂O₃ has k = 9. Si₃N₄ sits at the threshold, not above it, and an oxynitride lies between 3.9 and 7. M07 §1.

Choose one. Under a fixed interfacial SiO₂ layer, the top layer's dielectric constant is raised. The lowest EOT the stack can reach — (a) increases (b) decreases (c) does not change (d) none of the above

(c). By (7.6) the SiO₂ term enters at its full thickness and does not depend on the top layer's k, so however high k goes, the EOT cannot fall below tSiO₂: the notes' point that the lowest-k layer sets a limit on the minimum equivalent thickness. The EOT of any given stack falls; its floor does not move. M07 §4.

Choose one. At the same physical thickness, a gate dielectric of higher k raises Cox at fixed Cd. The long-channel subthreshold swing — (a) increases (b) decreases (c) does not change (d) none of the above

(b). St = 2.3(kT/q)(1 + Cd/Cox): a larger Cox shrinks the second factor toward 1, and the swing toward the ideal 59.5 mV/decade. M07 §5.

Choose one. By Lecture 13's rule of 0.7× per generation, the node three generations after 90 nm is — (a) 63.0 nm (b) 44.1 nm (c) 30.9 nm (d) 22.0 nm

(c). 90 → 63.0 → 44.1 → 30.9 nm. Each step multiplies the cell area by 0.7² = 0.49, so at a constant 30 fF the capacitance per unit footprint doubles each generation. M07b §4.

Choose one. Which method deposits a film with 100% step coverage whose thickness is set by counting cycles — (a) reactive sputtering (b) CVD (c) MBE (d) ALD

(d). In atomic layer deposition one reactant is introduced at a time and chemisorbs until the surface saturates, so each cycle adds a fixed amount wherever the surface faces (notes p. 65). Sputtering is line-of-sight, and in CVD the reactants are present together. M07 §7.

Choose one. Which candidate was eliminated as a gate dielectric because its conduction-band offset to silicon is only about 0.35 eV — (a) HfO₂ (b) Al₂O₃ (c) Ta₂O₅ (d) ZrO₂

(c). A barrier that low lets channel electrons cross by thermal emission or tunnelling, so Ta₂O₅'s physical thickness buys little; it is also unstable on silicon. HfO₂ (1.4 eV), ZrO₂ (1.5 eV) and Al₂O₃ (2.8 eV) pass the ≈ 1 eV criterion. The criterion is beyond the notes. M07 §5.

Choose one. As-deposited CVD Ta₂O₅ in a DRAM capacitor leaks because it is — (a) polycrystalline (b) oxygen deficient and amorphous (c) too thick (d) nitrided

(b), notes p. 58. It is densified in an O₂ anneal or an oxygen RTA at 600–700 °C, which is thought to fill the oxygen vacancies (Wolf) and also crystallises the film. Lecture 13's 900–1000 °C is above Wolf's ceiling of about 800 °C, where silicon crosses the nitrided interlayer and SiO₂ grows. M07b §5.

Choose one. In a DRAM capacitor, BST is operated — (a) ferroelectric, below its Curie point (b) paraelectric, just above its Curie point (c) amorphous, as deposited (d) none of the above

(b). Strontium brings the Curie point below room temperature, so the film has a large k without a remanent polarisation that would add history-dependent charge (Wolf). The lectures called BST ferroelectric; the notes introduce it among "ferroelectrics or near-ferroelectrics", and the ferroelectric pair is PZT and SBT for FeRAM. M07b §6.

The interfacial SiO₂ under a high-k gate dielectric: why it is there, what it costs, and how it is kept in check — with a sketch.

Deposited directly on silicon, high-k films have a high density of interface states (notes p. 51). A thermally grown oxide forms its interface by consuming silicon, inside the original crystal, so that interface is clean; a deposited film forms its interface on the surface as it was (Lecture 3). The notes' cure is to grow one to two atomic layers of SiO₂, a few ångströms, before the high-k film. The cost is series capacitance: by (7.6), EOT = tSiO₂ + thigh-k(3.9/k), and the SiO₂ counts at its full thickness, so the lowest-k layer dominates and sets the minimum EOT at any k. In the notes' HfO₂ capacitor (EOT 12 Å) the 38 Å of HfO₂ supplies 6.2 Å and the ≈ 6 Å interlayer the rest. The layer also grows when it is not wanted: Ta₂O₅ reacts with silicon to put up to 2 nm of SiO₂ beneath itself, and oxygen from anneals diffuses through ZrO₂ and HfO₂ to oxidise the silicon. The fixes: keep the layer a few ångströms; raise its k by nitriding it (N₂O) or grading it into a silicate, the notes' pseudo-binary ideal (SiON/Ta₂O₅/TiN at 1.8 nm EOT); dope ZrO₂ with Al to slow oxygen diffusion; under Ta₂O₅, a 2 nm oxide nitrided to bar oxygen from the silicon, or an NO-grown interlayer.

Full treatment: M07 §4 and §6.

Why must a DRAM cell keep about 30 fF as it shrinks, and how was that capacitance kept through the generations?

A 1T1C cell stores its bit as charge on a capacitor reached through a pass transistor. The notes give three reasons the capacitance cannot shrink with the cell (pp. 52–53): α-particles from uranium and thorium in the packaging, and cosmic rays, generate minority carriers that can discharge a node, and ≈ 30 fF keeps the soft-error rate low; the sense amplifier's detection capability has stayed the same, and the read signal is the stored voltage scaled by Ccell/(Ccell + Cbitline); and the retention time has stayed the same while the leakage paths do not shrink. The supply fell from 5 V to 3 V and toward 1 V, so the stored charge fell anyway; holding the capacitance was the most that could be done. With C = ε0kA/t, three approaches came first (pp. 53–54): three-dimensional trench or stacked capacitors for area; thinner dielectric, down to ONO at 50 Å, below which tunnelling leaks; and HSG polysilicon, roughly doubling the bottom electrode's area. ONO with HSG served 0.18 and 0.15 µm DRAMs but not beyond 256 Mb, for leakage. Raising k came next: Ta₂O₅ (k = 25) up to 4 Gb, on a nitrided interlayer and densified at 600–700 °C, then BST (k = 90 in DRAM films) between platinum-group electrodes, since a simple 0.15 µm stack needs an EOT of about 0.43 nm, below Ta₂O₅'s practical 1.5 nm.

Full treatment: M07b §2 and §3.

How is a film grown by atomic layer deposition, step by step, and what makes it different from CVD?

In CVD the reactants are mixed in the chamber and react together. ALD separates them in time (notes p. 65), as for Al₂O₃ from TMA and ozone at 450 °C:

  1. Pulse the metal precursor, TMA. It chemisorbs on the surface hydroxyls, releasing CH₄, until every site has reacted; the methyl-covered surface takes no more — the step is self-limiting.
  2. Purge with inert gas, removing the excess TMA and the CH₄, so the two reactants never meet in the gas.
  3. Pulse the oxidant, O₃ (or H₂O), which converts the adsorbed layer to oxide and leaves a surface ready for the next pulse.
  4. Purge again, and repeat. The thickness is the number of cycles times the growth per cycle, a few ångströms at most (below a monolayer: 1.1–1.2 Å for TMA/H₂O); switching in under a second gives 50–200 Å/min.

Because every exposed surface saturates, the film has 100% step coverage; it grows at low temperature (< 400 °C typical), with atomic-level control of composition and low impurity relative to CVD. ZrO₂ is grown the same way from ZrCl₄ and H₂O, with HCl as the by-product.

Full treatment: M07 §7.

What is a ferroelectric, and why is BST used paraelectric in a DRAM while PZT is used ferroelectric in an FeRAM?

The titanates are perovskites, ABO₃: titanium sits in an octahedron of six oxygens with barium, strontium or lead in the cavities between octahedra. The restoring force on the titanium is weak and its effective charge large, so ionic polarisation gives k in the hundreds or thousands. Below a critical temperature, the Curie point, the titanium moves off-centre spontaneously and neighbouring cells align into domains: the material is ferroelectric, its polarisation traces a hysteresis loop against field, and at zero field it keeps a remanent polarisation of either sign. Above the Curie point it is paraelectric — no spontaneous polarisation, but still a very large k close to the transition (the notes' "near-ferroelectrics"). A DRAM must store a charge that follows its voltage, so it uses BST, BaxSr1−xTiO₃ with 0.5 < x < 0.7, in which strontium brings the Curie point (≈ 120 °C for BaTiO₃) below room temperature; a remanent polarisation would add charge that depends on the cell's history. A non-volatile FeRAM stores its bit as the direction of that remanent polarisation, which persists without power, so it uses ferroelectric PZT or SBT (the notes give k = 1000–1500, PZT's value) in a stacked Pt/ferroelectric/Pt capacitor. The name is an analogy with ferromagnetism; no iron is involved.

Full treatment: M07b §6 and §10.

EOT and its inverse. Illustrative inputs, not the course's. (a) What is the equivalent oxide thickness of a 4.0 nm ZrO₂ film, taking the notes' table value k = 25? (b) What physical thickness gives an EOT of 0.9 nm in HfO₂ at the table value k = 24, in HfO₂ at k = 30, and in Al₂O₃ (k = 9)?

(a) Equation (7.3). tox,eq = 4.0 nm × 3.9/25 = 0.624 nm.

(b) Rearrange (7.3). thigh-k = EOT × k/3.9. HfO₂ at 24: 0.9 nm × 24/3.9 = 5.54 nm. HfO₂ at 30: 0.9 nm × 30/3.9 = 6.92 nm. Al₂O₃: 0.9 nm × 9/3.9 = 2.08 nm.

0.624 nm; 5.54 nm, 6.92 nm and 2.08 nm

Check. A film is thicker than its EOT by the factor k/3.9, 6.41 for ZrO₂ at 25. The HfO₂ thickness is proportional to k, so the answer depends on which value is used: the stated one, or 24 when none is stated.

Method: M07 §3; formula sheet (7.3).

A stack and its interlayer. Illustrative inputs. (a) Find the EOT of 0.4 nm of SiO₂ under 4.0 nm of a film with k = 20. (b) A stack with 4.0 nm of HfO₂ (k = 24) measures an EOT of 1.3 nm: how much SiO₂-equivalent thickness is not accounted for by the HfO₂? (c) On a 0.4 nm interlayer, what is the lowest EOT any top layer can give, and what HfO₂ thickness (k = 24) gives 0.7 nm?

(a) High-k term, (7.3). 4.0 nm × 3.9/20 = 0.780 nm. Stack, (7.6). tox,eq = 0.4 + 0.780 = 1.18 nm, of which the interlayer is 33.9%, from 0.4 nm of a 4.4 nm stack.

(b) HfO₂ term. 4.0 nm × 3.9/24 = 0.650 nm. Remainder. 1.3 − 0.650 = 0.650 nm: half the EOT lies in a lower-k layer.

(c) Floor. As k grows the high-k term tends to zero, so the EOT tends to 0.4 nm and never goes below it. Thickness for 0.7 nm. thigh-k = (0.7 − 0.4) × 24/3.9 = 1.85 nm, a stack only 2.25 nm thick.

1.18 nm; 0.650 nm of interlayer equivalent; floor 0.4 nm, and 1.85 nm of HfO₂ for 0.7 nm

Check. (b) repeats the reading of the notes' 12 Å capacitor, where half the EOT was also interlayer. In (c) the HfO₂ is barely thicker than a few atomic layers: most of the leakage benefit has been spent on the interlayer. That is the notes' point: the lowest-k layer sets a limit on the minimum equivalent thickness.

Method: M07 §4; formula sheet (7.6).

Charge in a DRAM cell. Illustrative inputs: a 20 fF cell. (a) Find the stored charge and the number of electrons at 1.5 V. (b) What voltage would put 1×10⁶ electrons on it? (c) What does dividing 20 fF by q alone give, and in what unit?

(a) Equation (7b.2). Q = 20×10⁻¹⁵ F × 1.5 V = 3.00×10⁻¹⁴ C = 30.0 fC; N = 3.00×10⁻¹⁴ C ÷ 1.602×10⁻¹⁹ C = 1.87×10⁵ electrons.

(b) Inverted. V = Nq/Ccell = 1×10⁶ × 1.602×10⁻¹⁹ C ÷ 20×10⁻¹⁵ F = 8.01 V.

(c) Capacitance over charge. 20×10⁻¹⁵ F ÷ 1.602×10⁻¹⁹ C = 1.25×10⁵ per volt (F/C = 1/V): electrons per volt, the count at 1 V, not the count stored.

30.0 fC, 1.87×10⁵ electrons; 8.01 V; 1.25×10⁵ electrons per volt

Check. 20 fF at 1.5 V stores the same 30 fC as the notes' 30 fF at 1 V, 1.9×10⁵ electrons. As with the notes' 1–2×10⁶, a count of 10⁶ needs more voltage than any supply the notes mention (5.3 V even at 30 fF).

Method: M07b §2; formula sheet (7b.2).

Trench depth. Illustrative inputs. A trench capacitor with a square opening 0.4 µm on a side must hold 25 fF. (a) How deep must it be with 8 nm of SiO₂? (b) How deep with 8 nm of Si₃N₄ (k = 7)?

(a) Capacitance per area, (7b.3). 3.9 × 8.854×10⁻¹² F/m ÷ 8×10⁻⁹ m = 4.32×10⁻³ F/m² = 4.32 fF/µm². Area. 25 ÷ 4.32 = 5.79 µm². Geometry. Four walls of area ad and a floor a², so d = (A − a²)/(4a) = (5.79 − 0.16) µm² ÷ (4 × 0.4 µm) = 3.52 µm.

(b) Si₃N₄. 7 × 8.854×10⁻¹² ÷ 8×10⁻⁹ = 7.75 fF/µm²; 25 ÷ 7.75 = 3.23 µm²; d = (3.23 − 0.16) ÷ 1.6 = 1.92 µm.

3.52 µm with SiO₂; 1.92 µm with Si₃N₄

Check. The SiO₂ trench is 8.8 times as deep as it is wide and its area is 36.2 times its footprint — the scale of Lecture 13's ≈ 5 µm trench. The nitride trench is 0.545 as deep, a shade less than 3.9/7 because the floor's fixed 0.16 µm² is a larger share of the smaller area.

Method: M07b §3; formula sheet (7b.3).

Mask budget for ion milling. Illustrative inputs. A 0.6 µm BST film is milled with a 20% over-etch; the BST etches at 0.8 times the rate of the mask (S = 0.8). (a) What is the minimum mask thickness? (b) How much of a 1.2 µm mask is left?

Etch time in film thickness. Clearing plus over-etch: 0.6 µm × 1.2 = 0.72 µm of BST-equivalent etching.

(a) Mask consumed, (7b.6). The mask etches 1/S times as fast: 0.72 µm ÷ 0.8 = 0.90 µm, so tmask ≥ 0.90 µm.

(b) Remaining. 1.2 − 0.90 = 0.30 µm.

At least 0.90 µm; 0.30 µm left from 1.2 µm

Check. With S below 1 the mask must be thicker than the film it patterns, as in M07b's measured-selectivity case; at S = 1 and no over-etch, 0.6 µm would just suffice.

Method: M07b §9; formula sheet (7b.6).

Subthreshold swing. Illustrative inputs. (a) A transistor has St = 75 mV/decade. By what factor is its drain current at 0.3 V below threshold smaller than at threshold, and by what factor for an ideal transistor at 300 K? (b) Another transistor's current falls by 10⁴ over 0.32 V below threshold. What is its swing?

(a) Decades. 300 mV ÷ 75 mV/decade = 4.00 decades: a factor of 1.00×10⁴. Ideal. ln 10 × 25.85 mV = 59.5 mV/decade; 300 ÷ 59.5 = 5.04 decades, a factor of 1.10×10⁵.

(b) Swing. 10⁴ is 4.00 decades over 320 mV: St = 320/4 = 80.0 mV/decade.

1.00×10⁴ (1.10×10⁵ ideal); 80.0 mV/decade

Check. (b) is the notes' Ta₂O₅ value; an ideal transistor would gain the same four decades in 238 mV. A larger swing means more off-state leakage at the same threshold.

Method: M07 §5; formula sheet (7.7).

A midgap metal gate on both transistors. TiN, taken as a midgap metal at 4.6 eV, replaces the p⁺ polysilicon gate (5.17 eV) of a p-channel transistor and the n⁺ polysilicon gate (4.05 eV) of an n-channel one, with everything else unchanged. Find the threshold shift of each.

p-channel, (7.9). ΔVT = 4.6 − 5.17 = −0.57 V.

n-channel, (7.9). ΔVT = 4.6 − 4.05 = +0.55 V.

−0.57 V (p-channel); +0.55 V (n-channel)

Check. Both magnitudes are the notes' "about 0.6 V", and both are increases in |VT|: the p-channel threshold is negative and becomes more negative. Lowering them by counter-doping gives buried channels; dual band-edge metals avoid it.

Method: M07 §9; formula sheet (7.9).

3 · Chapter 5, first part: wells and field isolation

The first part of Chapter 5 (notes pp. 68–74, taught in Lectures 15 and 16) is how the two wells of a twin-well CMOS process are made, and M08 teaches it. Its problem-and-fix runs in two stages. The diffused well — implanted at 50–100 keV, then driven in for 12–24 h above 1100 °C to 3–4 µm — spreads sideways about 0.7 times its depth, loses boron to the LOCOS field oxide grown after it, needs a channel-stop implant that then creeps into the active areas, and can only compromise between the dopings that threshold, hot carriers, punch-through, soft errors and latch-up each want. The retrograde well replaces it: implanted at 300 keV–1 MeV after the field oxide, by a chain of implants through one thick resist mask, with only a short activation anneal, so each depth keeps the doping its implant gave it. The chapter's vocabulary comes from Chapters 1–2 of the notes (the threshold equation of M03, punch-through of M02 §5, LOCOS of M02 §9), and it hands on to the second part of Chapter 5 (§4: the channel and the drain, built in these wells) and to Chapter 9 (§7: the trench isolation that replaced LOCOS).

3.1 Facts and lists

Twin-well CMOS and the starting wafer (notes pp. 10, 68)

ItemThe course's version
Twin well
M08 §1		"Each well is formed separately by implanting the well dopants into a lightly doped substrate", which "allows performance adjustments for both PMOS and NMOS tailored separately" (p. 68): threshold, punch-through margin and junction capacitance of each transistor type set by its own implant. Wolf calls it the dominant CMOS technology in production
Starting wafer
M08 §1		A p⁺ substrate carrying a lightly doped p-type epitaxial layer (notes p. 10; or the n-type equivalent); the wells are formed in the epitaxial layer, and the p⁺ substrate is a low-resistance path against latch-up
Well contacts
M08 §1		p⁺ diffusion ties the p-well to ground, n⁺ diffusion ties the n-well to VDD: each well at its transistor's source potential, so every source and drain junction stays at zero or reverse bias
Lecture values (not in the notes)
M08 §1		p⁺ substrate about 10¹⁹ cm⁻³; epitaxial layer about 10¹⁵ cm⁻³, 3–5 µm thick in earlier processes and 1–2 µm now ("about three micron" in Lecture 16); well doping 10¹⁶–10¹⁷ cm⁻³; wafer 600–750 µm (Lectures 15–16; the notes give no values; Chapter 2 gives 675, 725 and 775 µm for 150, 200 and 300 mm)
Channel stops in Figure 1
M08 §3.2		Only beneath the field oxide on the p-well: there boron is lost; under the n-well's field oxide phosphorus piles up and the positive interface charge pushes the n-type surface away from inversion

The diffused well (notes pp. 68–70)

ItemThe course's version
Implant
M08 §2.1		Moderate energy, 50–100 keV (p. 70): the dopant stays within a few tenths of a micrometre of the surface (SRIM: 0.17–0.32 µm for boron at 50–100 keV)
Drive-in
M08 §2.1		"Long 12–24 hr, high temperature (>1100 °C) diffusion" (p. 68); wells 3–4 µm deep (p. 69). Lecture 15 gave the same figures
Profile
M08 §2.1		Highest at the surface and decreasing monotonically with depth (p. 70; the handwriting reads "Drive in after drive-in")
Species
M08 §2.1		The notes name none. Plummer: boron p-well, phosphorus n-well, intrinsic diffusivities 1.4×10⁻¹³ and 1.5×10⁻¹³ cm²/s at 1100 °C, so one drive-in takes both to about the same depth. Lecture 15 also named arsenic for the n-well: 2.1×10⁻¹⁴ cm²/s, about seven times slower, and heavier, so Plummer passes it over
Dose conservation
M08 §2.1		With a capping oxide the drive-in neither adds nor removes dopant: the dose Q is fixed, the profile spreads, a thin concentrated layer becomes a deep dilute well. Equal areas on a linear axis, not on the notes' log axis
Why deep
M08 §2.2		"Deep drive-in step was needed to prevent punch through from the MOSFET drain regions at the surface to the substrate under well" (p. 68): vertical punch-through, the drain's and the well bottom's depletion regions must not meet. Depth and doping both decide it
Plummer's design example
M08 §2.1		Boron, xj = 3 µm, CB = 10¹⁵, Cs = 4×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ (from 900 Ω/□), D = 1.5×10⁻¹³ cm²/s at 1100 °C: Dt = 3.7×10⁻⁹ cm², 6.8 h, Q = 4.3×10¹³ cm⁻²

Five problems of the diffused well below 0.5 µm (notes pp. 68–69, in the notes' order)

ProblemThe notes' version, and the mechanism
1. Lateral diffusion
M08 §2.3		"Lateral (as well as vertical) diffusion … has reduced packing densities." Lateral diffusion ~0.7 × the junction depth: 3–4 µm deep gives 2–3 µm sideways; maximum aspect ratio (width to depth) about 1.6:1, narrower wells "would intermingle with the dopant of adjacent wells" (the condition is Wolf's). Lecture 15 said lateral equals vertical (flagged, §3.4)
2. Boron lost to LOCOS
M08 §3.2		"Boron from the p-well dopant implant-step segregates into the oxide during the long, high temperature field oxide growth step"; the fix, "a separate field threshold-adjust implant (channel stop implant) prior to the field oxidation step", "adds process complexity"
3. Channel stop in the active area
M08 §3.3		"Diffusion of the channel-stop implant dopant into the active regions during the field oxidation adversely affects both junction capacitance and turn off characteristics": more boron against the n⁺ junction, narrower depletion region, larger Cj; turn-off of the n-channel transistors (Wolf), mechanism not given
4. Thermal budget
M08 §2.3		"Increases process cycle time, and increase in risk of Si wafer warpage, bow, flatness degradation": silicon is plastically soft near 1100 °C and slips under ramp stresses (ULSI spec: bow < 5 µm, flatness < 1 µm). Wolf adds metal contamination from the furnace elements
5. Compromise profile
M08 §2.3		"Compromises between the need to control threshold-voltage, to suppress hot-carrier effects, to control punch through and soft error effects, and to provide latch up immunity." Threshold wants the surface doping set; hot carriers want lighter doping at the drain; punch-through and soft errors want heavier doping below the surface; latch-up wants heavy doping deep. Dose and drive-in fix one monotonic profile

Field isolation (notes p. 69; field-oxide thickness from Chapter 3)

ItemThe course's version
Field-oxide thickness
M08 §3.1		Notes' Chapter 3 table: 200–400 nm for LOCOS (M05 §1); the lectures: about 1 µm (as M02 §9); Plummer's flow: about 0.5 µm
Field transistor
M08 §3.1		Any conductor crossing a field region (metal output line, polysilicon line) is the gate of a parasitic MOSFET: field oxide as gate insulator, p-type surface as channel, neighbouring n⁺ diffusions as source and drain. Under the output line it would short the two wells (Lecture 15). Plummer: field oxide transistors
Requirement
M08 §3.1		VT,field above the highest line voltage, the supply, with margin ("if you apply five volts you do not want any channel formation here", Lecture 16). Thick oxide and heavily doped surface raise it; the p-type field region is the vulnerable one
Segregation coefficient
M08 §3.2		m = CSi/CSiO₂: boron about 0.3, taken into the growing oxide, silicon beneath depleted; phosphorus, arsenic, antimony about 10, rejected, pile up ahead of the interface (Plummer §7.5.4; temperature-dependent, boron also on ambient moisture). Plummer's simulation: interface silicon keeps about a quarter of its boron; phosphorus piles up to about 1.4×
Wording
M08 §3.2		The notes' word is "segregates" (Lecture 16 said "boron penetration", flagged, §3.4)
Channel-stop implant
M08 §3.3		Boron into the p-type field regions before field oxidation, enough that after segregation VT,field stays above the supply. Plummer: about 10¹³ cm⁻² at 50 keV through the LOCOS nitride openings, resist/nitride/pad oxide masking the active areas; most diffuses ahead of the oxide, some is lost into it
Field thresholds, 0.5 µm oxide (constructed)
M08 §3.1		4.45 V at 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ (below a 5 V supply); 2.26 V at 5×10¹⁵ (half the boron lost); 14.0 V at 5×10¹⁶ (channel stop). At 1 µm: 9.1, 4.7, 28 V; at 0.3 µm: 2.6, 1.3, 8.3 V. The same 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ under a 10 nm gate oxide: −0.1 V

The retrograde well (notes pp. 70–71)

ItemThe course's version
Definition
M08 §4		"Medium energy (50–100 keV) is replaced with high energy (300 keV – 1 MeV) implant. This leaves the peak of the implant location deeper in Si substrate. This is retrograde." Concentration rises with depth below the surface
Implanter
M08 §4.1		Most cover 30–200 keV for singly charged ions; doubly charged ions double the energy at lower current; MeV energies from a small linear accelerator (Plummer; Lecture 16 named the linear accelerator)
Peak depth
M08 §4.1		Wolf: "roughly 1-µm for the energies most frequently used"; the notes' p. 70 sketch about 0.6 µm (a sketch). SRIM Rp: boron 0.79 µm at 300 keV, 1.13 µm at 500 keV; phosphorus 0.73 µm at 600 keV. Lecture 16: about 0.8 µm against about 0.1 µm at 50–100 keV (right order; about right for phosphorus, 0.07–0.13 µm, short for boron). Phosphorus needs about twice boron's energy for the same depth
Surface of the implant
M08 §4.1		The Gaussian fits only near the peak; boron is skewed toward the surface. Wolf's 400 keV boron p-well: a few times 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ at the surface, about a decade below the peak; the retrograde implant's own surface contribution should be at least five times below the final surface value
Advantage: lateral spread
M08 §4.2		"Much less lateral-dopant spread … for a retrograde well having the same junction depth as a diffused well." "Lateral straggle for 500 keV is ~0.7 µm. Lateral spread of dopant reaches ~1 µm in the process" (against at least 2 µm for a diffused well, Wolf). (SRIM's standard deviation: 0.18 µm for boron at 500 keV; flagged, §3.4)
Anneal
M08 §4.3		"Very little additional diffusion occurs after implantation as a short anneal is performed at a relatively low temp … to activate the implant." Plummer: about 10 s at 1000 °C or about 30 min at 800 °C; Lecture 16: 800–900 °C, or about 1000 °C briefly; Wolf: RTP well "drive-ins" of about 5 min. The peak stays at Rp
Order
M08 §4.4		"High energy implanted wells are formed after the long, high temperature cycle needed to grow the LOCOS field oxide, lateral-spread of the well dopant is further reduced" (p. 71). Also: no boron segregates, so the p-well field threshold stays higher (Wolf, after Rung et al. 1981) — removes problems 2 and 3
n⁺-to-p⁺ spacing
M08 §4.5		"The 7–9 µm n⁺ to p⁺ spacing is required for twin-well 1.2 µm technology. This can be reduced to 5-µm if retrograde wells are used instead. This permits higher circuit packing densities" (29–44% less). Measured from an n⁺ diffusion in the p-well to a p⁺ diffusion in the n-well
Well engineering
M08 §4.6		High-energy implant + lower-energy channel implants (VT adjust, punch-through stopper) + minimal diffusion = control of the doping throughout the depth. Optimises: device speed; carrier mobility and S/D capacitance; inter-device leakage; VT control; punch-through suppression; parasitic transistor effect; soft-error and latch-up resistance (p. 71)

The other advantages of retrograde wells (notes pp. 71–72, in the notes' order)

AdvantageThe notes' version, and the mechanism
1. Retarding field
M08 §5.2		"A retarded electric field is created in the parasitic-vertical bipolar transistor, thereby reducing its current gain." Donor doping rising from the p⁺ emitter toward the buried peak gives a field pointing up that pushes injected holes back; about 1.8 kV/cm one straggle above the 600 keV peak (beyond the notes)
2. Vertical punch-through
M08 §5.2		"Susceptibility to vertical punch-through is reduced without having to significantly raise the surface doping concentration": doping at the bottom narrows both depletion regions (clearance 1.14 µm at 10¹⁶, 0.31 µm at 10¹⁷ cm⁻³)
3. Conductive bottom
M08 §5.2		"The conductivity in the bottom of the well is increased, which also provides additional resistance against latch up": lower Rwell, larger current needed to drop 0.7 V, stronger shunt of the pnp's emitter junction
4. Shallower parasitic drains
M08 §5.2		"The shallower well depth should also make the drain of the parasitic n⁺-to n well (or p⁺-to p-well) parasitic FETs shallower … improvements in both the short channel threshold-roll off effect" [and their punch-through, Wolf p. 185; the notes' sentence stops after the first item]
5. S/D capacitance
M08 §5.2		"A reduced doping concentration under the bottom of the source and drain regions, which reduces the S/D-to body capacitance": Cj = εSi/W, W ∝ N−1/2. 10¹⁷ → 91.7 nF/cm², 10¹⁶ → 29.9 nF/cm²

Latch-up (notes pp. 71–72; Plummer, Sze & Ng)

ItemThe course's version
Vertical pnp
M08 §5.1		Emitter: p⁺ source of the p-channel transistor; base: n-well; collector: p-type substrate
Lateral npn
M08 §5.1		Emitter: n⁺ source of the n-channel transistor; base: p-well and substrate; collector: n-well. The pnp's collector is the npn's base and vice versa: a pnpn thyristor
Trigger
M08 §5.1		A transient current in the well or substrate drops about 0.7 V across Rwell or Rsub, forward-biasing an emitter junction; each collector current feeds the other's base. A particle strike is one trigger (single-event latch-up)
Consequence
M08 §5.1		A large current from VDD to ground that can destroy the chip; it persists until the supply is removed
Condition
M08 §5.1		With no shunting resistance: αnpn + αpnp ≥ 1, equivalently βnpnβpnp ≥ 1. Low well and substrate resistances raise the gain needed
Remedies
M08 §5.1		Lower a gain or a resistance: lightly doped epitaxial layer on a heavily doped substrate (Plummer); a MeV buried layer in a bulk wafer; the retrograde well (advantages 1 and 3)

Process integration (notes pp. 72–74)

ItemThe course's version
Thick resist
M08 §6.1		"A thick photo-resist as mask material 2.0–2.5 µm and special exposure and development techniques are required" (p. 72; again on p. 74). Rule of thumb t ≥ Rp + 5ΔRp in the resist: 600 keV P about 2.05 µm, 300 keV B 2.08 µm, 500 keV B 2.84 µm. Plummer: "thick (2-micron)", often not hard-baked. Wolf: tall walls shadow tilted beams, countered by splitting the dose into four rotations of the wafer
DRAM wells
M08 §6.2		"Require higher doses than those used in logic devices" (p. 73); Wolf: better suppression of minority carriers, lower soft-error rate; the higher dose needs a higher energy to keep its tail out of the channel
Chained implant
M08 §6.2		Well implant "performed together with VT adjust, punchthrough and channel stop isolation implants. All the implants are done in the same implanter. Highest energy implantation is done first and low energy implantation is done last." One resist mask per well; the SEMATECH comparison (Wolf): lower cycle time and cost. Implanter span: up to about 1 MeV down to about 10 keV (Wolf)
Why highest energy first
M08 §6.2		Wolf, not the notes: "ensures that no ions traverse the end-of-range (EOR) damage of a previous implant"
Modulated
M08 §6.4		"Modulated characteristics means that the doping concentration profile has several maxima and minima" (p. 74). n-well sum: maximum near the surface, minimum near 0.35 µm, well maximum at 0.73 µm, 150 keV shoulder between
Under the field oxide
M08 §6.4		Each profile sits about one oxide thickness shallower (boron goes about 10% farther in SiO₂, phosphorus up to about 6%). Under 0.3 µm of field oxide: threshold implants stop in the oxide; 150 keV boron peaks 0.18 µm into the silicon, surface about 4×10¹⁶ cm⁻³, field threshold about 7 V (5.5×10¹⁶ and 8.8 V if oxide stops as silicon); n-well surface about 8×10¹⁶ cm⁻³; well implants peak 0.44–0.51 µm below the silicon surface. The 150 keV implant is the punch-through stopper in the active area and the channel stop under the field oxide
Results
M08 §6.3		"Very good interwell isolation is achieved"; "Minimum p⁺ to n⁺ spacing 40% lower than the same process without retrograde wells" (p. 74; a separate process from the 7–9 → 5 µm example). Wolf: intra-well isolation of 0.5 µm between like diffusions without an extra channel stop
Two junctions?
M08 §6.4		600 keV P alone into 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ p-type would meet M03's (3.10) on the Gaussian (0.73 > 0.53 µm), but the real shallow tail does not follow the Gaussian; in the chain the 150 and 70 keV implants fill the top with donors: one junction, at the well bottom

3.2 Problems and fixes, and the process flows

XIts problemThe fix§

Deep drive-inLateral diffusion ~0.7xj (2–3 µm), wells ≥ 1.6× their depth wide, low packing densityHigh-energy implant, little diffusion: lateral spread ~1 µm; n⁺-to-p⁺ 7–9 → 5 µmM08 §2.3, §4.2, §4.5
12–24 h above 1100 °CCycle time; warpage, bow, flatnessShort low-temperature activation anneal (RTP, about 5 min)M08 §2.3, §4.3
Line over field oxideParasitic field transistor inverts the p-type surface, joins two n⁺ diffusionsThick field oxide and a heavily doped surface: VT,field above the supplyM08 §3.1
LOCOS grown after the p-wellBoron segregates into the oxide (m ≈ 0.3), smaller Qd, lower VT,field (4.4 → 2.3 V)Channel-stop boron implant before field oxidation (adds a step)M08 §3.2, §3.3
Channel stopDiffuses into the active area in the field oxidation: junction capacitance, turn-offImplant the wells after the field oxide; the chain's 150 keV implant is the channel stopM08 §2.3, §4.4, §6.4
Monotonic diffused profileCompromise among VT, hot carriers, punch-through, soft errors, latch-upWell engineering: each implant's dopant at its own depthM08 §2.3, §4.6
Parasitic pnp + npnLatch-up: thyristor from VDD to ground once triggeredEpi on p⁺ substrate; buried layer; retrograde well (retarding field, conductive bottom)M08 §5.1, §5.2

The two sequences side by side, wafer to gate oxide (notes p. 74; Wolf Fig. 5-4; M08 §8)

StageDiffused twin well, with LOCOSRetrograde twin well
Wafer
M08 §1		p⁺ substrate, lightly doped p-type epitaxial layerthe same
Wells
M08 §2.1		1 · n-well: phosphorus, 50–100 keV, through resist
2 · p-well: boron
3 · drive-in, 12–24 h above 1100 °C, to 3–4 µm		— (wells come after the isolation)
Isolation
M08 §3.3, §4.4		4 · LOCOS mask (pad oxide; nitride over the active areas) and channel-stop boron into the p-well field regions — needed because the boron is already there
5 · field oxidation: p-well boron segregates into the oxide; wells driven further		1 · LOCOS mask: pad oxide, nitride over the active areas
2 · field oxidation — or, for STI, trench etch, oxide fill, polish
Wells
M08 §6		— (already formed)3 · n-well: thick resist (2.0–2.5 µm); P at 600, 150, 70 keV, highest first, one implanter, through the field oxide
4 · p-well: thick resist; B at 300, 150, 30 keV
5 · short activation anneal, rapid thermal (Wolf: about 5 min); no long drive-in
Threshold and punch-through
M08 §8		6 · threshold and punch-through implants into each well in turn under resist (Wolf: B⁺ and As⁺ into the n-well, B⁺ into the p-well)— (inside the chain: the 150 keV and the 70 or 30 keV implants)

Lecture 16 drew the retrograde flow without LOCOS and with a long drive-in to 2–3 µm; the notes implant the wells through LOCOS islands (p. 74) and follow them with a short anneal only (§3.4).

After the wells, both sequences (Wolf "up to gate oxidation"; M08 §8)

  1. Gate oxide (M05, M06; high-k, M07).
  2. Polysilicon gate, deposited and patterned: n⁺ for both types in Chapter 1's process, dual-doped later.
  3. LDD or source–drain extension implants: n-type into the p-well under resist over the n-well, then p-type into the n-well.
  4. Halo or pocket implant, tilted, self-aligned to the gate, after the extension and before the spacer, so that it lies only beside the tips of the extensions, where punch-through starts in a short channel.
  5. Sidewall spacer, conformal deposition and etch-back.
  6. Deep source–drain contact implants, one well type at a time under resist.
  7. Activation anneal; no long drive-in in either sequence once the gate steps begin.
  8. Silicide (M10 treats the source–drain steps in full).

The modulated well on shallow trench isolation (notes p. 74, right column; M08 §7)

  1. Shallow trench formation: trenches etched, filled with deposited oxide, polished flat — no bird's beak, no surface step (M15).
  2. Modulated n-well implants and VTp adjust, through a resist mask over the p-well area ("n-well photo").
  3. Modulated p-well implants and VTn adjust, through a resist mask over the n-well area. Thick PR (2.0–2.5 µm) for the high-energy implants; the trench oxide absorbs part of each range as LOCOS does.

The chained-implant recipe (notes pp. 73–74; Wolf p. 187, from Tsukamoto et al. 1992; M08 §6.3)

Well · orderIon, energyDose (cm⁻²)Role, in the notes' wordsRp (µm)Cp (cm⁻³)
n-well · 1
M08 §6.3		P, 600 keV3×10¹³"Retrograde well formation"0.738.3×10¹⁷
n-well · 2P, 150 keV4×10¹²"punch-through prevention and intra field isolation (channel stop)"0.202.5×10¹⁷
n-well · 3P, 70 keV6×10¹²"sets the threshold voltage"0.0956.6×10¹⁷
p-well · 1B, 300 keV3×10¹³"Retrograde p-well implant"0.799.0×10¹⁷

Rp and Cp = Q/(√(2π)ΔRp) are M08's, SRIM-2013, each implant alone in bare silicon; straggles P 0.144, 0.063, 0.036 µm, B 0.133, 0.105, ≈0.048 µm. Boron at 300 keV matches phosphorus at 600 keV in depth (lighter ion, longer range); the threshold implants match at about 0.1 µm; the 150 keV pair does not (0.45 against 0.20 µm). Lecture 16 dictated the third implant as boron in both wells, for the field oxide (flagged, §3.4); the notes' p. 74 sketch writes "P + P + As⁺" — follow the recipe, all phosphorus.

Three implants called punch-through (M08 §8)

ImplantWhen and howWhere it acts
1 · Chapter 1's blanket anti-punch-through
M02 §5.1; notes p. 5		Boron over the whole wafer, no mask, after the threshold implant; introduced for n-channel devices as gate lengths approached 1 µmPeak below the channel, where the source and drain depletion regions approach
2 · Chapter 5's chained stopper
M08 §8, §6; notes pp. 73–74		P 150 keV 4×10¹² (n-well), B 150 keV 4×10¹² (p-well), through the well's resist, before the threshold implant (highest energy first), before any gate existsBelow the channel in the active area; at the surface under the field oxide, where it is the channel stop
3 · Halo or pocket
M09 §6, M02 §5.4; notes pp. 89–90		Dopant of the well's own type, after the gate is patterned, self-aligned, tilted, after the extension and before the spacerBeside the tips of the extensions, where short-channel punch-through starts. Lecture 16's implant "after the source–drain formation" is this one

The chapter in one line

The diffused well takes its depth from a drive-in, so its profile is one monotonic curve and it must precede the field oxide; the retrograde well takes each depth from an implant energy, so it follows the isolation, keeps its boron, needs no drive-in, and puts the doping where each job wants it — about 0.1 µm for VT, 0.20–0.45 µm for punch-through and channel stop, 0.73–0.79 µm for latch-up and vertical punch-through.

3.4 Where the course and the literature differ

PointThe course's version (use it in answers)The literature or the notes' source§
Lateral diffusion factor~0.7 × the junction depth (p. 69)Two-dimensional solution under a sharp mask edge: 0.77–0.87 for surface-to-background ratios 10²–10⁴. Lecture 15: lateral = vertical (wrong)M08 §2.3
Aspect ratioMaximum about 1.6:1 (p. 69)Wolf supplies the dropped condition (narrower wells intermingle). 2 × 0.7 = 1.4; M08's reading: 1.6 = 2 × 0.8, or 1.4 with margin. Use the two figures separatelyM08 §2.3
Lateral straggle~0.7 µm at 500 keV (p. 70, Wolf's figure)As a standard deviation, 0.18 µm for B at 500 keV (SRIM). M08's reading: the sideways reach to the background, 3–4 straggles (0.53–0.65 µm as implanted)M08 §4.2
