3.5 Self-check

Choose one. The diffused well's recipe in the notes — (a) 300 keV–1 MeV implant, 5 min RTP (b) 50–100 keV implant, 12–24 h above 1100 °C, wells 3–4 µm deep (c) 50–100 keV implant, 30 min at 800 °C, wells 1 µm deep (d) 600 keV implant, 12–24 h at 900 °C

(b). Moderate energy leaves the dopant near the surface, and the long drive-in diffuses it to 3–4 µm; (a) is the retrograde well. M08 §2.1.

Choose one. During the LOCOS field oxidation of a diffused-well process, the p-well's boron — (a) piles up in the silicon ahead of the interface (b) is taken into the growing oxide, depleting the silicon beneath (c) penetrates the gate oxide into the channel (d) is unaffected, since the oxide is grown after the drive-in

(b). Boron's segregation coefficient m = CSi/CSiO₂ ≈ 0.3, so the oxide takes it up as the interface sweeps through; phosphorus (m ≈ 10) piles up instead, (a). (c) is boron penetration, a gate problem. M08 §3.2.

Choose one. Under a fixed field oxide, halving the boron at the p-type surface makes the field threshold — (a) increase (b) decrease (c) not change (d) none of the above

(b). Less boron is a smaller depletion charge Qd, and |Qd|/CFOX is the term that holds VT,field up: 4.45 → 2.26 V under 0.5 µm of oxide. M08 §3.1.

Choose one. A short activation anneal after a 600 keV well implant makes the depth of the implant's peak — (a) increase (b) decrease (c) not change (d) none of the above

(c). Far from the surface the profile spreads symmetrically about Rp: σ2 = ΔRp2 + 2Dt, the peak height falls and its depth stays at 0.727 µm. Lecture 16 said the peak moves deeper; the notes and M08 do not. M08 §4.3.

Choose one. In the chained-implant n-well, the phosphorus implant at 150 keV, 4×10¹² cm⁻² — (a) forms the well bottom (b) sets the threshold voltage (c) prevents punch-through in the active area and acts as the channel stop under the field oxide (d) compensates boron lost to the field oxide

(c). The notes give it two jobs: "punch-through prevention and intra field isolation (channel stop)". Below the channel in the active area, it lies at the silicon surface under the field oxide. (a) is 600 keV, (b) 70 keV; (d) is how Lecture 16 described the third implant, which the notes do not support. M08 §6.3, §6.4.

Choose one. The implants of a chained retrograde well are done highest energy first because — (a) high-energy implanters warm up first (b) no later ion then crosses an earlier implant's end-of-range damage (c) the resist shrinks less at high energy (d) the threshold implant must be annealed last

(b). Wolf's reason; the notes state the order without it. A shallower implant done afterwards stops above the deep damage. M08 §6.2.

Choose one. For the twin-well 1.2 µm technology the notes give an n⁺-to-p⁺ spacing with retrograde wells of — (a) 7–9 µm (b) 5 µm (c) 3.6 µm (d) 1 µm

(b), down from 7–9 µm with diffused wells, 29–44% less, for packing density. M08 §4.5.

Choose one. Doping the bottom of a retrograde n-well more heavily makes the transient current needed to trigger latch-up — (a) increase (b) decrease (c) not change (d) none of the above

(a). The heavily doped bottom lowers Rwell, so a larger current is needed to drop the 0.7 V that forward-biases the pnp's emitter junction, and the retarding field lowers the pnp's gain (advantages 3 and 1). M08 §5.2.

The diffused well, its problems below 0.5 µm, and the fix — with a sketch of the two profiles.

A diffused well is implanted at 50–100 keV, which leaves the dopant within a few tenths of a micrometre of the surface, then driven in for 12–24 h above 1100 °C to 3–4 µm; the dose is conserved and the profile is highest at the surface, falling monotonically. It had to be deep to stop vertical punch-through from the drain to the substrate. The notes list five problems below 0.5 µm: (1) lateral diffusion of about 0.7 times the depth, 2–3 µm, so wells narrower than about 1.6 times their depth intermingle and packing density falls; (2) with LOCOS grown afterwards, the p-well's boron segregates into the field oxide and a separate channel-stop implant is needed; (3) that channel stop diffuses into the active areas, raising junction capacitance and degrading turn-off; (4) the long drive-in costs cycle time and risks warpage, bow and flatness; (5) one monotonic profile can only compromise between threshold, hot carriers, punch-through, soft errors and latch-up. The fix is the retrograde well: a 300 keV–1 MeV implant puts the peak deep (0.73 µm for phosphorus at 600 keV), it is implanted after the field oxide (no segregation, less spread), and only a short activation anneal follows, so the lateral spread stays about 1 µm and the chained shallower implants set the surface separately.

Full treatment: M08 §2.3 and §4.

The field transistor, boron segregation and the channel stop: what each is, and how they connect.

Any conductor crossing a field region is the gate of a parasitic MOSFET: the field oxide is its gate insulator, the p-type surface beneath its channel and the neighbouring n⁺ diffusions its source and drain. Its threshold is M03's equation with CFOX = 3.9ε0/tFOX, and it must exceed the supply; both charge terms scale with tFOX, and the surface doping acts through Qd (4.45 V at 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ under 0.5 µm). In the diffused-well flow the field oxide grows after the wells, and boron, with m = CSi/CSiO₂ ≈ 0.3, segregates into it, leaving the silicon beneath depleted of boron; the smaller Qd lowers the field threshold (2.26 V with half the boron). The channel-stop implant restores it: boron into the p-type field regions through the LOCOS nitride openings before oxidation (Plummer: about 10¹³ cm⁻² at 50 keV), enough that the surface still holds the threshold above the supply (14.0 V at 5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³). It costs a step, and during the field oxidation it diffuses into the active areas against the n⁺ junctions (problem 3). The n-well side needs none: phosphorus piles up there. Wells implanted after the field oxide lose no boron, and the chain's 150 keV implant becomes the channel stop.

Full treatment: M08 §3.

How is a chained-implant retrograde twin well made, step by step, on LOCOS?
  1. Start from a p⁺ substrate with a lightly doped p-type epitaxial layer.
  2. LOCOS: pad oxide, patterned nitride over the active areas, field oxidation — before the wells, so no well boron segregates.
  3. n-well mask: thick resist, 2.0–2.5 µm, over the p-well area (special exposure and development).
  4. In one implanter, highest energy first: phosphorus 600 keV, 3×10¹³ cm⁻² (well, peak 0.73 µm); 150 keV, 4×10¹² (punch-through stopper, and channel stop under the field oxide); 70 keV, 6×10¹² (threshold). The implants pass through the field oxide, so the well bottom steps up beneath it.
  5. p-well mask over the n-well area; boron 300 keV, 3×10¹³; 150 keV, 4×10¹²; 30 keV, 5.5×10¹² cm⁻².
  6. Short activation anneal (RTP, about 5 min); no long drive-in, so the modulated profile — several maxima and minima — survives.
  7. Gate oxide and the shared steps that follow.

Full treatment: M08 §6 and §8.

What is latch-up, and what does the retrograde well do about it — with a sketch.

A CMOS structure contains two parasitic bipolar transistors. The vertical pnp has the p⁺ source of the p-channel transistor as emitter, the n-well as base and the p-type substrate as collector; the lateral npn has the n⁺ source of the n-channel transistor as emitter, the p-well and substrate as base and the n-well as collector. Each one's collector is the other's base, a pnpn thyristor. Normally every emitter junction sits at zero bias, but a transient current in the well or substrate that drops about 0.7 V across Rwell or Rsub turns one transistor on; its collector current turns on the other, which feeds the first. With no shunting resistance the loop holds once αnpn + αpnp ≥ 1; it can carry a large current from VDD to ground until the supply is removed. Remedies lower a gain or a resistance: an epitaxial layer on a heavily doped substrate, a buried layer, or the retrograde well, whose doping rising toward the buried peak creates a retarding field that lowers the pnp's gain (advantage 1) and whose heavily doped bottom lowers Rwell (advantage 3).

Full treatment: M08 §5.1 and §5.2.

Dose of a shallow layer. An implanted layer 0.05 µm thick at a uniform 4×10¹⁸ cm⁻³ is driven in until the well is 2.5 µm deep (illustrative figures). Find the dose and the mean concentration of the finished well.

Dose. Q = 4×10¹⁸ cm⁻³ × 0.05×10⁻⁴ cm = 2×10¹³ cm⁻².

Mean concentration. The drive-in conserves Q: 2×10¹³ cm⁻² ÷ 2.5×10⁻⁴ cm = 8×10¹⁶ cm⁻³.

Q = 2×10¹³ cm⁻²; the 2.5 µm well averages 8×10¹⁶ cm⁻³

Check. Inside the lectures' 10¹⁶–10¹⁷ cm⁻³ well doping; the profile is higher near the surface and lower near the junction, and none of it is near the 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ background.

Method: M08 §2.1; formula sheet (8.1).

Drive time and depth. Plummer's dose, 4.3×10¹³ cm⁻² of boron, into CB = 10¹⁵ cm⁻³ at 1100 °C (D = 1.5×10⁻¹³ cm²/s), is driven in for 3 h and for 12 h (illustrative times). Find the junction depth each time, and the ratio.

3 h. t = 10 800 s; Dt = 1.62×10⁻⁹ cm²; Cs = Q/√(πDt) = 6.03×10¹⁷ cm⁻³; ln(603) = 6.40; xj = 2√(1.62×10⁻⁹ × 6.40) = 2.04 µm.

12 h. t = 43 200 s; Dt = 6.48×10⁻⁹ cm²; Cs = 3.01×10¹⁷ cm⁻³; ln(301) = 5.71; xj = 3.85 µm.

Ratio. 3.85/2.04 = 1.89 for four times the time.

2.04 µm after 3 h, 3.85 µm after 12 h: ×1.89 for ×4 in time

Check. √4 = 2; the ratio is a little lower because Cs halves as the dose spreads and the logarithm shrinks. 12 h gives the notes' 3–4 µm; M08's 6.8 h gives 3 µm, between the two.

Method: M08 §2.1; formula sheet (8.1), (8.2).

Lateral spread. A diffused well is driven to 2.5 µm (illustrative). By the notes' rules, how far does it spread beneath the mask edge, and what is the narrowest such well?

Lateral spread, (8.5). 0.7 × 2.5 µm = 1.75 µm.

Narrowest well. Width ≥ 1.6 × depth = 1.6 × 2.5 µm = 4.0 µm.

1.75 µm of sideways spread; wells at least 4.0 µm wide

Check. Use the two notes' figures separately: the 1.6:1 width is not twice the 0.7 spread (2 × 0.7 = 1.4), and M08 does not derive one from the other. The spread is the notes' 2–3 µm scaled to a shallower well.

Method: M08 §2.3; formula sheet (8.5).

Spacing saved. A process's n⁺-to-p⁺ spacing falls from 6.5 µm with diffused wells to 4.5 µm with retrograde wells (illustrative figures). Find the reduction.

Fractional reduction. (6.5 − 4.5)/6.5 = 30.8%.

30.8% less spacing

Check. Inside the 29–44% of the notes' 1.2 µm example and below the chained process's 40%.

Method: M08 §4.5.

Junction capacitance. An n⁺ drain at 10²⁰ cm⁻³, zero bias, sits on a p-type body doped 5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³ or 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ (illustrative dopings; course constants). Find the depletion width and the capacitance per unit area of each, and their ratio.

5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³. Vbi = 0.0259 ln(10²⁰ × 5×10¹⁶/2.25×10²⁰) = 0.975 V; W = √(2 × 1.044×10⁻¹² × 0.975/(q × 5×10¹⁶)) = 0.159 µm; Cj = εSi/W = 65.5 nF/cm².

5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³. Vbi = 1.03 V; W = 0.0519 µm; Cj = 201 nF/cm².

Ratio. 201/65.5 = 3.07.

0.159 µm and 65.5 nF/cm²; 0.0519 µm and 201 nF/cm²: ten times the doping, 3.07 times the capacitance

Check. W ∝ (Vbi/N)1/2: √10 = 3.16, a little reduced by the larger Vbi, as in M08's 10¹⁶/10¹⁷ pair (29.9 and 91.7 nF/cm²). More doping, narrower depletion, larger capacitance.

Method: M08 §5.2; formula sheet (8.12), (2.4), (2.5).

A heavier well implant. The n-well's 600 keV phosphorus implant is raised to 5×10¹³ cm⁻² (illustrative dose, as a DRAM well might take), with SRIM's ΔRp = 0.144 µm. Find the peak concentration.

Units. √(2π) × 1.44×10⁻⁵ cm = 3.61×10⁻⁵ cm.

Peak. Cp = 5×10¹³/3.61×10⁻⁵ = 1.39×10¹⁸ cm⁻³, at Rp = 0.73 µm.

Cp = 1.39×10¹⁸ cm⁻³ at 0.73 µm

Check. The peak scales with the dose: 5/3 = 1.67 × M08's 8.3×10¹⁷ cm⁻³. The energy, not the dose, sets the depth.

Method: M08 §6.3; formula sheet (3.9).

Field threshold under 0.4 µm. A field oxide tFOX = 0.4 µm, inside the notes' 200–400 nm, over a p-type surface; Ni = 10¹¹ cm⁻²; an n⁺ polysilicon line, φMS = −(Eg/2q + φF). Find VT,field at NA = 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ and with a channel stop at 5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³ (M08's illustrative dopings; course constants).

Oxide capacitance. CFOX = 3.9 × 8.85×10⁻¹⁴/0.4×10⁻⁴ = 8.63×10⁻⁹ F/cm².

Interface charge term. Qi/CFOX = 1.857 V, entering as −1.857 V.

10¹⁶ cm⁻³. φF = 0.347 V, 2φF = 0.695 V, φMS = −0.907 V; Wm = 0.301 µm, |Qd|/CFOX = 5.587 V. Sum: −0.907 − 1.857 + 5.587 + 0.695 = 3.52 V.

5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³. φF = 0.389 V, 2φF = 0.778 V, φMS = −0.949 V; Wm = 0.142 µm, |Qd|/CFOX = 13.22 V. Sum: −0.949 − 1.857 + 13.22 + 0.778 = 11.2 V.

VT,field = 3.52 V at 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ (below a 5 V supply); 11.2 V with the channel stop

Check. Between M08's 0.5 µm values (4.45 and 14.0 V) and its 0.3 µm values (2.6 and 8.3 V): both charge terms scale with tFOX. With half the boron lost (5×10¹⁵ cm⁻³) the same oxide gives 1.76 V: the thinner field oxide makes the channel stop indispensable.

Method: M08 §3.1; formula sheet (8.6).

4 · Chapter 5, second part: the channel and the source/drain

Notes pp. 75–90 finish Chapter 5, taught in Lectures 17 and 18 and the recorded class of 23 October. With the wells of §3 in place, they shape the doping under the gate and rebuild the source and drain for gate lengths of 0.13 µm and below. M09 takes the channel: at 0.13 µm punch-through needs a mean doping above 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ while the surface must stay below it, so the profile becomes super-steep retrograde, implanted with the heavy ions indium, arsenic and antimony, and pocket implants then add punch-through doping only at the two ends of the channel. M10 takes the source and drain: a shallow extension and a deeper silicided contact, four series resistances held to about 10 % of the channel's own resistance, the silicon the silicide consumes, the elevated source/drain, and the sub-keV implant and spike anneal that make an ultra-shallow junction. Two effects run through both modules, channelling, which a tilt, a screen oxide or a low energy suppresses, and transient enhanced diffusion, which low energies and fast anneals suppress. Both modules end where ion implantation may no longer suffice: the 7 nm step of the 50 nm channel, and the 20–25 nm extension of the same node.

4.1 Facts and lists

What the channel doping must do (notes p. 75)

ItemThe course's version
Punch-through
M09 §1.1		For a 0.13 µm gate the doping between source and drain must exceed 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ (notes; Wolf: the mean channel doping). The professor's rule: no punch-through while L > 2W (Lectures 3 and 17), with W the depletion width of one source or drain junction; W ∝ NA−1/2, so more doping, shorter W
Mobility
M09 §1.1		To avoid a loss of mobility the doping is kept below 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ (notes; Wolf: the doping at the surface, for the carriers to follow the universal mobility curve). Two mechanisms: ionised dopants in the inversion layer scatter the carriers; the depletion charge adds to the transverse field that presses them against the interface
The resolution
M09 §1.1		Surface concentration low, mean concentration above the value: a vertical retrograde profile, and, because the change from light to heavy happens over a short distance, a super-steep retrograde (SSR) profile (p. 75)
Inversion layer
M09 §5.4		A few nanometres thick against the oxide; typically thinner than 7 nm (notes p. 76). Punch-through is decided tens of nanometres down, by the doping averaged over the junction depletion depth
Names for the buried heavy layer
M09 §1.1		"SSR" or "punch-through-control layer" (notes: "punch through control" region, "retrograde" profile). Lecture 17 also said "punch-through implantation" and "retrograde implantation" — and called it "halo" (flagged in the box below)

The super-steep retrograde layer is not the halo

Lecture 17 drew the heavy layer beneath the channel and said it "is called halo". In the notes (pp. 89–90), Wolf, Plummer and Taur & Ning the halo and the pocket are lateral structures. The SSR layer varies in depth: a blanket layer under the whole channel, implanted before the gate exists, in place of the threshold implant. The halo and pocket vary along the channel: implanted after the gate is patterned, self-aligned to it, with dopant only at the two channel ends beside the source and drain. Use "SSR" for the vertical profile and "halo" or "pocket" for the lateral one (M09 §1.1).

Super-steep retrograde channels, 0.18 to 0.1 µm (notes pp. 75–76)

ItemThe course's version
What it replaces
M09 §5.2		The conventional threshold-adjust implant (Wolf): the 30 keV, 5.5×10¹² cm⁻² boron step at the end of M08's chained recipe (notes p. 74). Made by ion implantation from the 0.18 µm node to about 0.1 µm
Species
M09 §5.2		n-channel: indium replaces boron. p-channel: arsenic or antimony replaces phosphorus (p. 75)
The notes' two reasons
M09 §2, §4.4		(1) Heavier ions have a smaller straggle, so a steeper as-implanted profile; (2) lower diffusivity, they "provide decreased spread during subsequent thermal process" (p. 76). The first holds as implanted; whether the second survives TED is disputed (§4.4 below)
Energies and profile
M09 §5.2		In and As implanted at 100–200 keV: about 5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³ at the gate-oxide interface and a peak of about 10¹⁸ cm⁻³ 80 nm below it (notes p. 76; Wolf p. 193; the notes say "below the interface", M09 follows Wolf's "at"). SRIM places an indium peak at 80 nm near 160 keV. The implant's own Gaussian tail gives only 3×10¹⁴ cm⁻³ at the surface; the sources do not say what sets the 5×10¹⁶
Indium's limits
M09 §5.2		Solid solubility caps the peak near 10¹⁸ cm⁻³ (Wolf); a deep acceptor, only partly ionised at room temperature (M02 §5.3)
Indium source
M09 §5.3		Solid InCl₃ in the source vaporiser (p. 76). Wolf: hygroscopic (long outgassing); low melting point (a second vaporiser's heat evaporates it): charge lost, source coated. Indium coating the beamline is knocked loose later: cross-contamination. Fixes: active source cooling; an electrostatic filter near the end station
What SSR achieved
M09 §5.2		Less short-channel effect and DIBL; overall gain "not equally certain"; slope improved in some indium studies, degraded in another (Wolf). A 1999 100 nm In/As SSR process: 1.04 (n) and 0.46 (p) mA/µm at 1.5 V, 3 nA/µm off
Electrostatics of the light layer
M09 §5.1		An undoped surface layer xs removes up to qNAxs/Cox from VT (all of it when xs ≪ Wm) while Wdm grows only as (Wm2 + xs2)1/2: lower threshold, slightly wider depletion. Extreme retrograde (ground-plane) limit: half the depletion term of a uniform channel of the same depletion width. Ideal profile low–high–low: the heavy layer kept out from under the source and drain (Taur & Ning)
The 50 nm node
M09 §5.4		10¹⁷ cm⁻³ surface layer over a 10¹⁹ cm⁻³ punch-through-control layer that begins 7 nm below the surface (notes p. 76; Lecture 17 gave the same levels). "It is not possible" by ion implantation (notes; Wolf: "probably will no longer be possible"); low-temperature selective epitaxy, perhaps of Si–Ge layers, "likely" (Wolf)
Atoms in the light layer
M09 §5.4		Below ten at 50 nm and below (Wolf): 1.75 under a 50 nm × 50 nm gate, σ = 1.32. The layer "may as well be undoped"; VT is then set by the step depth and the doping below. A retrograde channel also reduces random-dopant VT fluctuation (Taur & Ning)

Why a heavy ion straggles lessThe course's version
Which loss dominates
M09 §2.2		Nuclear stopping is 89 % of the stopping for In at 150 keV, 33 % for B at 20 keV; over the whole slowing-down, 92 % of indium's energy and 48 % of boron's goes to nuclear collisions, which carry the random deflections
Largest deflection per collision
M09 §2.2		arcsin(M2/M1) for an ion heavier than the target atom: In on Si 14.2°, Sb 13.3°. Boron, lighter than silicon, can be turned through any angle, even straight back
Depth at equal energy
M09 §2.2		The heavy ion stops shallower (more energy lost per unit path); so indium's channel implants run at 100–200 keV. Lecture 3's "penetrates deeper" has the direction reversed (M02 §5.3)

Channelling (Lecture 18; M09 §3)

ItemThe course's version
What it is
M09 §3		Along low-index directions the atom rows form open channels: axial along ⟨110⟩ and ⟨100⟩, planar between {111} planes. An ion entering one at a small angle is steered by gentle scattering off the rows, loses energy mainly to electrons, and stops far below the random-target range: a deep tail on the profile, which needs a second distribution to fit
Who suffers most
M09 §3		Low doses: the first ions of a high dose damage the surface and block the channels for the rest. Threshold and SSR implants are of order 10¹² cm⁻² (the notes' threshold implant 5.5×10¹² cm⁻², p. 74), the kind most exposed
1. Tilt and rotate
M09 §3		7–10° (Lecture 18; Plummer's problems use 7°) so the beam meets no low-index direction; cannot remove channelling completely (a scattered ion can enter a channel below the surface). Cost: shadowing beside every gate — 18.4 nm at 7° and 26.4 nm at 10° for a 150 nm gate; many processes implant at 0° instead
2. Screen oxide
M09 §3		A thin amorphous oxide scatters the beam into random directions before it reaches the crystal. Cost: dose stopped in the oxide is lost when it is stripped, a large fraction at sub-keV (notes p. 88)
3. Low energy
M09 §3		Channelling scales with Rp; minimal for low-energy, high-current implants, and for sub-keV boron unimportant next to TED (Wolf); hence 0° for shallow extensions. Cost: beam current, 1–2 mA against 10–25 mA above 10 keV (notes p. 85)
High dose, pre-amorphisation
M09 §3, M10 §6.1		A high dose blocks the channels with its own damage, but the dose is fixed by the doping wanted. Pre-amorphising the surface is "elaborate" and not expected at 0.1 µm and below (Wolf)

Transient enhanced diffusion (notes pp. 85–86; M09 §4)

ItemThe course's version
Diffusion partners
M09 §4		Boron and phosphorus move mainly with interstitials, antimony with vacancies, arsenic with both; diffusivity ∝ the concentration of the partner defect
"+1"
M09 §4.1		After early recombination, about one excess interstitial per implanted atom remains (Plummer: better "+n", n of order one). Evidence: implants at the same depth with very different energies give identical TED
Extended defects
M09 §4.1		Interstitials coalesce into rod-like {311} clusters (visible after 1 s at 600 °C) and more stable dislocation loops; the clusters are metastable and emit interstitials as they dissolve
Supersaturation
M09 §4.2		More than 10 000-fold in Plummer's simulation of a 40 keV, 10¹⁴ cm⁻² boron implant at 750 °C; boron's diffusivity rises by the same factor. Ends when the clusters are gone and the interstitials have reached the surface and been annihilated: hence "transient"
What increases it
M09 §4.3		Eq. (9.3): more dose N, a deeper Rp, and a lower dissolution temperature (negative activation energy, −1.4 eV). Remedies: low-energy implants (notes p. 86; below 1 keV each ion also does less damage) and rapid thermal anneals that pass the low temperatures in seconds
Reverse short-channel effect
M09 §4.4		Interstitials from the source/drain implants flow to the oxide interface under the gate and carry boron "uphill" to it; the pile-up is a larger fraction of a short channel, so VT rises slightly as L falls, before punch-through and charge sharing take over. It works against a retrograde profile. Indium channels suffer less; Wolf: lower TED susceptibility; Plummer: identical TED, the benefit is indium's segregation to the oxide

Pocket implants (notes pp. 89–90; M09 §6)

ItemThe course's version
Names
M09 §6.1		Large-angle tilt implanted punch-through stopper (LATIPS), also known as a pocket implant: "lateral tailoring of the channel doping profile" (p. 89). The same structure implanted at normal incidence is a halo (Wolf); Plummer calls a 30° implant a halo, Taur & Ning write "halo or pocket". All agree both are lateral, at the channel ends
What it does
M09 §6.1		"Puts additional dopants in the channel close to the S/D junctions for punchthrough control and leaves much of the channel region with lighter doping as needed for low VT"; its peak doping is somewhat higher than a uniform punch-through layer would need (p. 89)
When and masked by
M09 §6.2		After the source/drain extension implant, before the spacer: the gate alone masks it, self-aligned (pp. 89–90)
Dopant
M09 §6.2, §6.3		Well type: boron or indium for an n-channel device; an n-type dopant for a p-channel one (the notes name antimony). Indium and antimony increasingly used, for low diffusivity and small straggle (p. 90; the diffusivity half is disputed, see 4.4); the main production use of In and Sb implants around 2000 (Wolf)
Tilt and quad
M09 §6.2		20–45° from the normal, for "greater lateral penetration… than the adjacent SDE implant without thermal diffusion" (p. 90); Wolf: the pocket dopant should lie outside the final extension profile. Four equal segments with 90° rotations, so every gate edge of both orientations sees a beam tilted toward it
What it buys
M09 §6.3		Junction capacitance reduced by 65 % (notes; Wolf "up to 65 %"): the junction bottoms face the light well, not a uniform heavy layer. Steeper subthreshold slope: a lighter channel centre, smaller Cd. Wolf adds: the pocket compensates part of the extension and keeps it shallower
Threshold roll-off
M09 §6.1		Pockets keep their width as the gate shortens, so they make up more of a short channel: higher average doping and VT, offsetting roll-off. An optimised "superhalo" can in principle hold VT for channel lengths within 20–30 nm (Taur & Ning)
Tilt trend
M09 §6.2		Shorter gates need less undercut; tilt requirements fall below 0.25 µm and were expected to reach the 10° "low-tilt" threshold by 0.1 µm (Wolf). At 45° the shadow equals the gate height

The implants that shape the channel (M09 Table 2, §7)

ImplantVariesWhen, and masked byn-channel / p-channel speciesWhat it setsWhere
Threshold-adjustdepth (shallow peak)before the gateboron in both, for VT (Lecture 2)VTM03 §9
Blanket anti-punch-through; chained punch-through stopdepth (deeper peak)before the gate; none, or the well resistblanket: boron (n-channel); chained: boron / phosphorusdepletion spread below the channelM02 §5.1, M08 §8
Super-steep retrogradedepth, steepbefore the gate, in place of the threshold implant; resistindium / arsenic or antimony; In and As at 100–200 keV (Wolf)light surface, heavy layer below: VT and Wdm separatelyM09 §5
Haloalong the channelafter the gate; gate, normal incidencewell type (boron for n-channel, Lecture 3)doping at the channel endsM02 §5.4
Pocket (LATIPS)along the channel, under the gate edgeafter the extension, before the spacer; gate, 20–45° tilt, four 90° rotationswell type; indium / antimony increasinglypunch-through at the ends; less Cj, steeper swing, roll-off offsetM09 §6

The principle the table encodes: put each dopant only where it does its job — threshold and mobility in the top few nanometres, punch-through tens of nanometres down and at the channel ends, junction capacitance under the source and drain. The limits are the straggle, channelling, TED and, below 0.1 µm, the number of atoms (M09 §7).

The two-region source/drain (notes p. 77; M10 §1)

ItemThe course's version
Source/drain extension (SDE)
M10 §1.1		Shallow, junction depth Xj, from the channel out under the sidewall spacer; the part the channel sees. Role: reduce short-channel effects
Contact region
M10 §1.1		Deeper, junction depth Xjc, beyond the spacer; carries the silicide and the metal contact. Must be thick enough to hold the salicide, which consumes silicon, and deep enough that the junction under it does not leak
Depth rules (ITRS, via Wolf)
M10 §1.1		Xjc: 0.8 LL (1999) relaxed to 1.1 LL (2001). Xj: 0.4 LL relaxed to 0.55 LL (the notes use 0.55). At a 50 nm gate: extension 20–28 nm, contact 40–55 nm
The chain (p. 77)
M10 §1.1		Deeper Xjc → higher-energy contact implant → more lateral straggle → wider spacer → longer extension → more RSDE. Hence Xjc "should not be larger than that needed"; design goal: minimise both the series resistance and the silicide–silicon contact resistance
History
M10 opening box, §1.2; M02 §6.1–6.2		LDD (about 10¹⁷ cm⁻³) added near 2 µm (notes; Wolf: below 1.5 µm) against hot-carrier injection. At 0.18 µm the supply fell to about 2 V, the LDD was no longer needed, and the near region was redoped at 5–10×10¹⁴ cm⁻²: the SDE. "First region had a lighter doping than the second region" (notes)
Lecture 17's three advantages
M10 §1.2		Lower parasitic capacitance, lower field in the extension, suppressed hot-electron injection; the second and third describe the LDD it descends from
Extension doping
M10 §1.2		5.5×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ at the 50 nm node (notes p. 82; Lecture 18, "no longer an LDD"); Wolf: peak above 10¹⁹ cm⁻³ (Lecture 17 said "may be 10¹⁷", the LDD's level)

Series resistance and its yardstick (notes pp. 78–82; M10 §3)

ItemThe course's version
Current path
M10 §3.1		Enters the silicon perpendicular to the surface at the contact, turns, and travels parallel to the surface to the channel (Wolf)
The four resistances
M10 §3.1		Rco contact (metal or silicide to silicon); Rsh the body of the source/drain (with an extension, RSDE); Rsp spreading from the thin surface layer; Rac accumulation layer under the gate edge. In series; source and drain each contribute one set; each ∝ 1/W, so R·W is compared (pp. 78–79)
Rsat and the goal
M10 §3.2		Rsat = VDD/IDsat (p. 79); source and drain resistance may reduce IDsat by no more than 10 %. Wolf: an external resistance of about 10 % of Rsat costs about 8 % of IDsat. RsatW ∝ tox, so the budget shrinks every node
Source against drain
M10 §3.2		The drop across the source resistance is subtracted from the gate drive as well as from the drain voltage: "particularly troublesome" (Taur & Ning)
Rac
M10 §3.3		The channel ends where ND ≈ NA (p. 80); beyond it the gate accumulates the n-type silicon, about 10¹⁹ cm⁻³ induced against a 10²⁰–10²¹ cm⁻³ source/drain peak (Wolf). Short overlap: Rac "does not play a significant role"
Rsp
M10 §3.4		Current crowding from a layer Xc thick (the accumulation/inversion layer) into the extension depth Xj; "plays a significant role" (p. 81). β 0.37–0.9 (Wolf uses 0.58, Taur & Ning 0.75). Typical heavily doped case: 17–23 Ω·µm
The bracket
M10 §3.5		(Rsp + RSDE) = RSDE × 2.09 at 250 nm, × 1.48 at 50 nm (Wolf): spreading is 52 % of the sum, then 32 %, because Xj shrinks toward Xc
Extension geometry
M10 §3.5		ℓSDE ≈ LL/2; Xj ≈ 0.55 LL; ℓSDE/Xj ≈ 1.2 (notes p. 81; the two rules give 0.91, flagged in 4.4)
Sheet resistance required
M10 §3.5		About 650 Ω/□ at the 250 nm node; "maintain" it as the extension thins by raising the doping to 5.5×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ at 50 nm (p. 82), which is "difficult". Wolf's figure: 650 Ω/□ at 250 nm, about 900 Ω/□ at 50 nm; n-channel, p-channel about 20 % higher
The other lever
M10 §3.5		Shorten ℓSDE with a narrower spacer (p. 82); fails in a conventional structure, because the deep contact implant then straggles under the gate edge → the elevated source/drain
In practice; measurement
M10 §3.2, §2.1		Best total RsdW made: 100–200 Ω·µm (Taur & Ning). Sheet resistance by four-point probe: on a large thin film V/I = ρs ln 2/π ≈ 0.22 ρs

Contact resistance and the silicide (notes p. 83; M10 §4)

ItemThe course's version
ρc targets
M10 §4.1		10⁻⁷ Ω·cm² at 0.25 µm, 4×10⁻⁸ Ω·cm² at 50 nm (notes), for contacts of source and drain together at or below 10 % of Rsat (Wolf). For a barrier of about 0.5 eV they need silicon doped above 10²⁰ cm⁻³ at the interface
Why the doping matters
M10 §4.1		Tunnelling through the barrier: ρc ∝ exp[(4πφB/h)(m*εSi/Nd)1/2] (Taur & Ning): a modest fall in surface doping raises ρc by a large factor
Scaling
M10 §4.1		At fixed ρc, RcoW rises five-fold from 250 to 50 nm while RsatW falls. Measured TiSi₂ ρc: 10⁻⁶–10⁻⁷ Ω·cm²; long-contact estimate 50–200 Ω·µm per side (Taur & Ning)
Silicides named
M10 §4.2		TiSi₂ or CoSi₂ (notes p. 83), by sputtering Ti or Co and annealing. Silicide sheet resistance about 2–10 Ω/□, one to two orders below the silicon, so it shunts the contact region and makes the whole silicided surface the contact. A midgap work function (TiSi₂, CoSi₂, NiSi) keeps the barriers to n⁺ and p⁺ about equal (Taur & Ning)
Silicon consumed
M10 §4.3		"During silicide formation nearly 75% of Si is consumed. For this deep contact region is required" (p. 83); Lecture 18: "some 75% of the silicon", beside the 0.46 of oxidation. Read as a thickness ratio, Si consumed per nm of silicide: 0.75 for TiSi₂ (Wolf); 0.90 TiSi₂, 1.03 CoSi₂, 0.78 NiSi (Plummer, used for computation)
Two consequences (Wolf)
M10 §4.3		(1) The contact junction must be deep enough not to be consumed or leak: the wide spacer and long extension. (2) The consumed top layer was the most heavily doped, so the interface sits in lighter silicon and ρc rises; boron also segregates into the silicide
Anneals
M10 §4.2		Lecture 18: a single RTA at 900–1000 °C in argon. Production Ti and Co: two anneals with the strip between (C49 at 500–700 °C, then C54, about 800 °C for Ti, Plummer), which prevents bridging over the spacer (flagged in 4.4)

Silicides against aluminium (M10 Table 1, §4.2: Plummer Tables 11-5 and 11-2; Lecture 18)

FilmResistivity (µΩ·cm)Formation (°C)nm Si per nm metalnm silicide per nm metalLecture 18
TiSi₂ (C54, low-resistivity phase)13–16700–9002.272.51"10 to 12"
TiSi₂ (C49, first-anneal phase)60–70500–7002.272.51—
CoSi₂14–20600–8003.643.52about 20
NiSi14–20400–6001.832.34named
Al (thin film)2.7–3.0———"around 2.4"

The low-resistivity silicides are four to seven times as resistive as aluminium: TiSi₂ 4.3–5.9 times, CoSi₂ 4.7–7.4 times. They are used because a 30 nm film of 13–16 µΩ·cm has a sheet resistance of 4.3–5.3 Ω/□, against hundreds of ohms per square for the silicon beneath, and because they form in place with a clean interface. Lecture 18's 10–12 µΩ·cm for TiSi₂ and 2.4 µΩ·cm for aluminium sit below the table's ranges; M10 computes with the table (M10 §4.2).

The elevated source/drain (notes pp. 83–84; M10 §5)

ItemThe course's version
Structure
M10 §5		A heavily doped contact layer grown on the source and drain by selective epitaxy (single-crystal Si, or perhaps Si–Ge), only on exposed silicon, not on the spacer or isolation oxide; the silicide is formed in the grown layer
Three advantages (notes)
M10 §5		(1) A much narrower spacer: no deep contact implant, so shorter ℓSDE and lower RSDE. (2) A contact layer thick enough for the silicide without junction leakage: the consumed silicon comes from the grown layer. (3) Heavy in-situ doping lowers ρc
Two growths
M10 §5		n⁺ and p⁺ layers need two separate selective epitaxy processes (p. 84); Lecture 18 tied the word "selective" to growing the two types separately
Temperature
M10 §5		Below 850 °C, "to prevent the SDE implant from diffusing too far under the gate edge" (notes; Wolf: "probably below 850°C")

Making an ultra-shallow junction (notes pp. 84–88; M10 §6)

ItemThe course's version
Energies
M10 §6.1		Single-digit keV for contacts, sub-keV for extensions at 0.13 and 0.1 µm (Wolf); 0.2–0.3 keV for the extension (Lecture 18 and the class of 23 October)
Three reasons lower energy is shallower (pp. 84–85)
M10 §6.1		(1) Smaller Rp, peak nearer the surface. (2) Straggle and channelling tail scale with Rp ("especially important for B implants", Wolf). (3) TED reduced: interstitials start nearer the surface (ΔXj ∝ Rp1/2)
Beam current
M10 §6.2		Above 10 keV: 10–25 mA, 230 wafers per hour at 4×10¹⁵ cm⁻². Sub-keV: 1–2 mA (Wolf: the mid-1990s maximum). "Recently" 4 mA at 0.2 keV (Wolf: by 2000), with which extensions meeting 0.1 µm requirements were shown. Cause (Wolf): Child's law, Imax ∝ V3/2
Decel
M10 §6.2		Extract at a higher voltage, decelerate to the final energy. Cost: energy contamination — ions neutralised before or in the decelerating field are not counted and arrive too energetic, putting dopant deeper (Wolf)
Batch against single-wafer
M10 §6.2, §7.3		Batch: wafers on a spinning disk through a fixed beam. Single-wafer, the notes' advantages: angle controlled more accurately; no metal sputtered from the disk, batch machines' largest contamination source. Wolf: a fixed beam tunes faster (3–4 batch against 5–9 single-wafer machines for 5000 wafers a week at 0.18 µm); the batch disk's cone varies the angle by up to 1.5° (Wolf §5.3.5, beyond the notes)
Why RTA (p. 86)
M10 §6.3		Ideally "zero" time at temperature; activation energy for activation exceeds that for diffusion (boron: 5.4 eV against 3.4 eV, Wolf), so short and hot favours activation. And TED is largest at low temperature, which a furnace ramp crosses slowly (notes: "Less time is spent during annealing")
Spike anneal
M10 §6.3		Ramp up 100–200 °C/s, down 50–80 °C/s, zero time at the peak (notes; the class of 23 October the same). Wolf: about 200 °C/s achievable, little gain beyond about 100 °C/s; about 90 % of activation occurs within roughly 50 °C of the peak, a better figure of merit than the ramp rate
RTA tools (p. 86)
M10 §6.3		Five: linear lamps heating both sides; axisymmetric lamps heating one side; an arc lamp; a hot plate; a hot-wall RTP chamber (the class of 23 October described four)
More activation (p. 87)
M10 §6.3		Laser annealing; strain-compensation implants (germanium offsets the strain of the smaller boron, raising its solubility, Wolf); RTP lamps that respond quickly, for a faster ramp-down
Where it stood (Wolf, 2001)
M10 §6.3		0.5 keV boron with a spike anneal: about 35 nm and 350 Ω/□, apparently the practical limit. The 50 nm node needs 20–25 nm extensions; whether implantation and RTA could do it was unclear
Dose lost (pp. 87–88)
M10 §6.4		The notes name three mechanisms; Wolf's numbers for boron: (1) Self-sputtering, about 1 Si per 4 B at 0.5 keV: a nominal 10¹⁵ cm⁻² retained 10 % low at 0.5 keV, 20 % at 0.2 keV, fully at 2 keV. (2) Out-diffusion, 1000 °C for 15 s: 10 % at 0.5 keV, 15 % at 0.2 keV. (3) The stripped protective oxide: 25 % at 0.5 keV, over 40 % at 0.2 keV (printed for 15 nm). Together up to about 40 % at 0.2 keV; correct for retained dose before comparing junctions (p. 87)
BF₂⁺
M10 §6.4		2.2 keV BF₂⁺ carries its boron at 0.49 keV, the 0.5 keV B⁺ implant made with the molecule; retained dose about 20 % low against 10 % for B⁺, since two fluorine atoms also sputter

Where the extension ends under the gate (notes pp. 88–89; M10 §7)

ItemThe course's version
Too much overlap
M10 §7.1		Shorter electrical channel, more short-channel effect: excessive leakage (off-current above its design maximum, of order 1 nA/µm, Wolf); larger gate–source and gate–drain overlap capacitance, slower switching
Too little overlap
M10 §7.1		Channel end and extension "not electrically well coupled", "causing excessive resistance": IDsat falls. Below two to three oxide thicknesses the extension may end outside the gate, exposed to hot-carrier injection (Taur & Ning)
The study (Wolf)
M10 §7.2		0.25 µm n-channel, arsenic extensions 50 nm deep, lateral diffusion 0.6–0.7 Xj; IDsat fell rapidly below 20 nm overlap: a minimum of about 20 nm recommended. Set with an offset spacer, varied from 0 to 50 nm
Lateral abruptness (beyond the notes)
M10 §7.3		Wolf §5.3.5: at least one decade within about 3 % of the gate length, for Rsp under 5 % of Rsat — about 3 nm per decade at 100 nm, the slope of as-implanted 0.2 keV boron. A 0° parallel-beam implant gives the most abrupt edge

Channel and source/drain sequence, n-channel (notes pp. 74–90; M09 §7, M10 §1 and §7.2)

  1. Well and punch-through implants in place (§3); gate not yet formed.
  2. SSR implant in place of the threshold implant: indium (arsenic or antimony for the p-channel device), 100–200 keV, resist-masked — sets the light surface and the heavy layer below.
  3. Gate oxide and gate patterned.
  4. Offset spacer, where used: a thin spacer on the gate after the polysilicon etch, its width chosen to set the final overlap (Wolf).
  5. Extension implant, sub-keV, the gate as the mask, 0° for the most abrupt edge — the shallow Xj.
  6. Pocket implant: well-type dopant, 20–45° tilt, four 90° segments, the gate as the mask — punch-through doping at the channel ends.
  7. Sidewall spacer: conformal oxide, anisotropic etch with no mask (M02 §6.1) — masks the contact implant from the gate edge.
  8. Contact implant, the spacer as the mask — the deep Xjc that will hold the silicide.
  9. Source/drain anneal by RTA, a spike — activation with the least diffusion and TED.
  10. Salicide on the contact region (flow below).

Pocket implant (notes pp. 89–90; M09 §6.2)

  1. After the extension implant, before the spacer — so the gate alone masks it and it is self-aligned.
  2. Dopant of the well type: boron or indium for n-channel, antimony for p-channel — the opposite type to the extension it sits beyond.
  3. Tilt 20–45° from the normal — lateral penetration beyond the adjacent extension without thermal diffusion.
  4. Dose split into four equal segments, wafer rotated 90° between them — every gate edge, in both orientations, sees a beam tilted toward it.

Salicide (notes p. 83; M10 §4.2, M02 §3.3; production sequence, Plummer)

  1. Sputter titanium or cobalt over the whole wafer.
  2. First anneal: the metal reacts only where it touches silicon — high-resistivity phase (C49 TiSi₂, 500–700 °C).
  3. Strip the unreacted metal from the spacers and oxide — prevents bridging from gate to source and drain.
  4. Second anneal: convert to the low-resistivity phase (C54, about 800 °C for titanium, Plummer; 13–16 µΩ·cm).

As taught (Lectures 3 and 18): sputter, one RTA at 900–1000 °C in argon, strip — a single anneal in place of steps 2 and 4. The two-anneal sequence above is the production process (Plummer).

Elevated source/drain (notes pp. 83–84; M10 §5)

  1. Extension implanted and in its final position — the epitaxy must not move it.
  2. Narrow spacer — possible because no deep contact implant follows.
  3. Selective epitaxy of a heavily doped layer of one type (single-crystal Si, or perhaps Si–Ge), below 850 °C — grows only on exposed silicon, not on the spacer or isolation oxide.
  4. A second selective epitaxy for the other type (n⁺ and p⁺ need separate growths), also below 850 °C.
  5. Silicide formed in the grown layer — the silicon it consumes lies above the junction.

Ultra-shallow extension (notes pp. 84–88; M10 §6)

  1. Sub-keV implant (0.2–0.3 keV in Lecture 18 and the class of 23 October), 0° for the most abrupt edge — small Rp, straggle and channelling, less TED.
  2. Through a thin protective oxide against metal contamination, or without it at ultra-low energy — Wolf: about 25 % of a 0.5 keV boron dose is lost in it, over 40 % at 0.2 keV (thickness printed as 15 nm, probably much thinner; see 4.4).
  3. Strip the oxide.
  4. Spike RTA: 100–200 °C/s up, zero time at the peak, 50–80 °C/s down — activation (5.4 eV) outruns diffusion (3.4 eV), and the low temperatures where TED is largest pass in seconds.
  5. Correct for the retained dose before comparing junctions — sputtering, out-diffusion and the oxide remove up to 40 %.

Setting the overlap (Wolf; M10 §7.2)

  1. Fix the extension and contact implants and anneal.
  2. Form an offset spacer after the polysilicon etch, before the extension implant; vary its width from 0 to about 50 nm.
  3. Measure off-current and IDsat for each width; keep the best combination — about 20 nm minimum overlap at 0.25 µm.

4.3 Equations and board problems

4.5 Self-check

Choose one. For super-steep retrograde channels from 0.18 to 0.1 µm, the notes replace boron and phosphorus with — (a) indium for boron; arsenic or antimony for phosphorus (b) BF₂⁺ for boron; arsenic for phosphorus (c) antimony for boron; indium for phosphorus (d) germanium for boron; arsenic for phosphorus

(a). Indium is p-type and replaces boron in the n-channel device; arsenic or antimony, n-type, replace phosphorus in the p-channel device. They are heavier: indium straggles about half as much as boron at the same depth (18.9 nm against 37.9 nm near 76 nm), antimony about two-thirds as much as phosphorus, and they diffuse less (M09 §5.2).

Choose one. In a conventional (not elevated) source/drain, the contact junction Xjc is made deeper and the spacer widened to keep the contact implant's lateral straggle off the gate edge. The extension resistance RSDE — (a) increases (b) decreases (c) does not change (d) none of the above

(a). The extension runs under the whole spacer, so ℓSDE grows with the spacer width, and RSDE = ρsℓSDE/W grows with it: the notes' chain on p. 77 (M10 §1.1).

Choose one. The reverse short-channel effect is — (a) a slight rise of VT as the gate shortens, from channel boron carried to the oxide interface by interstitials released from the source/drain implant damage (b) the fall of VT as the source and drain take over depletion charge in short channels (c) punch-through between source and drain at the shortest lengths (d) the rise of subthreshold swing in short channels

(a). During the source/drain anneal the interstitials flow to the nearest sink, the interface under the gate, and boron moves "uphill" with them; the pile-up is a larger fraction of a short channel. (b) is roll-off, which takes over at the shortest lengths. The effect undoes a retrograde profile (M09 §4.4).

Choose one. The same implant's extended defects dissolve at 850 °C in one process and at 1050 °C in another. By eq. (9.3), the TED junction deepening ΔXj at 850 °C relative to 1050 °C is — (a) 0.335 (b) 1.00 (c) 2.98 (d) 8.90

(c). The ratio of ΔXj2 is exp[(1.4/k)(1/1123.15 − 1/1323.15)] = 8.90, (d), and ΔXj is its square root, 2.98. The lower temperature gives more: the negative activation energy. At fixed temperature and dose, a 2 keV boron implant against 10 keV gives (9.7/40.0)1/2 = 0.49 (M09 §4.3).

Choose one. A 7 nm surface layer at 10¹⁷ cm⁻³ under a 70 nm × 70 nm gate holds, on average — (a) 0.343 (b) 3.43 (c) 34.3 (d) 343 dopant atoms

(b). 10¹⁷ cm⁻³ × (70×10⁻⁷ cm)² × 7×10⁻⁷ cm = 3.43, with a Poisson spread σ = 3.431/2 = 1.85, 54 % of the mean — consistent with Wolf's "below ten" at these sizes, which is why the layer "may as well be undoped" (M09 §5.4).

Choose one. An extension of ρ = 2×10⁻³ Ω·cm has Xj/Xc = 30. With Wolf's β = 0.58, RspW is — (a) 3.64 Ω·µm (b) 36.4 Ω·µm (c) 364 Ω·µm (d) −50.2 Ω·µm

(b). (2ρ/π) ln(0.58 × 30) = 1.273×10⁻³ Ω·cm × ln 17.4, converted with 1 Ω·cm = 10⁴ Ω·µm: 36.4 Ω·µm. Over β = 0.37 to 0.9 it runs 30.6 to 42.0 Ω·µm, a factor of 1.37 for a factor of 2.43 in β. (d) is what the inverted ratio Xc/Xj gives — a negative resistance, the sign of the error (M10 §3.4).

Choose one. An arsenic extension is 60 nm deep, its lateral diffusion under the gate edge is 0.6–0.7 Xj, and the overlap must stay at least 20 nm. The widest usable offset spacer is — (a) 10–15 nm (b) 16–22 nm (c) 36–42 nm (d) 40 nm

(b). Lateral diffusion 0.6–0.7 × 60 = 36–42 nm, the overlap with no offset spacer (c); minus the 20 nm minimum leaves 16–22 nm. (a) is the 50 nm study's answer; (d) subtracts 20 nm from the depth, not from the lateral diffusion (M10 §7.2).

Choose one. The notes' targets for the specific contact resistivity at the 0.25 µm and 50 nm nodes are — (a) 10⁻⁷ and 4×10⁻⁸ Ω·cm² (b) 10⁻⁶ and 10⁻⁷ Ω·cm² (c) 8.7×10⁻⁷ and 4×10⁻⁸ Ω·cm² (d) 4×10⁻⁷ and 10⁻⁸ Ω·cm²

(a), notes p. 83, reached with silicon doped above 10²⁰ cm⁻³ at the interface for a 0.5 eV barrier. (b) is the measured TiSi₂ range Taur & Ning quote; 8.7×10⁻⁷ Ω·cm² is where Wolf's own equation reaches the 10 % share at 0.25 µm, which M10 flags; the notes' 10⁻⁷ is the course's value (M10 §4.1).

What is channelling, and how is it prevented? Name the cost of each remedy.

A silicon wafer is a single crystal. Seen along low-index directions its atoms line up in rows with open space between them: axial channels along ⟨110⟩ and ⟨100⟩, planar channels between {111} planes. An implanted ion that enters one at a small angle to its axis is steered by many gentle collisions with the atoms lining the walls, loses energy mainly to the electrons, and travels far beyond the projected range of a random target before electronic drag or a hard collision stops it. The profile gains a deep tail. It is worst for low doses, because the first ions of a high dose damage the surface and block the channels; threshold and SSR implants, about 10¹² cm⁻², are the most exposed, and the tail spoils exactly the profiles meant to be steep. Remedies: (1) tilt the wafer 7–10° (Lecture 18) and rotate it, so the beam meets no open direction — it cannot remove channelling entirely, and it shadows a strip beside every gate (18.4 nm at 7° for a 150 nm gate); (2) implant through a thin amorphous screen oxide that randomises the ions' directions — part of the dose stops in the oxide and is stripped with it; (3) use a low energy, since channelling scales with Rp and for sub-keV boron matters less than TED — the beam current falls to 1–2 mA. A high dose also blocks the channels, but the dose is fixed by the doping wanted.

Full treatment: M09 §3.

The super-steep retrograde profile: what it was for between 0.18 and 0.1 µm, how it was made, and why the 50 nm step cannot be implanted.

At 0.13 µm punch-through control needs a mean channel doping above 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ — eq. (9.1) with L > 2W gives 6.93×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ at 1.2 V — but the surface must stay below 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³, because ionised dopants in the inversion layer scatter the carriers and the depletion charge raises the transverse field and the threshold voltage. A uniform profile cannot do both. The fix is a super-steep retrograde profile, light at the surface and heavy below: an undoped layer xs removes up to qNAxs/Cox from VT while Wdm grows only as (Wm2 + xs2)1/2. From 0.18 to 0.1 µm it was implanted in place of the threshold implant, with indium for boron and arsenic or antimony for phosphorus, In and As at 100–200 keV: about 5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³ at the oxide and 10¹⁸ cm⁻³ at 80 nm. Indium straggles half as much as boron (18.9 against 37.9 nm), so the flank is steeper, and they diffuse less. At 50 nm the notes need 10¹⁷ cm⁻³ for only 7 nm over 10¹⁹ cm⁻³: a two-decade step inside 7 nm needs ΔRp ≤ 2.3 nm, boron at 7 nm depth still straggles 5.7 nm, indium cannot exceed about 10¹⁸ cm⁻³, and the anneal only widens the profile; the light layer holds about two atoms under a 50 nm gate. By implantation it is "not possible" (notes; Wolf "probably"); low-temperature selective epitaxy, perhaps of Si–Ge, is the likely route, with the surface layer effectively undoped.

Full treatment: M09 §1.1, §5.

How do the super-steep retrograde layer and the pocket implant differ, and how is a pocket implanted, step by step?

Both supply punch-through doping, in different directions. The SSR layer varies in depth: a blanket layer under the whole channel, implanted before the gate exists, in place of the threshold implant, light at the surface and heavy below (Lecture 17 called it "halo"; the notes, Wolf, Plummer and Taur & Ning reserve halo and pocket for the lateral structure). The pocket varies along the channel: the large-angle tilt implanted punch-through stopper (LATIPS) adds dopant only near the source and drain, where punch-through starts, and leaves the channel centre light for a low VT. Steps: (1) after the extension implant and before the spacer, so the gate alone masks it and it is self-aligned; (2) a dopant of the well type — boron or indium for n-channel, antimony for p-channel; (3) a tilt of 20–45°, so it reaches under the gate beyond the extension tip without thermal diffusion (boron at 20 keV and 30°: 38.5 nm under the gate against an extension tip at 30–35 nm); (4) the dose split into four segments with 90° rotations, so gate edges of both orientations are reached. What it buys: junction capacitance down by 65 % (notes p. 90; Wolf "up to"), because the junction bottoms face the light well; a steeper subthreshold slope (p. 90); and threshold roll-off offset (Taur & Ning), because the pockets fill more of a short channel. A normal-incidence version is a halo (Wolf), reaching under the gate only by lateral straggle.

Full treatment: M09 §6, with §1.1 for the naming.

The source/drain extension as the gate shrinks: its resistance problem, and the structure that fixes it.

Every ampere passes four resistances in series on each side: Rco at the silicide–silicon contact, RSDE of the extension, Rsp where the current spreads from the thin surface layer into the extension depth, and Rac of the accumulation layer under the gate edge, which the notes call insignificant because the overlap is short. They are measured against Rsat = VDD/IDsat, with RsatW ≈ tox/(0.376vsatεox) = 0.347 Ω·cm at 4.5 nm, and may cut IDsat by no more than 10 %. As tox falls the allowance falls, while the extension gets shallower (0.55 LL), so its sheet resistance, 650 Ω/□ at 250 nm, can be held only by doping it to 5.5×10¹⁹ cm⁻³ at 50 nm. The other lever, a shorter extension under a narrower spacer, fails in the conventional structure: the contact junction must be deep to hold the silicide (TiSi₂ consumes 0.75–0.9 nm of silicon per nm formed), its high-energy implant straggles sideways, and a narrow spacer lets it reach the gate edge. The elevated source/drain breaks the chain: a heavily doped layer grown by selective epitaxy below 850 °C (n⁺ and p⁺ in two growths) holds the silicide above the original surface, so no deep contact implant is needed, the spacer can be narrow, ℓSDE and RSDE fall, and the in-situ doping lowers ρc.

Full treatment: M10 §3, §5.

Body doping for a 0.10 µm channel. L = 0.10 µm, n⁺ source and drain at 10²⁰ cm⁻³, uniform p-type body, drain reverse bias 1.0 V (illustrative). Find the smallest uniform body doping that keeps L > 2W, check W, and compare with the 0.13 µm case and the notes' 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³.

First guess for the built-in potential. At 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³, Vbi = 0.0259 ln(5×10¹⁷ × 10²⁰/(1.5×10¹⁰)²) = 1.035 V.

First pass. NA = 8 × 1.044×10⁻¹² F/cm × (1.035 + 1.0) V / (1.6×10⁻¹⁹ C × (1.0×10⁻⁵ cm)²) = 1.06×10¹⁸ cm⁻³.

Second pass. At 1.06×10¹⁸ cm⁻³, Vbi = 1.054 V, and eq. (9.1) returns 1.07×10¹⁸ cm⁻³; a third pass changes nothing at three figures.

Check of W. W = (2εSi(Vbi + VR)/(qNA))1/2 = 50.0 nm, half of 100 nm.

NA ≥ 1.07×10¹⁸ cm⁻³ (Vbi = 1.054 V); 5.41×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ at 0 V

Check. 1.55 times the 0.13 µm requirement (6.93×10¹⁷ at 1.2 V), as NA ∝ 1/L2 roughly demands with a lower bias; at the notes' 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ and 1.0 V, 2W = 146 nm, well beyond 100 nm. A uniform doping this heavy would sit at the surface, which is why the profile has to be retrograde.

Method: M09 §1.1; formula sheet (9.1).

Flank of a shallow channel implant. Boron at 10 keV (Rp 40.0 nm, ΔRp 23.2 nm) against indium at 50 keV (32.9 nm, 9.2 nm), SRIM values from M09 Table 1. For each, find the distance from the peak over which the concentration falls one and two decades, whether the two-decade point is reached on the surface side, and the fraction of the peak left at the surface.

Factors. Eq. (9.2): one decade, (2 ln 10)1/2 = 2.15; two decades, (2 ln 100)1/2 = 3.03.

Boron. 2.15 × 23.2 = 49.8 nm; 3.03 × 23.2 = 70.4 nm. The peak is only 40.0 nm deep, so the concentration never falls two decades on the surface side.

Indium. 2.15 × 9.2 = 19.7 nm; 3.03 × 9.2 = 27.9 nm, so two decades below the peak at 32.9 − 27.9 = 5.0 nm below the surface.

At the surface. Boron: exp(−40.0²/(2 × 23.2²)) = 0.226 of the peak. Indium: exp(−32.9²/(2 × 9.2²)) = 1.67×10⁻³.

Two decades in 70.4 nm (B) against 27.9 nm (In); B leaves 0.226 of its peak at the surface, In 1.67×10⁻³

Check. The straggles differ by 2.52 and so do the flank widths; the surface fractions differ by far more, because the exponent goes as 1/ΔRp2 — the point of M09 §2.2's factor of 390. These are as-implanted Gaussians: channelling and TED only widen them.

Method: M09 §2.1; formula sheet (9.2).

A pocket at 25°. n-channel boron pocket at 20 keV (Rp 77.1 nm), tilted 25° toward the gate edge; gates 120 nm tall (illustrative); the 50 nm arsenic extension of M09 §6.2, reaching 30–35 nm under the gate. Find the mean depth and lateral reach of an ion entering at the gate edge, the shadow a neighbouring gate casts, and the shadow at the 7° channelling tilt.

Depth. 77.1 nm × cos 25° = 69.9 nm.

Lateral reach. 77.1 nm × sin 25° = 32.6 nm under the gate edge.

Shadow. 120 nm × tan 25° = 56.0 nm; at 7°, 120 × tan 7° = 14.7 nm.

69.9 nm deep, 32.6 nm under the gate; shadow 56.0 nm (14.7 nm at 7°)

Check. (69.9² + 32.6²)1/2 = 77.1 nm, the range. At 25° the innermost mean stopping point lies 2.6 nm beyond a 30 nm extension tip but 2.4 nm short of a 35 nm one — marginal against Wolf's aim that the pocket lie outside the extension; 30° gave 38.5 nm, clear of both. The straight-path estimate ignores straggle.

Method: M09 §6.2 and §3.

Resistors by squares. (a) A 350 Ω/□ layer patterned with L/W = 100/5 (the variant offered in Lecture 17). (b) A 120 Ω/□ layer patterned as a strip 30 µm long and 1.5 µm wide (illustrative). (c) The length of a 1.5 µm wide strip of the 120 Ω/□ layer that gives 5 kΩ.

(a) Squares, then ohms. 100/5 = 20 squares; 20 × 350 Ω = 7000 Ω.

(b) The strip. 30 µm/1.5 µm = 20 squares; 20 × 120 Ω = 2400 Ω.

(c) Length for 5 kΩ. 5000 Ω/120 Ω = 41.7 squares; × 1.5 µm = 62.5 µm.

7.00 kΩ; 2.40 kΩ; 62.5 µm

Check. Ω/□ × □ = Ω. A 3 µm × 0.15 µm strip of the 120 Ω/□ layer is also 20 squares and also 2400 Ω: resistance counts squares, not area. No thickness enters; it is inside ρs.

Method: M10 §2.1; formula sheet (10.1).

The resistance budget at 130 nm. tox = 3.0 nm, VDD = 1.5 V, LL = 130 nm (illustrative), vsat = 10⁷ cm/s, εox = 3.9 × 8.854×10⁻¹⁴ F/cm; contacts at the notes' 50 nm target, ρc = 4×10⁻⁸ Ω·cm². Find RsatW, the current per micrometre, the 10 % budget, and RcoW with its share.

Denominator. 0.376 × 10⁷ cm/s × 3.453×10⁻¹³ F/cm = 1.298×10⁻⁶ F/s (A/V).

Channel resistance. RsatW = 3.0×10⁻⁷ cm / 1.298×10⁻⁶ = 0.2311 Ω·cm = 2311 Ω·µm.

Current. With VT = 0.2 × 1.5 = 0.30 V and Cox = 3.453×10⁻¹³/3.0×10⁻⁷ = 1.151×10⁻⁶ F/cm², IDsat/W = 0.47 × 10⁷ × 1.151×10⁻⁶ × (1.5 − 0.30) = 6.49 A/cm = 649 µA/µm; 1.5 V / 6.49 A/cm = 0.2311 Ω·cm, the same.

Budget. 10 % of 2311 Ω·µm = 231 Ω·µm for source and drain together, 116 Ω·µm per side.

Contact. RcoW ≥ 4×10⁻⁸ Ω·cm² / (2 × 1.3×10⁻⁵ cm) = 1.54×10⁻³ Ω·cm = 15.4 Ω·µm; both contacts take 2 × 15.4/2311 = 1.33 % of Rsat.

RsatW = 2311 Ω·µm; 649 µA/µm; budget 231 Ω·µm (116 per side); RcoW ≥ 15.4 Ω·µm, 1.33 %

Check. The budget is 0.667 of the 0.25 µm one, the ratio of the oxides (3.0/4.5); the contacts would use the full 10 % only at ρc = 3.00×10⁻⁷ Ω·cm². The bound is optimistic: at 0.25 µm Taur & Ning's long-contact estimate is 2.5 to 10 times it.

Method: M10 §3.2, §4.1; formula sheet (10.3), (10.7).

Silicon consumed by 20 nm of silicide. Using Plummer's ratios (M10 Table 1), find the metal to deposit, the silicon consumed, and where the silicide's top lies relative to the original surface, for 20 nm of TiSi₂, CoSi₂ and NiSi; then TiSi₂ with the notes' 0.75, and the TiSi₂ consumption as a fraction of a contact junction at LL = 50 nm (0.8–1.1 LL).

Metal. Silicide per nm of metal 2.51, 3.52, 2.34: Ti 20/2.51 = 7.97 nm; Co 20/3.52 = 5.68 nm; Ni 20/2.34 = 8.55 nm.

Silicon consumed. TiSi₂ 2.27 × 7.97 = 18.1 nm; CoSi₂ 3.64 × 5.68 = 20.7 nm; NiSi 1.83 × 8.55 = 15.6 nm.

Position of the top. TiSi₂ 20 − 18.1 = 1.9 nm above the original surface; CoSi₂ 0.7 nm below it; NiSi 4.4 nm above.

The notes' ratio. 0.75 × 20 = 15.0 nm for TiSi₂.

Against the contact junction. Xjc = 40–55 nm at LL = 50 nm: 18.1 nm is 33–45 % of it.

Silicon consumed: TiSi₂ 18.1 nm (15.0 nm with the notes' 0.75), CoSi₂ 20.7 nm, NiSi 15.6 nm; TiSi₂ uses 33–45 % of a 50 nm node's contact junction

Check. The per-nm ratios 0.904, 1.03 and 0.782 keep the order CoSi₂ ≥ TiSi₂ ≥ NiSi of M10's 30 nm example, and 20/30 of its 27.1 nm is 18.1 nm. A silicide that cannot be thinned with the junction is what drives the elevated source/drain.

Method: M10 §4.3.

Spike anneal at a higher peak. Boron activation 5.4 eV, diffusion 3.4 eV (Wolf); k = 8.617×10⁻⁵ eV/K. By what factor does each rate rise from 950 °C to 1100 °C (illustrative temperatures), how much less Dt accompanies the same activation at 1100 °C, and how much shorter is the diffusion length?

Temperatures. 1223.15 K and 1373.15 K; 1/T1 − 1/T2 = 8.931×10⁻⁵ K⁻¹.

Activation. (5.4/8.617×10⁻⁵) × 8.931×10⁻⁵ = 5.597; e5.597 = 270.

Diffusion. (3.4/8.617×10⁻⁵) × 8.931×10⁻⁵ = 3.524; e3.524 = 33.9.

Per unit of activation. 270/33.9 = 7.95 times less Dt; the diffusion length goes as (Dt)1/2, so 7.951/2 = 2.82 times shorter.

Activation × 270, diffusion × 33.9; 7.95 times less Dt, diffusion length 2.82 times shorter

Check. Smaller than M10's 9.4 for 900 → 1050 °C, because 1/T changes less over the same 150 °C at higher temperature. Only the 2.0 eV difference matters; with equal energies temperature would buy nothing. TED, with its negative activation energy, adds a second reason to pass the low temperatures fast.

Method: M10 §6.3.

Beam current, beam time and the molecular ion. (a) By Child's law, the current extractable at 1 kV relative to 10 kV. (b) Beam-on time for a 2×10¹⁵ cm⁻² extension dose on a 300 mm wafer, singly charged ions, at 3 mA and at 20 mA (illustrative). (c) The BF₂⁺ energy that gives the boron 0.3 keV, and the boron energy in a 1.0 keV BF₂⁺ ion (B = 11, F = 19).

(a) Child's law. (1/10)3/2 = 0.0316: about 3 % of the current.

(b) Charge. Area π × (15 cm)² = 706.9 cm²; ions 2×10¹⁵ × 706.9 = 1.41×10¹⁸; charge × 1.602×10⁻¹⁹ C = 0.2265 C. Time 0.2265/0.003 = 75.5 s; 0.2265/0.020 = 11.3 s.

(c) Mass share. BF₂ = 11 + 2 × 19 = 49. For 0.3 keV boron, 0.3 × 49/11 = 1.336 keV of BF₂⁺; in a 1.0 keV BF₂⁺ ion the boron carries 11/49 = 0.224 keV.

Current × 0.0316; 75.5 s at 3 mA against 11.3 s at 20 mA; 1.34 keV BF₂⁺; 0.224 keV boron

Check. Time scales as 1/I: 20/3 = 6.7 = 75.5/11.3. M10's 200 mm, 10¹⁵ cm⁻² case gives 5.03×10⁻² C; this one has 2.25 times the area and twice the dose, 4.5 times the charge. Wolf's 2.2 keV BF₂⁺ (0.49 keV boron) lost about 20 % of its dose against 10 % for B⁺ at equal boron energy, since two fluorine atoms also sputter.

Method: M10 §6.2, §6.4; formula sheet (10.8).

5 · Chapters 6–7: lithography

Chapters 6 and 7 of the notes (pp. 91–105) are the step that gives every earlier structure its outline: a photoresist exposed through a mask and developed, repeated more than 20 times per wafer in a 2002 flow. The recorded class of 23 October supplied the vocabulary and the shadow-printing limit the notes omit, Lecture 19 worked the resolution and depth of focus of projection printing and the anti-reflective coatings, and Lecture 20 the phase-shift mask and the non-optical methods, X-ray, extreme ultraviolet, electron and ion beams, largely from outside the notes. M11 teaches the resist side and the two optical limits; M12 the sources, the lens materials, the tricks that lower k1 and the methods beyond light. The chapter's chain runs: the feature shrinks below the wavelength, a larger NA buys resolution at the cost of depth of focus as 1/NA², a shorter wavelength makes the mercury-line resists opaque and the mercury lamp too weak, and chemical amplification, excimer lasers, anti-reflective coatings, phase-shift masks and immersion each answer one link of it. The shrinking depth of focus makes wafer planarity critical, which is where Chapter 8's chemical mechanical polishing enters (M13).

5.1 Facts and lists

Masks, tone and printing tools (not in the notes: the recorded class of 23 October; Plummer)

ItemThe course's version
Mask
M11 §1.1		Glass or fused-silica plate carrying one layer's pattern as an opaque film, normally chromium. Dark-field: mostly opaque, transparent openings; clear-field (bright-field): mostly transparent, opaque features. A reticle carries the pattern enlarged, to be reduced optically
Resist tone
M11 §1.1		Positive: exposed areas become more soluble and dissolve, pattern = the mask. Negative: exposed areas become less soluble and remain, pattern = the inverse. Industry relies mainly on positive resists (finer features, Plummer); positive tone became the preferred DUV resist; negative prints isolated lines better through a dark-field mask (Wolf p. 229)
Contact printing
M11 §2.1, §9		Mask pressed onto the resist, 1:1. Good resolution, but contact damages mask and resist and transfers particles; abandoned for defect density, not resolution (Plummer §5.2.2). Class: photographic-emulsion masks scratch quickly, chromium masks last longer; soft contact leaves a small gap for mask life at some cost in resolution
Proximity printing
M11 §2.1		Mask held a gap s above the resist, 1:1; no damage, but diffraction across the gap blurs the image (eq. 11.1)
Projection printing
M11 §2.2		A lens images the mask; early aligners 1:1 across the wafer. Stepper: one field at a time, step and repeat, reticle 4× or 5× (Plummer). Scanner (step-and-scan): slit illuminated, reticle and wafer moved past it, reticle 4–5× faster, larger field. Well above the limit a reticle error prints at a quarter or a fifth; near the limit less reduced or magnified (Wolf pp. 293–294)
Alignment
M11 §1.2		To marks (crosses or squares) printed at the first level. Class example: a contact shifted off its n⁺ region covers less of it, the contact area shrinks and the contact resistance rises (M10 §4.1)

Resolution and depth of focus (notes pp. 97–98)

ItemThe course's version
Numerical aperture
M11 §3.1		NA = sin θ, θ the half-angle of the converging cone (the notes' drawing marks the whole cone; Lecture 19 used 30°, NA = 0.5). In air NA < 1; in general NA = n sin θ
The two limits
M11 §3.1		F = (0.5λ/NA), DOF = (0.6λ/NA²); the image plane may lie within ±DOF/2 of best focus. The notes write "DF" and call it depth of field in the formula, depth of focus in the heading (p. 97); the quantity is the depth of focus
The notes' example
M11 §3.1		λ = 100 nm, NA = 0.5: F = 0.1 µm, DOF = 240 nm, focus within ±0.12 µm. "The required wavelength of the source is 100 nm": no such source exists; the shortest the notes discuss are F₂ 157 nm and Ar₂* 126 nm
Trends
M11 §3.1		F ∝ 1/NA, DOF ∝ 1/NA². At fixed NA a shorter λ shrinks both. At a fixed feature, DOF = (2.4F²/λ): a shorter λ gives more DOF — 0.13 µm features, 0.164 µm at 248 nm against 0.210 µm at 193 nm
Constants
M11 §3.2		General form F = k1(λ/NA), DOF = k2(λ/NA²). k1 0.8 in 1980, below 0.5 by 2001 (Wolf). Plummer: Rayleigh 0.61, practical 0.6–0.8, DOF = ±(λ/(2NA²)). The notes' 0.5 and 0.6 (an aggressive F, a conservative DOF) are the course's values for any calculation
Planarity
M11 §3.3		DOF of a few tenths of a µm, comparable with the resist and with the steps of earlier levels: planarity at each exposure is "a critical issue" (notes); Lecture 19: this is where CMP enters. CMP let the industry relax the lens DOF to about ±0.2 µm (Wolf p. 297)
Mix-and-match
M11 §3.3		Newest tools only on critical levels (notes p. 98); at 0.13 µm: gate, active area, contact, first metal, first via (Wolf). Implant masks: large features in thick resist, which a high-resolution stepper prints worse for its limited DOF
0.13 µm
M11 §3.3		At 248 nm only by pushing k1 below 0.5 with RETs; at 193 nm and NA 0.75 with k1 = 0.5, without them (Wolf p. 296)

Wavelengths, sources and resists (notes pp. 91–92, 96)

Wavelength
M11 §4.1, M12 §1.1		SourcePhoton energyFeaturesResist platform
436 nm (g-line)Hg arc lamp, filtered2.84 eVi-line printed 0.35 µm with DNQ/novolac; could not print 0.25 µm even with phase-shift masks and off-axis illumination (Wolf pp. 227–228)DNQ/novolac
365 nm (i-line)Hg arc lamp, filtered3.40 eV
