Why the mercury-line resists stopped, and chemical amplification (notes p. 91)

ItemThe course's version
DNQ/novolac
M11 §4.2		Novolac resin plus diazonaphthoquinone (DNQ), which inhibits dissolution in base until light converts it to an acid that no longer does
Failure 1: absorption
M11 §4.2		Novolac and DNQ absorb strongly near 250 nm; light absorbed at the top never reaches the bottom, so the opening narrows downward or never clears. More dose over-exposes the top and edges first
Failure 2: sensitivity
M11 §4.2		The Hg lamp is far weaker in the DUV than at the i-line; DNQ quantum efficiency ≈ 0.3, so at most about threefold gain — too little light for an economic exposure
The notes' sentence
M11 §4.2		Regular resists could no longer print 0.25 µm; illumination moved to the 248 nm KrF laser and a resist based on chemical amplification, offering "increased photo speed and contrast"
CA components
M11 §5.1		A matrix resin whose acidic sites are protected (IBM: polyhydroxystyrene, pHOST, with t-BOC groups) and a photoacid generator (PAG; early PAGs onium salts)
Exposure
M11 §5.1		A photon decomposes a PAG molecule into one acid molecule, efficiency below one: a latent image in acid, nothing else changed
Post-exposure bake
M11 §5.1		Of order 120 °C for a few minutes (Plummer). Each acid removes a protecting group and is regenerated: 500–1000 reactions per photon absorbed (Wolf), "tens or hundreds" per acid (Plummer). Deprotected resin dissolves in TMAH
Gain
M11 §5.1		Dose: DNQ ≈ 100 mJ/cm²; CA 20–40 mJ/cm² (Plummer), 10–50 mJ/cm² (Wolf). Overall quantum efficiency can far exceed 1. Higher contrast: a small dose change switches solubility, sharpening a blurred aerial image. Developed at IBM in the early 1980s because the Hg lamp gave little light at 248 nm. Negative CA resists use the acid to cross-link
Cost 1: airborne base
M11 §5.2		Amines, ammonia, NH₄OH, the HMDS adhesion promoter, N-methylpyrrolidone, of order 10 ppb in fab air, neutralise the acid in the top surface before the bake: the surface stays insoluble, a T-top. Delay usually < 3 min, sometimes ≥ 10 min; 10 min in ≥ 5 ppb amine can shift the CD by 10–20 nm. After the PEB the resist is no longer susceptible
Remedies for base
M11 §5.2		Activated-charcoal filtration of tool and track air to < 1 ppb (ITRS), protected holding positions, a removable water-soluble top film, stabilising additives, densifying the resist by a bake (Wolf p. 232)
Cost 2: PEB temperature
M11 §5.2		Shipley APEX: 16 nm per °C; later 2.6–1.8 nm/°C, one resist ≈ 0. Even 2 nm/°C needs hot plates uniform well within 0.5 °C. Early 193 nm resists: 10 nm/°C against a 3 nm/°C target

Resist polymers, requirements and collapse (notes pp. 91–93)

ItemThe course's version
Why not hydroxystyrene at 193 nm
M11 §6.1		Its aromatic ring gives etch resistance but absorbs strongly at 193 nm. The 193 nm resists, "all different than the hydroxy styrene based resist" (p. 91), carry alicyclic groups (saturated rings) for etch resistance with less absorption
Acrylates
M11 §6.1		Acrylate polymers with alicyclic groups: best dense-line performance ("adamantane methylacrylate", Wolf); generally poor etch resistance
Pure cyclic olefins
M11 §6.1		Backbone built from cyclic olefins: better etch resistance, robust in harsh etches
COMA
M11 §6.1		Cyclic olefin/maleic anhydride free-radical copolymer: very cost-effective
Resist thickness
M11 §6.1		193 nm: 400–600 nm against ≈ 1000 nm earlier (Wolf p. 240), for depth of focus and collapse; where the resist cannot survive the etch, a hard mask replaces it
157 nm resists
M11 §6.2, §6.3		7.9 eV photons; most earlier polymers absorb strongly (notes p. 92). Highly fluorinated hydrocarbons (fluoropolymers on norbornane, Wolf) and siloxanes (Si–O backbone; hard to reach a high glass transition). Existing resists 60–70 nm thick used as a stop-gap (Wolf p. 244)
Outgassing
M11 §6.2		Fragments settle on the final lens; at 157 nm "even a monolayer of oxygen or water can significantly change the transmission" (notes p. 93). Fluorocarbon deposits removed by bleeding O₂ with the laser on; siloxanes leave SiO₂; N₂ purge of the lens–wafer gap. Already a problem at 193 nm (shorter working distance)
Three requirements of every CA resist
M11 §6.3		(1) High transparency at the exposure wavelength; (2) sufficient resistance to plasma etching; (3) functional units that undergo efficient photochemical transformations changing their solubility (notes p. 92). Wolf adds a glass transition above process temperatures, shelf life, low toxicity
Resist collapse
M11 §7		During rinse and dry, surface tension in the draining water pulls closely spaced lines together; lines held only at the base bend and stick. "Becomes common when resist-feature aspect-ratios exceed about 4:1" (pp. 91–92): hmax = 4w. Dense 130 nm lines: 0.52 µm; a 400 nm film allows 100 nm lines, 600 nm only 150 nm
What sets resist thickness
M11 §7		Depth of focus and collapse pull it down; etch resistance pulls it up. Remedies: hard mask; bilayer resist (thin imaging layer over a thick etch-resistant, anti-reflective underlayer)

Reflections and anti-reflective coatings (notes pp. 93–95)

ItemThe course's version
Standing waves
M11 §8.1		Substrate reflection interferes with the incoming light: dose varies with depth with a period of half the wavelength in the resist, rippling the sidewall. Coupled dose varies with resist thickness, so linewidth varies over topography (Wolf's clearing-dose swing). A PEB smooths standing waves in the older resists (M11 §1.2)
Reflective notching
M11 §8.1		A sloped surface (a step, a poly edge) reflects light sideways under the opaque area, narrowing the line locally. Both effects worsen at shorter λ, where substrate reflectivity rises. ARCs "minimize standing waves and reflective notching" (p. 93)
Two mechanisms
M11 §8.2		Attenuation (absorb what passes) or index matching (reflections from the coating's two surfaces cancel); all BARCs use some of both (Wolf). Useful k 0.22–0.5, reflectance back into the resist < 10 %; too high a k makes the coating's own top reflective. Thick absorbing layers (60–90 nm, i-line) gave way to index matching at 0.25 µm and below
TAR and BARC
M11 §8.2		Top ARC on the resist; bottom ARC under it, acting at the interface that causes the problem. Both together for the tightest levels (poly gate). At 0.25 µm and below a BARC must suppress more than 99 % of the substrate reflection (notes p. 94)
Quarter-wave
M11 §8.3		"An optical path length of λ/4" giving a "half-wavelength phase shift" (p. 94): nd = (λ/4), two-pass path (λ/2); or (3λ/(4n)). Needs index rising resist → BARC → substrate and weak absorption. Tolerance tight: 3 nm off a 31 nm layer changes the path by 12 nm, about 10 % of the half-wave shift
Organic BARC
M11 §8.4		Spun on; 60–120 nm (notes); mainly absorption by dyes; self-planarizes (thicker in hollows); opened by O₂ plasma before the main etch; ≈ 200 nm wet-opened films before DUV (unsuitable below 0.5 µm). "Cannot be used with CA resist" (notes p. 94; Wolf: acidity intermixes, so chemistry is matched to the resist — see 5.4)
Inorganic BARC
M11 §8.4		PECVD SiON or SiN, 30–50 nm (notes); index matching and absorption, n, k, thickness tuned by the CVD; conformal; hard to remove, some processes leave it; i-line, 248, 193 nm (at 193 nm made more oxide-like: Si-rich SiON is opaque). Amine (NH₃) groups poison CA resists: thin oxide cap or O₂/N₂O treatment
PVD TiN
M11 §8.4		Reactive sputtering, ≈ 80 nm (Wolf), absorption (k ≈ 1.1); left in place, can serve as a diffusion barrier; mainly i-line patterning of Al; 20–30 % reflective in the DUV
UARL
M11 §8.4		Dual damascene over transparent IMD and reflective Cu or poly at several depths: one BARC cannot cancel all (notes p. 95). Universal anti-reflective layer of two or three layers: opaque bottom layer hides everything below; upper layer cancels its one known reflection; optional thin oxide keeps them from the DUV resist

Excimer lasers, optics and masks (notes pp. 96–97)

ItemThe course's version
Excimer
M12 §1.1		"Excited dimer" of a noble gas and a halogen, bound only when excited. Capacitor at ≈ 12 kV discharges in ≈ 75 ns through the gas; KrF* emits 248 nm and the unbound ground state dissociates, so the lower level stays empty; spontaneous lifetime ns–µs. KrF becomes ArF by changing only the gas and mirrors
KrF output
M12 §1.2		2–20 W average, 200–2500 Hz, pulses 5–20 ns (notes p. 96), ≈ 10 mJ each (Wolf): MW peak powers that can damage coatings and lens materials
Dose
M12 §1.2		Integrated pulse energies; at least ≈ 100 pulses per exposure for 1 % dose control (notes; Wolf)
Servicing
M12 §1.2		Gas refilled ≈ every 5 days (notes); cavity cleaned after 10⁹ pulses, replaced after 2.5×10⁹ (Wolf 1998, repeated in the notes); ≈ 2×10⁹ pulses a year in production
Line narrowing
M12 §1.3		Free-running KrF ≈ 300 pm wide; an uncorrected DUV lens needs < 1 pm (dispersion, chromatic aberration). Prisms and gratings in the cavity; 1 pm of drift moves focus ≈ 0.15 µm. Later < 0.5 pm, and < 0.4 pm after 180 nm (Wolf)
ArF
M12 §1.4		20 W average at 4 kHz (notes p. 97), 5 mJ per pulse; pulse lengthened to cut peak power, because optics degrade faster at 193 nm
F₂
M12 §1.4		Two lines, 157.63 and 157.52 nm, 87 % in the longer (notes; Lecture 19 said 13 % — see 5.4); 110 pm apart, so a refractive tool runs on 157.63 nm alone
Lens materials
M12 §2		Glass absorbs below ≈ 350 nm (Lecture 19). Fused silica, the notes' "quartz optics", at 248 and 193 nm; crystalline quartz is birefringent. Fused silica compacts under ArF light, so 193 nm lenses use some CaF₂ where intensity is highest. At 157 nm CaF₂ is "virtually the only" material: pure, hygroscopic, soft, chips, 50–100 % longer to make, intrinsically birefringent (found May 2001). Lecture 19 added MgF₂, AlF₃, LaF₃, LiF
157 nm tool
M12 §2		Whole optical path N₂-purged; scanner $12–20 M (notes p. 97); catadioptric optics about a third the size and weight (at 193 nm: 1.7 kg of CaF₂ against 48 kg refractive)
Reticle
M12 §2		Fused silica 0.5 ppm/K, CaF₂ 19 ppm/K (38×): 0.1 K over 100 mm misplaces 5 nm against 190 nm. At 157 nm the reticle can still be fused silica with little OH and some F (82 % transmission); below it a CaF₂ reticle would be needed

Lowering k1: off-axis illumination, OPC and phase-shift masks (notes pp. 99–101)

ItemThe course's version
RETs
M12 §3		Unaided, k1 above ≈ 0.75; ARCs, top-surface imaging, off-axis illumination, OPC and PSMs took it toward 0.35 (Wolf). The notes treat only the PSM
Off-axis illumination
M12 §3.1		Image needs the zero and at least one first order (sin θ1 = (λ/p)). On axis, smallest pitch (λ/NA), k1 = 0.5; tilted so zero and first order sit at opposite pupil edges, (λ/(2NA)), k1 = 0.25. Orders equidistant from centre → more DOF. Tuned to one pitch; isolated lines gain little; dipole, quadrupole, annular apertures. Mack and Fehrs 1989; Canon and Nikon steppers 1992
Optical proximity effect
M12 §3.2		Printed size depends on neighbours: iso-dense bias (0.35 µm isolated lines 0.09 µm wider than dense), line-end shortening, corner rounding (square contacts print round)
OPC
M12 §3.2		Pre-distorted mask: line bias, serifs, jogs, sub-resolution scattering bars at the dense pitch. Rule-based (minutes to hours) or model-based (hours to days). Costs mask data, writing time and inspection; corrects distortion, not resolution
PSM principle
M12 §4.1		Levenson, Viswanathan and Simpson, 1982 (notes p. 99; IBM). Binary mask: fields of neighbouring openings have one sign and add under the chromium. A 180° shifter on one opening reverses its field; fields cancel midway, intensity goes to zero
Shifter
M12 §4.2		d = λ/(2(n − 1)) (notes p. 99), layer added or recess etched into the plate. Fused silica: 243.8 nm at 248 nm, 172.6 nm at 193.40 nm; one wavelength only
The notes' example
M12 §4.3		248 nm, NA 0.6: 0.3 µm binary → 0.18 µm with PSM (notes p. 101), k1 0.726 → 0.435; 40 % is Wolf's maximum for a strong PSM. Uses: a better image, or more DOF at constant resolution through a lower NA (Plummer's two)
Alternating PSM
M12 §4.4		Shifters on alternate openings (Levenson PSM): the largest gain, up to 40 % in resolution. Fails for brick-wall layouts and isolated features; a shifted/unshifted boundary prints a dark line (90° step or negative resist). The notes' cost: "arbitrary mask shapes"
Attenuated and rim PSM
M12 §4.4		Chromium replaced by a film passing ≈ 10 %, shifted 180° (i-line: chromium oxynitride or molybdenum silicon oxynitride); any layout incl. contacts; made and repaired like binary; first PSM in production. Weak PSMs: less resolution, more DOF. 2000: attenuated on 0.18 µm contacts, alternating on gates, interconnect, arrays; 1997: ≈ 1 % of masks shifted. Attenuated PSM + OAI especially effective
Mask error factor
M12 §4.5		Reduction divides mask errors only for features above ≈ 150 % of λ; MEF > 4 at (0.35λ/NA), 125 nm at 248 nm and NA 0.7: the mask must meet wafer tolerances

The optical limit and immersion (notes pp. 101–102)

ItemThe course's version
Why optics outlived the 1 µm forecast
M12 §5		Wolf: diffraction-limited lenses at NA 0.8 by 2001; λ 436 → 193 nm; resists took k1 from ≈ 0.8 to below 0.5; CMP cut the DOF needed to ≈ ±0.2 µm; RETs
The notes' limits
M12 §5		70 nm needs 157 nm (F₂) or 126 nm (Ar₂*) with CaF₂ single crystals for the mask; CMP improves DOF to ±0.2 µm; lower limit of optical lithography about 70–50 nm. Wolf adds: CMP cannot flatten further, NA cannot exceed 1
Immersion (Lecture 19; not in the notes)
M12 §5.1		Water between lens and resist, n ≈ 1.44 at 193 nm: NA = n sin θ ceiling 1.44, wavelength in water 134 nm. Lin 2004: 193 nm immersion in place of 157 nm. NA 1.35 scanners print 38–40 nm in production (71.5 nm by the course's F). It removed NA < 1, not the flatness demand: higher NA shortens DOF further

Beyond light: the non-optical methods (notes pp. 102–105; Lecture 20)

Method
M12 §6		Radiation and wavelengthOpticsMaskStatus in the sources
X-ray proximity (§7)Photons, 4–20 Å, 0.6–3.1 keV (Lecture 20); ≈ 1 nm, 1–10 keV (Plummer)None; 1:1 shadow printingThin membrane with Au, Pt or W absorberAbandoned by 2001 (Wolf)
EUV (§8)Photons, 10–15 nm, ≈ 100 eV; 13.5 nm in practiceMultilayer mirrors; 4–5× reduction; vacuumReflective multilayer with absorberLeading candidate in 2001; now in production
E-beam direct write (§9)Electrons, ≈ 0.1 Å at 10 kVElectrostatic and magnetic deflectionNone; pattern from dataMasks and research; too slow for wafers
E-beam projection, SCALPEL (§9.4)Electrons, 100 keV (Plummer)Electron lenses; 4:1 reductionSiN membrane with W or Au scatterersIn decline by 2001 (Wolf)
Ion beam (§9.5)Ga⁺ ions (Lecture 20; not in the notes)Electrostatic focusingNone (focused beam)"Maybe one day" (Lecture 20)
Common to the mask methodsMasks $5000–100,000 (notes p. 102); no pellicle can be mounted, so masks are far more vulnerable to particles (for EUV, nothing transmits the radiation)
ItemThe course's version
X-ray mask and source
M12 §7		No lens or mirror focuses X-rays (index within a small fraction of 1). Membrane a few µm of Si or SiN (Lecture 20; Plummer adds SiC, BN), absorber Au or Pt (Lecture 20), W where Au contamination matters; fragile, stress distorts, 1:1 features written by e-beam. Resist PMMA, positive (Lecture 20 said negative). Synchrotron source (Berkeley, Brookhaven, Argonne named); its cost was the lecture's reason for the move to EUV
EUV in general
M12 §8		"Needs few changes in manufacturing from the current methods" (notes p. 102): still reduction projection, so the mask is easier than a 1:1 X-ray mask. Nothing transmits EUV: every element, the mask too, a mirror; beam path in vacuum
Mo/Si mirror
M12 §8.1		≈ 40 pairs of high-Z Mo and low-Z Si, layers tens of Å, deposited to better than 1 Å; at the Bragg period reflections add, "as high as 70 %" (notes p. 103). d = λ/(2 sin θ): 6.75 nm per pair at 13.5 nm, 270 nm stack. Deposition on curved surfaces difficult "because of the extremely tight tolerance" (notes)
Nine reflections
M12 §8.2		Two illuminator mirrors, the mask, six projection mirrors: 3.1 % passes at 68 % (Wolf, achieved), 4.0 % at 70 % (notes), 13.4 % at 80 % (Lecture 20). 68 → 70 % gives ≈ 30 % more light
EUV mask
M12 §8.3		Mo/Si on a flat substrate, absorber Cr, TiN or TaN (notes; Lecture 20 added Au and Pt and fewer pairs). Mo and Si intermix above 150 °C, so absorber steps stay below it. Resist < 200 nm (Wolf)
EUV source
M12 §8.3		Notes: Nd:YAG laser (1.06 µm, Lecture 20) on a supersonic Xe jet; 1500 W of laser → 10 W EUV (pp. 103–104); Wolf: production needs > 50 W. Production today: CO₂ laser on tin droplets, 210 W in 2017; volume EUV from 2019
Electron wavelength
M12 §9.1		λ = √(150/V) Å (Lecture 20): 0.122 Å at 10 kV, 0.055 Å at 50 kV, some 16 000 times shorter than ArF light: diffraction plays no part
Beam-writer limits
M12 §9.2		Scattering in resist and substrate (10–20 keV electrons travel > 1 µm): the proximity effect, corrected by point-by-point dose. Serial writing: ≈ 1 wafer/h against > 50 for a stepper, so used for masks (and research). Exposure charges the gate oxide
Raster and vector
M12 §9.3		Raster sweeps every row, blanked where empty: time ∝ field area, suits dense patterns (Lecture 20: ≈ 80 % written). Vector jumps between pattern elements: time ∝ pattern area plus settling per jump, suits sparse patterns
SCALPEL
M12 §9.4		Bell Labs 1989; 4:1. SiN membrane 100–150 nm with 25–50 nm W or Au; both transmit, metal scatters strongly, a back-focal-plane aperture blocks the strongly scattered electrons. Advantages (notes p. 105): no synchrotron; added one stepper at a time; thin SiN heats and distorts little; DOF 50× that of 0.25 µm optics. Limitations: throughput, proximity effects, radiation damage, charging, no good resist (notes pp. 104–105; Lecture 20 only distinguished mask projection from the beam writer)

One mask level, positive resist (not in the notes: the recorded class of 23 October and Plummer; M11 §1.2)

  1. Clean and dehydrate; apply hexamethyldisilazane (HMDS), usually as a vapour: an adhesion promoter, one end bonding to SiO₂, the other to the resist.
  2. Spin-coat at 3000–6000 rpm for ≈ 30 s: 0.6–1 µm, set by spin speed and viscosity (solvent content); thicker for an implant mask.
  3. Prebake (soft bake), hot plate, 90–100 °C: solvent ≈ 25 % → ≈ 5 %, better adhesion, spin stress relaxed.
  4. Align to the previous level's marks and expose, field by field: the resolution is won or lost here.
  5. Post-exposure bake: optional for older resists (smooths standing waves); essential for CA resists (completes the chemistry).
  6. Develop 30–60 s at room temperature, spray or puddle, in aqueous base: TMAH, KOH or NaOH.
  7. Postbake (hard bake), 100–140 °C: hardens the resist for the etch or implant.
  8. Etch or implant; strip the resist, usually in an oxygen plasma.

M11's figure of the sequence omits the hard bake.

Chemically amplified imaging (notes p. 91; M11 §5.1–5.2)

  1. Coat with the CA resist: protected resin (pHOST with t-BOC in IBM's) plus PAG.
  2. Expose (20–40 mJ/cm²): each effective photon releases one acid from a PAG; a latent image in acid.
  3. Hold briefly (usually < 3 min) in filtered air (< 1 ppb base): airborne base would neutralise the surface acid.
  4. PEB, ≈ 120 °C for a few minutes, temperature uniform well within 0.5 °C: acid-catalysed deprotection, acid regenerated, 500–1000 reactions per photon. Afterwards the resist is no longer susceptible to base.
  5. Develop in TMAH: deprotected (exposed) resin dissolves.

EUV exposure path (notes pp. 102–104; M12 §8)

  1. Nd:YAG pulses (1.06 µm) strike a supersonic xenon jet; the plasma emits broad-band light with an EUV part: 1500 W of laser → 10 W EUV in 2001.
  2. Two illuminator mirrors, Mo/Si, ≈ 70 % each (notes).
  3. Reflective mask: Mo/Si multilayer on a flat substrate with a patterned Cr, TiN or TaN absorber, processed below 150 °C.
  4. Six projection mirrors, 4–5× reduction, all in vacuum: ≈ 4 % of the EUV entering the optics reaches the wafer.
  5. Resist below 200 nm thick (Wolf), since nearly everything absorbs EUV.

SCALPEL exposure (notes pp. 104–105; M12 §9.4)

  1. A 100 keV electron beam (Plummer) illuminates the mask: 100–150 nm SiN membrane with 25–50 nm W or Au pattern.
  2. SiN scatters the electrons weakly, through small angles; the metal strongly, through large angles; both transmit, so the mask stays cool.
  3. An electron lens focuses the beam; an aperture in its back focal plane passes the weakly scattered electrons and blocks most of the strongly scattered.
  4. The wafer receives a 4:1-reduced, high-contrast image with the metal regions dark.

Raster against vectorRaster for dense (≈ 80 % written), vector for sparseOne garbled sentence of Lecture 20's captions prefers vector at high densityM12 §9.3

The notes' "half the period" is the inverse

The notes (p. 101), copying Plummer §5.2.4, and Lecture 20 say the pattern a phase shifter adds has "half the period of the original grating". A shifter on every other opening repeats every two openings, so the shifter pattern and its field have twice the grating's period; their spatial frequency is halved, which is why the lens can collect it. The intensity still repeats with the grating's own period. Read the sentence as "half the spatial frequency" (M12 §4.1).

5.5 Self-check

Choose one. Which coating suppresses the substrate reflection at the interface that causes it — (a) a top anti-reflective coating (b) a bottom anti-reflective coating (c) a pellicle (d) a hard mask

(b). A BARC lies between resist and substrate and attenuates or cancels the light reflected there; a TAR sits on the resist's top surface and cannot remove the substrate reflection. At 0.25 µm and below a BARC must suppress more than 99 % of it (notes p. 94). M11 §8.2.

Choose one. At a fixed wavelength, the lens NA of a projection tool is raised. The depth of focus — (a) increases (b) decreases (c) does not change (d) none of the above

(b). DOF = (0.6λ/NA²) falls as 1/NA² while F falls only as 1/NA, so resolution is gained linearly and depth of focus lost quadratically. M11 §3.1.

Choose one. A feature size is held fixed and the exposure moves from 248 nm to 193 nm, the NA chosen to print that feature. The depth of focus — (a) increases (b) decreases (c) does not change (d) none of the above

(a). Eliminating NA gives DOF = (2.4F²/λ): the shorter wavelength reaches the feature with a smaller NA. For 0.13 µm features, 0.164 µm at 248 nm against 0.210 µm at 193 nm. At fixed NA the answer would be (b). M11 §3.1.

Choose one. In the notes, resist collapse becomes common above an aspect ratio of about — (a) 2:1 (b) 3:1 (c) 4:1 (d) 10:1

(c). "When resist-feature aspect-ratios exceed about 4:1" (notes pp. 91–92): surface tension in the draining rinse water pulls neighbouring lines together, so hmax = 4w. M11 §7.

Choose one. The 193 nm resist platform with the best dense-line performance but generally poor etch resistance — (a) hydroxystyrene (b) acrylates (c) pure cyclic olefins (d) cyclic olefin/maleic anhydride

(b). Acrylates with alicyclic groups; pure cyclic olefins give better etch resistance, COMA is the cost-effective one, and hydroxystyrene is excluded at 193 nm because its aromatic rings absorb there. M11 §6.1.

Choose one. T-topped lines in a positive chemically amplified resist are caused by — (a) over-exposure at the resist surface (b) airborne base neutralising acid in the surface before the post-exposure bake (c) standing waves from the substrate (d) a hard bake above 140 °C

(b). Amines and ammonia at the order of 10 ppb neutralise the photo-generated acid in the top layer while the wafer waits; that skin stays protected and insoluble and overhangs the dissolved resist below. After the PEB the resist is no longer susceptible. M11 §5.2.

Choose one. The photon energy of the Ar₂* excimer at 126 nm — (a) 5.00 eV (b) 6.42 eV (c) 7.90 eV (d) 9.84 eV

(d). E = 1239.84 eV·nm / 126 nm = 9.84 eV; (a)–(c) are KrF, ArF and F₂. The 126 nm photon carries 1.25 times the F₂ photon's energy. M11 §4.1; formula sheet (11.3).

Choose one. The notes' reflectance of one Mo/Si EUV mirror — (a) up to 60 % (b) 68 % (c) up to 70 % (d) 80 %

(c), "as high as 70 %" (notes p. 103). The sources differ: Wolf reports 68 % achieved at 13.4 nm with more than 70 % needed, Plummer above 60 %, and Lecture 20 said 80 %. Over nine reflections 70 % passes 4.0 % of the light. M12 §8.2.

Chemically amplified resists: the problem they solved, how they work, and the problem they brought — with a sketch.

At 248 nm the DNQ/novolac resists of the mercury lines failed twice: novolac and DNQ absorb strongly near 250 nm, so light absorbed near the top never exposed the bottom, and the mercury lamp, the only DUV source at first, was weak there, while a DNQ resist uses only about 30 % of its photons. A chemically amplified (CA) resist separates the chemistry from the photon count. Its resin is soluble in base but its acidic sites are capped by protecting groups (IBM: polyhydroxystyrene with t-BOC); a photoacid generator (PAG) releases one acid molecule per effective photon, a latent image in acid. In the post-exposure bake, about 120 °C for a few minutes, each acid removes a protecting group and is regenerated, 500–1000 reactions per photon (Wolf), and the deprotected resin dissolves in TMAH. The dose falls from about 100 mJ/cm² to 20–40 mJ/cm², and the contrast rises. The catalyst is also the weakness. Base in fab air (amines, ammonia, HMDS, NMP, about 10 ppb) neutralises the small acid concentration in the surface before the bake, leaving an insoluble skin, a T-top, and a 10-minute wait in 5 ppb or more can move the CD by up to 10–20 nm; filtration below 1 ppb, a top coat and a prompt bake are the fixes. The linewidth also follows the bake temperature, 16 nm/°C in an early 248 nm resist, 2.6–1.8 nm/°C later, which demands hot plates uniform well within 0.5 °C.

Full treatment: M11 §5.

Light reflected from the substrate: the problem and the fix — with a sketch.

Light that crosses the resist partly reflects from the substrate and travels back up, interfering with the incoming light. The dose then varies with depth with a period of half the wavelength in the resist, a standing wave that ripples the developed sidewall, and the dose coupled into the film varies with its thickness, so linewidth varies over topography. Over a slope the reflection is thrown sideways under the opaque area of the mask and narrows the line locally, reflective notching. Both worsen at shorter wavelengths, where the substrate reflects more. An anti-reflective coating (ARC) suppresses the reflection by attenuation, absorbing the light, or by index matching, cancelling it by interference (notes p. 93); a bottom ARC (BARC) acts at the substrate, a top ARC (TAR) only at the resist's top surface. Index matching uses the quarter-wave condition: a BARC of optical path nd = λ/4 returns the substrate reflection a half wavelength, 2nd = λ/2, behind the reflection from its top, and the two cancel. At 0.25 µm and below the BARC must remove more than 99 %. The coatings are organic (spun on, 60–120 nm), inorganic PECVD SiON or SiN (30–50 nm) and PVD TiN. Over copper at several depths a single BARC fails, and the UARL's opaque bottom layer leaves one reflection to cancel.

Full treatment: M11 §8.

How is one level patterned in positive resist, step by step, from the bare wafer to the stripped resist?
  1. Clean and dehydrate the surface; prime with HMDS vapour, which bonds to the oxide at one end and the resist at the other, for adhesion.
  2. Spin-coat the resist at 3000–6000 rpm for about 30 s to 0.6–1 µm; speed and viscosity set the thickness.
  3. Soft bake at 90–100 °C on a hot plate: solvent from about 25 % to 5 %, better adhesion, spin stress relaxed.
  4. Align to the marks of the previous level and expose field by field, by stepper or scanner through a 4–5× reticle.
  5. Post-exposure bake: completes the deprotection in a CA resist, smooths standing waves in older ones.
  6. Develop 30–60 s in aqueous base (TMAH): the exposed resist dissolves and the pattern copies the mask.
  7. Hard bake at 100–140 °C so the resist survives the etch or implant.
  8. Etch or implant through the openings, then strip the resist in an oxygen plasma.

Full treatment: M11 §1.2.

What is a phase-shift mask, what does it gain, and what limits the alternating type?

A binary mask passes light (amplitude 1) or blocks it (0). Near the resolution limit the lens spreads the field of each opening under the chromium beside it, and with a binary mask the fields of two neighbouring openings have the same sign and add there, so the dark gap receives light and the lines are barely resolved. A phase-shift mask (Levenson, Viswanathan and Simpson, 1982) adds a transparent layer of index n and thickness d = λ/(2(n − 1)) over alternate openings, or etches a recess of that depth: the light through it is delayed half a wavelength, its field changes sign, and the two fields cancel midway, so the intensity there falls to zero however much they overlap. The notes' case: 0.3 µm with a binary mask becomes 0.18 µm at 248 nm and NA 0.6, k1 0.726 → 0.435, the 40 % that is the most a strong PSM gives. The gain can instead be taken as depth of focus by printing the same feature at lower NA. The alternating PSM needs every opening to differ in phase from all its neighbours: brick-wall layouts cannot be assigned, isolated features gain nothing, and a shifted region meeting an unshifted one prints a dark line (90° steps or a negative resist suppress it) — the "arbitrary mask shapes" the notes name as its cost. The attenuated PSM, a ≈ 10 %, 180°-shifted film in place of chromium, gains less but works on any layout, contacts included.

Full treatment: M12 §4.

Shadow printing at the i-line. A 1.2 µm resist is exposed at the i-line, λ = 0.365 µm. (a) What grating period and line width does hard contact allow? (b) What period does an 8 µm proximity gap allow, and by what factor is it worse?

Bracket, hard contact. s + d/2 = 0 + 1.2 µm/2 = 0.6 µm.

Under the root. λ(s + d/2) = 0.365 µm × 0.6 µm = 0.219 µm²; √ = 0.4680 µm.

(a) Period and line. 2bmin = 3 × 0.4680 µm = 1.404 µm; bmin = 0.702 µm.

(b) With the gap. 0.365 µm × (8 + 0.6) µm = 3.139 µm²; √ = 1.772 µm; 2bmin = 5.32 µm, bmin = 2.66 µm.

1.404 µm period (0.702 µm lines) in hard contact; 5.32 µm (2.66 µm lines) across 8 µm, 3.8 times worse

Check. Only the bracket changes, so the ratio is √(8.6/0.6) = 3.79: the gap, not the wavelength, dominates the blur.

Method: M11 §2.1; formula sheet (11.1).

Feature size and depth of focus. (a) A KrF stepper (248 nm) has a converging cone of half-angle 40° (illustrative). Find NA, F, DOF and the focus tolerance. (b) What wavelength would print F = 0.10 µm at NA 0.75, and with what DOF?

(a) Numerical aperture. NA = sin 40° = 0.643; NA² = 0.413.

(a) Feature size. F = 0.5 × 0.248 µm / 0.643 = 0.193 µm.

(a) Depth of focus. DOF = 0.6 × 0.248 µm / 0.413 = 0.360 µm, so ±0.180 µm about best focus.

(b) Wavelength. From F = 0.5λ/NA, λ = 2F·NA = 2 × 0.10 µm × 0.75 = 0.150 µm = 150 nm.

(b) Depth of focus. DOF = 0.6 × 0.150 µm / 0.5625 = 0.160 µm.

(a) NA 0.643, F = 0.193 µm, DOF = 0.360 µm (±0.180 µm); (b) 150 nm, DOF 0.160 µm

Check. DOF/F = 1.2/NA = 1.87 in (a); in (b), 2.4F²/λ = 2.4 × 0.01 / 0.150 = 0.160 µm, the same. No source emits 150 nm; the nearest, F₂ at 157 nm, gives 0.105 µm at NA 0.75.

Method: M11 §3.1; formula sheet (11.2).

A phase-shift mask at the i-line. (a) A shifter material has n = 1.47 at 365 nm (illustrative). How thick is the 180° layer? (b) An i-line tool at NA 0.6 prints 0.35 µm. What k1 is that, what k1 would 0.25 µm need, and how large a reduction is it?

(a) Shifter. d = λ/(2(n − 1)) = 365 nm / (2 × 0.47) = 388.3 nm.

(b) Present k1. k1 = R·NA/λ = 0.35 × 0.6 / 0.365 = 0.575.

(b) Needed k1. 0.25 × 0.6 / 0.365 = 0.411.

(b) Reduction. 1 − 0.25/0.35 = 28.6 %.

d = 388.3 nm; k1 0.575 → 0.411, a feature 28.6 % smaller

Check. (n − 1)d = 0.47 × 388.3 = 182.5 nm = 365/2. The reduction is inside the 40 % a strong PSM gives at best (0.21 µm from 0.35 µm), yet Wolf records that the i-line could not print 0.25 µm even with phase-shift masks and off-axis illumination (M11 §4.1).

Method: M12 §4.2, §4.3; formula sheet (12.1).

Electron wavelength. Find the electron wavelength by the course's formula at 25 kV and at 100 V, the relativistic correction at 25 kV, and the ratio of the ArF wavelength to the 25 kV value.

25 kV. 150/25 000 = 0.006; √0.006 = 0.0775 Å.

100 V. √(150/100) = 1.225 Å.

Relativity. qV/(2m0c²) = 0.0245 at 25 kV; the wavelength is shorter by the factor 1/√1.0245, 1.2 %: 0.0765 Å.

Against ArF. 193 nm = 1930 Å; 1930/0.0775 = 2.49×10⁴.

0.0775 Å at 25 kV (0.0765 Å relativistic), 1.225 Å at 100 V; ArF light is 2.49×10⁴ times longer

Check. λ ∝ 1/√V: the 10 kV value, 0.1225 Å, times √(10/25) gives the same 0.0775 Å. The correction lies between M12's 0.35 % at 10 kV and 2.2 % at 50 kV. Diffraction plays no part; scattering in the resist (the proximity effect) limits the beam.

Method: M12 §9.1; formula sheet (12.5).

Resist collapse. (a) What is the thickest resist dense 90 nm lines allow? (b) A 500 nm resist is required for an etch; what are the narrowest dense lines it supports, and what aspect ratio would it give at 90 nm?

(a) Ceiling. hmax = 4w = 4 × 90 nm = 360 nm.

(b) Narrowest lines. wmin = h/4 = 500 nm / 4 = 125 nm.

(b) Aspect ratio at 90 nm. 500/90 = 5.56, above 4.

360 nm; 125 nm lines; 5.56:1 at 90 nm, beyond the 4:1 limit

Check. 360 nm lies below the 400–600 nm planned for 193 nm resists, so at 90 nm the resist alone may not survive the etch; a hard mask or a bilayer resist separates imaging from etch resistance.

Method: M11 §7.

Quarter-wave BARC at 193 nm. A BARC has n = 1.7 at 193 nm (illustrative). Find the quarter-wave thickness and the thicker solution, and check the two-pass path.

Wavelength in the film. λ/n = 193/1.7 = 113.5 nm.

Quarter-wave. d = 113.5 nm / 4 = 28.4 nm.

Thicker solution. 3λ/(4n) = 85.1 nm.

Two-pass path. 2nd = 2 × 1.7 × 28.4 nm = 96.5 nm = 193/2.

d = 28.4 nm or 85.1 nm

Check. The vacuum quarter-wave, 48.3 nm, would be 1.7 times too thick. 28.4 nm lies just below the notes' 30–50 nm for inorganic BARCs, and a film tuned for 248 nm is the wrong thickness here.

Method: M11 §8.3; formula sheet (11.4).

EUV mirrors. (a) Nine reflections at 65 % (illustrative) and at the notes' 70 %: what fraction reaches the wafer, and how much of a 10 W beam? (b) What is the Mo/Si period at 13.4 nm, normal incidence, and the thickness of 50 pairs?

(a) 65 %. 0.65⁹ = 0.0207 = 2.07 %; 10 W × 0.0207 = 0.207 W.

(a) 70 %. 0.70⁹ = 0.0404 = 4.04 %; 0.404 W.

(a) Ratio. 4.04/2.07 = 1.95.

(b) Period. d = λ/(2 sin 90°) = 13.4/2 = 6.70 nm.

(b) Stack. 50 × 6.70 nm = 335 nm.

2.07 % (0.207 W) and 4.04 % (0.404 W), nearly double; 6.70 nm per pair, 335 nm for 50 pairs

Check. Five points per mirror nearly double the throughput over nine mirrors, as two points gave 30 % in M12's example. 6.70 nm against 6.75 nm at 13.5 nm: d scales with λ.

Method: M12 §8.1, §8.2; formula sheet (12.4).

Immersion. A 193 nm lens has a 60° half-angle (illustrative) and works in water, n = 1.44. Find NA, F and DOF by the course's forms, and the same lens dry.

NA. NA = n sin θ = 1.44 × 0.866 = 1.247.

Feature. F = 0.5 × 193 nm / 1.247 = 77.4 nm.

Depth of focus. DOF = 0.6 × 193 nm / 1.247² = 74.5 nm.

Dry. NA = 0.866: F = 111.4 nm, DOF = 154.4 nm.

NA 1.247, F = 77.4 nm, DOF = 74.5 nm (dry: 111.4 nm, 154.4 nm)

Check. 77.4/111.4 = 0.694 = 1/1.44: the water divides F by n at the same angle. The course's DOF form treats the higher NA like any other; real immersion systems behave more favourably, beyond the notes.

Method: M12 §5.1; formula sheet (12.3), (11.2).

Mask error factor. A 4× reticle line is 12 nm too wide. What printed error results at MEF = 3, and at MEF = 1?

Rearrange. ΔCDprinted = MEF × ΔCDmask / M.

MEF = 3. 3 × 12 nm / 4 = 9.0 nm.

MEF = 1. 12 nm / 4 = 3.0 nm.

9.0 nm at MEF 3, against 3.0 nm for plain demagnification

Check. MEF = 3 near the resolution limit triples the printed error; at MEF = 4 the whole 12 nm would reach the wafer.

Method: M12 §4.5; formula sheet (12.2).

6 · Chapter 8: chemical mechanical polishing

Chapter 8 of the notes (pp. 106–130), taught in Lectures 20–24, answers a problem that the wiring above the transistors creates: every conformal level reproduces the steps beneath it and adds its own, until the surface is rougher than the ±0.5 µm that submicron lithography can focus over. Reflow, sacrificial etchback and spin-on glass smooth only over distances below about 10 µm and fail beyond three metal levels; chemical mechanical polishing (CMP) planarizes over several millimetres. M13 covers why planarization is needed, how it is classified and measured, the tool, Preston's law and the hydration-and-plowing mechanism, and why the high regions polish first. M14 covers the slurries, Kaufman's model of metal polishing, pads, the tungsten-plug and copper damascene processes with their dishing and erosion, endpoint detection, post-CMP cleaning, and the RC delay that Lecture 22 led on to, with the low-k dielectrics that reduce it. The polisher is the one that gave the bare wafer its finish in Chapter 2 (M04 §6.4), and the ceria slurry and nitride polish stop of this chapter are what Chapter 9's shallow trench isolation is built on.

6.1 Facts and lists

Why multilevel interconnect needs planarization (notes pp. 106–112)

ItemThe course's version
The requirement
M13 §1		Multilevel interconnection schemes require planarization, and global planarization of ULSI circuits requires CMP. PMD: the dielectric between polysilicon and metal-1; IMD: between metal levels; both usually CVD SiO₂, sometimes doped with P or B
The step stack
M13 §1.1		Field oxide 0.3 µm, polysilicon 0.4 µm, metal-1 0.5 µm, metal-2 1.0 µm, all conformal (p. 108): cumulative steps 0.3, 0.7, 1.2, 2.2 µm. Metal-1 is patterned over 0.7 µm, metal-2 over 1.2 µm
Three failures of a rough surface
M13 §1.2		(1) Poor step coverage: metal thinner on a high, steep step (Wolf: ts/tn), so higher resistance and current density there. (2) Metal stringers: a conformal film of thickness h2 over a step h1 is h1 + h2 thick vertically at the foot; an anisotropic etch timed for h2 leaves a strip that can short lines; clearing it needs an overetch that also erodes the resist. (3) Depth of focus: submicron lithography needs the surface planar within ±0.5 µm — the condition that makes planarization mandatory
Design rules set by the dielectric (p. 109)
M13 §1.3		(1) Minimum poly-to-metal-1 spacing where metal does not overlap poly; (2) minimum distance between coincident edges where metal-1 overlaps poly; (3) minimum metal-1-to-metal-1 spacing. Too narrow a gap gives a void or a crevice; stacked edges give a step too tall for metal-2. Planarization removes these rules
Four degrees of planarization (pp. 106–107)
M13 §2		1st, smoothing: slopes lessened, step height not significantly reduced. 2nd, partial (semi-) planarization: height reduced and slopes smoothed. 3rd, complete local planarization: steps eliminated where features are close together (Wolf: less than about 10 µm apart); wide, isolated features still show a step. 4th, complete global planarization: planar over arbitrary topography
Planarization factor
M13 §2		β = 1 − (tf/ti): 0 for none or smoothing only, 1 for complete. CMP 0.90–0.95 (p. 112), over several millimetres; Lecture 21: a third-degree process reaches "0.9 or something". The pre-CMP methods planarize only below about 10 µm (Wolf)
History
M13 §3		CMP spread through industry in the early 1990s (Lecture 21); developed at IBM from 1983, exclusively there until 1988, first published for oxide in 1989 (Wolf)
Methods before CMP (p. 112)
M13 §3		Thermal flow (reflow) of oxide layers, sacrificial resist etchback, spin-on glass. All local only; with more than three metal layers they are inadequate (p. 112)
Doped glass and reflow
M13 §3.1		BSG, PSG, BPSG: B₂O₃ and P₂O₅ in the SiO₂ network. Pure SiO₂ about 1700 °C, called a melting point (Lectures 22–23; no value in the notes or Wolf). Doped glass flows at 800–900 °C, reflow 850–900 °C (Lecture 23); Wolf: reflow of doped glasses above 800 °C, a 900 °C step. PMD only: after metal, steps stay at about 450–500 °C (Wolf). Lecture 23's use: rounding the top corners of a trench in doped oxide so the sputtered barrier is continuous
Sacrificial etchback
M13 §3.2		SOG or photoresist over the CVD dielectric, etched in O₂ or O₂ + CF₄ at equal rates (pp. 110–111). Used for two-level metal down to 1 µm technology; planarizes between steps 2–10 µm apart; resist alone exceeds 70 % planarization for features under about 10 µm (Wolf). Voids above a gap aspect ratio of 0.4; not feasible below 1.25 µm metal-1 spacing unless the oxide is deposited void-free at low temperature (Wolf: PECVD TEOS, O₃–TEOS, HDP CVD)
Where CMP is used
M13 §4		STI oxide, PMD and IMD oxides, tungsten plugs, copper lines, TiN and Ta barriers (Lectures 20, 22, 23). By 2000 a stacked-capacitor DRAM used upwards of nine polishing steps and a microprocessor up to sixteen (Wolf)

The tool and the metrics (notes pp. 112–117)

ItemThe course's version
What CMP is
M13 §4		Mechanical polishing assisted by chemical action, which produces selective material removal (p. 112)
Arrangement
M13 §4		Wafer face down on a rotating carrier with controlled downforce; pad on a rotating platen; aqueous slurry dripped on, a colloid with 10–100 nm particles (p. 124) that saturates the pad
Rotation
M13 §4		Same sense at the same angular velocity: every point sees relative speed ωd, uniform removal (M04 §6.4). Lecture 5: opposite senses (flagged, §6.4 below). In practice a constant frequency offset avoids vibration (Wolf)
Slurry
M13 §4		"Slurry is not recycled" (Lecture 21): used slurry carries particles from the mechanical action; re-use still in its infancy in 2002 (Wolf)
Tolerance
M13 §4		Oxide CMP removes about 0.5 ± 0.01 µm against wafer polishing's 10 ± 0.1 µm: ten times tighter absolute control (Wolf)
Goals (Wolf)
M13 §5		High DOP; < 150 nm between highest and lowest points of a die; within-wafer thickness ±2–5 % (1σ); RR 100–140 nm/min; uniform wafer to wafer; good selectivity or endpoint; few added defects; low cost per wafer
Removal rate, RR
M13 §5.1		Thickness removed per unit time from a non-patterned (blanket) wafer, ΔT/t (p. 113); most often in Å/min
Degree of planarization, DOP
M13 §5.2		((Hi − Hf)/Hi) × 100 %; 100 % when Hf = 0 (Lecture 22); DOP = 100β
WIDNU, WIWNU, WTWNU
M13 §5.3		σ/x̄ of n thickness readings, sample σ with n − 1: within one die, across one wafer, one per wafer over n wafers (pp. 113–116). Causes: within die, pattern dependence and dishing; within wafer, wafer thickness variation under a rigid pad, the rotation rates, an edge bead; wafer to wafer, pad ageing and slurry batches (Wolf)
Dishing (as a within-die effect)
M13 §5.3		Metal polished back from trenches in a planar dielectric ends recessed below it, especially in wide trenches (p. 114). Wolf also calls dishing the oxide eroded between planarized steps (the notes write "metal film", flagged)
Efficiency of planarization, %EOP
M13 §5.4		((Tr − Td)/Tr) × 100 (p. 117): Tr removed over a feature, Td lost at the centre of a wide space; ≈ 100 % is ideal. Enough oxide must remain over the lines to limit cross-talk and parasitic capacitance (p. 117)

How oxide is removed and how the surface is planarized (notes pp. 117–120)

ItemThe course's version
Preston's law
M13 §6.1		RR = KpPv, from glass polishing, F. W. Preston 1927 (Lecture 22 said 1938); empirical, mechanical variables only; Kp holds glass hardness, Young's modulus, slurry and pad. Typical 7 psi, 30–40 ft/min (Wolf)
Beyond the linear law
M13 §6.2		Not simply linear (p. 118): KpPαvβ fitted; Zhao and Shi (1999) KpvP2/3, the first model with pad and wafer hardness; a threshold pressure below which particles roll rather than slide (Wolf)
Silicon against oxide
M13 §6.3		Silicon: NaOH oxidizes it, colloidal silica (harder than the oxide, softer than Si) abrades. Oxide: already oxidized; silica in KOH or NH₄OH is about as hard as SiO₂ and cannot cut unreacted oxide. Water is essential: a silica slurry in oil polishes far more slowly (Wolf)
Oxide removal mechanism (p. 120)
M13 §6.3		(1) Plastic deformation by particles, growing with particle pressure and frictional heat; (2) hydration: deformed oxide takes up water and softens; (3) plowing: sliding particles cut the hydrated layer away, growing with pad speed; (4) repeat (implied). Co-factor: SiO₂ dissolution, faster at higher pH. Pressure drives step 1, speed step 3: hence Pv
Cook's model
M13 §6.3		Surface charge sets how often a particle reaches the oxide: silica on silica repel; a particle of another oxide attracted to SiO₂ polishes faster — the basis of ceria (Wolf)
Hardness and doping
M13 §6.4		RR rises as hardness falls: Si₃N₄ very hard and slowest; doped oxides and spin-on dielectrics faster (p. 118). P and B oxides (notes: "P₂O₃ and B₂O₅"; correctly P₂O₅ and B₂O₃) and F soften SiO₂; PSG and BPSG polish faster than undoped oxide. A hard layer under a soft one is a polish stop (nitride in STI)
Up-areas and down-areas
M13 §7.1		Up-areas must erode faster (p. 119); selectivity depends on feature size, pad structure and surface, and step height (also Lecture 23). Pad compliance puts the highest pressure on up-areas and most on the leading upper corner; selectivity falls as the step falls, leaving a small residual step
Pattern dependence
M13 §7.2		Narrow, isolated features planarize faster than wide, isolated or narrow, dense ones (pp. 115, 120). Rotation makes every edge a leading edge; wide features keep a slow centre. Beyond the pad's levelling distance there is no selectivity. Stiff pad: better planarization, worse WIWNU; standard stack IC-1000 on Suba-IV
Two kinds of polishing job (p. 119)
M13 §7.3		(a) Planarize a homogeneous film (ILD) and stop with film left everywhere (the notes write "complete", Wolf "incomplete"). (b) Remove a deposited film from the high regions so it remains only in recesses (damascene); non-selective until the field clears, then dishing, more in wide trenches (p. 120)

Slurries (notes pp. 122, 124–125)

ItemOxide slurryMetal slurry (W, Cu)
Abrasive
M14 §1.3		Fumed or colloidal silica; ceria for STI and ILD; zirconia investigated for low-kAlumina, usually (Lecture 24's Cu slurry: silica, flagged)
SolutionKOH or NH₄OH in DI water; few additives (alkali, buffer)Always an oxidizer (ferric nitrate Fe(NO₃)₃, H₂O₂) and a buffer; may add surfactant, complexing agent, corrosion inhibitor (BTA for Cu), selectivity additives
pH9–11 (ceria 6–7)1–5 W; 3–9 Cu (notes). Lecture 24: about 2–4 W, 3–6 Cu
What the chemistry doesWater hydrates and softens the oxide networkOxidizer turns the metal surface into a softer oxide that passivates the rest
SelectivityCeria: oxide to nitride up to 250:1Metal to oxide > 20:1 (alumina); W to SiO₂ e.g. 120:1
ItemThe course's version
Composition
M14 §1.1		Inorganic-oxide particles 10–100 nm in aqueous solution, roughly as hard as the film (p. 124); reagents: acids, bases, surfactants, buffers, corrosion inhibitors, complexing agents. Lecture 24: particles similar to the film in hardness or harder; chemicals about 99.9 % pure
Four requirements (pp. 122, 124)
M14 §1.1		High removal rate, good planarity, local film uniformity, high selectivity SA:B = RRA/RRB
Stability
M14 §1.1		Agglomerates cause microscratches. Particles of the same zeta-potential sign repel; silica about −30 mV at pH 7, more negative above; alumina positive in acid. Silica slurries are stabilized by raising the pH (e.g. NH₄OH) (Wolf)
Fumed against colloidal silica
M14 §1.2		Fumed: SiCl₄ burnt in a flame, a dry powder to be dispersed. Colloidal: grown in solution from sodium silicate, ion-exchanged; more stable, more uniform, scratches less, replacing fumed for STI, ILD and poly. Solids 5–35 wt%. RR rises with pH, concentration and particle size (Wolf)
Ceria (p. 125)
M14 §1.2		For STI and ILD (also Lecture 24): faster on oxide than silica, at pH 6–7, up to 250:1 oxide to nitride — why it was adopted for STI. Hard to make small; silica sometimes blended in. Softer than silica, not harder (Lecture 24 said harder, flagged); its rate is chemical (Cook)
Metal slurry stability
M14 §1.3		Alumina gives higher metal rates and > 20:1 to oxide but agglomerates; the oxidizer is often kept apart until use (Wolf). One slurry cannot clear both metal and barrier without excessive dishing; production uses one for each
Lecture 24's copper slurry
M14 §1.3		H₂O₂ + glycine (a complexing agent) in water with silica; for Ta, H₂O₂ to Ta₂O₅, then abraded (Wolf: Ta removed mainly by abrasion). Surfactant: lowers surface tension
Handling
M14 §1.3		Automatic feed wets the pad uniformly; dried slurry is very hard to remove, so wafers are rinsed on the pad and moved at once to wet tanks (p. 124)

Metal polishing, tools and pads (notes pp. 121–127)

ItemThe course's version
Kaufman's three steps (p. 121)
M14 §2.1		(1) Passivation: the oxidizer forms a metal oxide on up- and down-areas. (2) Abrasion of the up-areas: the softer oxide is removed where the pad touches. (3) Regrowth on the up-areas; the down-areas stay passivated. Notes' reaction: W + Fe(NO₃)₃ → WO₃ + H₂O, a schematic, unbalanced
The balance
M14 §2.2		Oxide soft enough to abrade, hard and impermeable enough to stop etching; if etching dominates the metal etches isotropically and nothing planarizes. Pourbaix: protective WO₃ below about pH 4, slightly positive potential, so W slurries at pH 2–3. Original IBM ferricyanide (pH 5.0–6.5), toxic, replaced by ferric nitrate, KIO₃ or H₂O₂ (Wolf)
Overpolish and endpoint
M14 §2.3		Every trace of metal must leave the field, so overpolish is required and the field oxide is exposed (p. 122); hence high selectivity. The end of a metal polish gives a clear signal: reflectivity, temperature, friction change (p. 122)
Seven tools and consumables (p. 123)
M14 §3		Polishing tools, slurries, slurry distribution system, pads, endpoint detection systems, cleaning tools, metrology tools
Four polisher configurations (p. 123)
M14 §3.1		Rotary: table and carrier rotate. Orbital: carrier rotates, table orbits. Linear: carrier rotates, belt moves straight. Fixed table: table still, carrier orbits
Carriers (pp. 123–124)
M14 §3.1		Retaining ring keeps the wafer in the pocket at high downforce and speed. Levelling by a compressible backing film, a pneumatic bladder, or a lip-sealed air bearing — uniform pressure, since by Preston any local excess becomes non-uniformity
Pads (p. 126)
M14 §3.2		Polyurethane-impregnated felt: porous, soft, compressible (Suba IV). Cast closed-cell polyurethane with filler: hard, low compressibility (IC-1000, pores about 30 % of volume). Hard pad: within-die planarity; soft: across-wafer uniformity; stacked IC-1000 on Suba IV. Grooves or perforations 1 mm wide, 250 µm deep carry slurry (Wolf)
Glazing and conditioning (p. 126)
M14 §3.3		Roughness and porosity set slurry supply, debris removal and contact area. Glazing: pad smooths plastically and fills its pores; RR falls (Wolf: 210 → 75 nm/min in 50 wafers). Conditioning: a diamond-grit disk swept across the pad reopens pores. Wolf: 80–120 grit in nickel for hard pads, a nylon brush for soft; continuous in-situ conditioning; break-in on dummy wafers. Lectures gave every 1–2 wafers (L21) and every 3–4 (L22); the notes give none
Slurry-free (fixed-abrasive) pads (p. 127)
M14 §3.3		Abrasive in the pad, particle-free solution (basic for dielectrics, oxidizing at pH 2–7 for metals). Benefits: higher DOP; lower cost of ownership; no slurry handling or distribution; no slurry-stability or solution-variation problems

Damascene, dishing and erosion (notes pp. 119–122, 129–130)

ItemThe course's version
Why damascene
M14 §4		Al is patterned subtractively; Cu conducts better but is difficult to plasma-etch, so the pattern is etched in the dielectric, overfilled and polished back. Name: metal inlay of Damascus (Wolf); Lecture 24's drainage-system derivation is unsupported
Tungsten plugs
M14 §4.1		TiN/Ti liner, CVD W (from WF₆), W and liner polished off the field: a single damascene step under etched Al lines. Replaced W etchback; higher yields (Wolf). Lecture 24: Ti forms TiSi₂ at the contact bottom on anneal; contacts are round; in Cu chips the W contacts remain
Barrier, seed and plate (Lectures 23–24)
M14 §4.2		Trench about 1 µm deep. Barrier Ta, TaN, Ti or TiN sputtered, 200–300 Å (Lecture 23; Lecture 24: 100–200 Å): copper adheres poorly to oxide and diffuses fast. Cu seed about 0.1 µm (1000 Å), sputtered. Cu electroplated 1–2 µm. Nitride cap on top
Dual damascene (p. 130)
M14 §4.3		Over a Cu line: Si₃N₄ etch stop / insulator / Si₃N₄ / insulator / top Si₃N₄; via and trench opened, TiN barrier and Cu seed sputtered into both, one plating, one CMP
Dual damascene: five advantages (Wolf)
M14 §4.3		(1) Lower resistivity and better electromigration than W-plug/Al; (2) combines with low-k; (3) one CMP step per level instead of two; (4) copper never etched; (5) one interface per via instead of two. Costs: barrier coverage in high-aspect-ratio features, complex Cu/barrier CMP, electroplating in the fab, low-k integration
Tungsten rates
M14 §5.1		W > 350 nm/min; TiN/Ti liner 3–8 times slower. Three platens: W, liner, oxide buff with silica. A seam in the fill opens into plug coring (Wolf)
Copper rates
M14 §5.2		Cu about 350 nm/min, 150:1 to oxide; Ta about 30 nm/min. Needed: Cu:barrier near 1:1 and Cu:insulator > 100:1, so two or three slurries (bulk Cu, barrier, oxide buff). Galvanic corrosion of trench Cu once barrier and Cu are both exposed (Wolf)
Dishing
M14 §5.3		Thinning of the film left in recesses: the pad sags into a wide recess, so it grows with feature width. Wolf's STI example: fields wider than 100 µm lose more than 50 nm. Lecture 23: appears when the barrier is removed, worse in wide trenches, cannot be prevented, only reduced
Erosion
M14 §5.3		Loss of dielectric thickness during the metal polish; grows with pattern density, not width: dense regions clear first and are overpolished longest (Wolf). Lecture 23 put the density dependence under dishing (flagged)
Remedies (Wolf)
M14 §5.3		Two-slurry process; dummy structures (dummy active areas in wide STI, support features in pads, buses, wide lines); harder pad; relatively non-selective slurry; efficient endpoint; slurry-free pads. Dummy structures complicate design and masks
Cost of the recess
M14 §5.4		R = ρL/A: a dished line is thinner, and millions of lines add up (Lecture 23). The barrier also takes section: it costs narrow lines most; dishing costs wide lines most

Endpoint and cleaning (notes pp. 122, 127–129)

ItemThe course's version
Two kinds of endpoint (p. 127)
M14 §6		Dielectric to a known final thickness (nothing changes at the surface); inlaid metal until it clears and lands on the dielectric (surface material changes). In-line = measured between polishes; real-time in situ = during the polish. In 2002 only metal polishes could be stopped in situ (Wolf)
In-line loop
M14 §6.1		T0 measured over dense features; time from calibration; up to 20 % (1σ) scatter in development. Too thick can be repolished, too thin is scrapped, so underpolish on purpose, measure, repolish; often on a pilot wafer per lot. Costs: measurement time, idle polisher, breakage risk
In-situ methods (p. 128)
M14 §6.2		(1) Front-side laser interferometry or spectroscopy through a clear pad window (Wolf: 45°): intensity oscillates as a transparent film thins; on Cu, reflectance high, drops at the Ta breakthrough; accuracy within 20 nm in development. (2) Back-side optical (infrared through the silicon), up to four sensors per carrier, spots about 0.5 mm. (3) Carrier motor current (shunt or Hall probe) at constant speed: friction changes when a layer clears (W to oxide, Cu to Ta, STI oxide to nitride); useless for oxide-only polishes
Fourth signal (p. 122)
M14 §6.2		Surface temperature change when the metal clears. Aim: clear endpoint signal, then a short overpolish
Why clean (p. 129)
M14 §7		CMP is inherently dirty: slurry particles, pad debris, removed film; metal contaminants and chemicals. Particles block connections, void the next film, corrode metal
Adhesion and removal
M14 §7.1		Van der Waals (strongest), static, capillary. Removal: (1) electrical repulsion by zeta potential; (2) chemical: dissolve, oxidize and dissolve, or undercut (SC-1); (3) mechanical shear: brush, megasonic. Immersion alone fails. Keep wafers wet: 1–2 min in air can make a wafer uncleanable
Cleaning chemistry
M14 §7.1		Silica on oxide: above pH 7. Exposed metal, alumina, or to stop re-adhesion: above pH 8 (alumina is positive below about pH 8). NH₄OH in the brush water cut defects by up to 75 %. About 1:50 chemical to DI water (Wolf)
Brush scrubbing
M14 §7.2		PVA sponge cylinders, both faces at once, drag of the liquid removes particles down to 0.12 µm. Oxide: DI water + NH₄OH, often H₂O₂. Metal: oxide-free, smooth, not attacked; citric acid and HF for metal ions. Particles in recesses remain (Wolf: in a 0.7 µm, 3:1 contact)

RC delay and low-k dielectrics (Lectures 22 and 24; not in the notes)

ItemThe course's version
Definitions
M14 §8		Low-k: k below thermal SiO₂, 3.9; high-k: above Si₃N₄, about 7 (Wolf). SiO₂ 3.9–4.5 by formation; thermal oxide about 2.3 g/cm³
Two levers
M14 §8.1		Materials factor ρk; geometry (l/p)²: halving the pitch quadruples RC. Interconnect delay became significant near 0.5 µm feature sizes (Wolf). Tall lines (A > 1) dominated by neighbour capacitance
Three routes to lower k (Wolf)
M14 §8.2		(1) Lower density (pores; the only route below about 2; weaker films). (2) Less ionization: Si–CH₃ for Si–O. (3) Less polarization: Si–F for Si–O. Generations: 2.8–3.5; 2.5–2.8; ultra-low-k below 2.0
FSG
M14 §8.3		3.9 → 3.3 (Lecture 22; Lecture 24 "around three"). Wolf: minimum about 3.5 with ≤ 6 % F (more is unstable, HF corrodes Al; F attacks Ta), 0.2–0.3 lower in Cu damascene (about 3.2–3.3). IBM at 0.35, 0.25, 0.18 µm
Other lecture values
M14 §8.3		B- and P-doped oxide: perhaps 3.8 (Lecture 22). BN/BCN: about 2.2, the lecturer's group's films (Lecture 22; Lecture 24: 2.2–2.6). Teflon and polymers 2.6–2.8 (Lecture 24; Wolf: Teflon 1.9–2.1, most polymers 2.5–2.9, polyimides 3.0–3.6)
Metal resistivities
M14 §8.3		Lecture 24: Al 2.3, Cu 1.8, Ag 1.2 µΩ·cm. Handbook (CRC, 20 °C): 2.65, 1.68, 1.59; Wolf: Al 2.7, Cu 1.7. Silver's advantage over copper is about 5 %, not a third. Silver rejected for cost and "oxidizing" (its tarnish is a sulfide). Al first because easy to deposit; Cu when RC mattered; Cu capped with nitride
Low-k under CMP
M14 §8.4		Usually capped with oxide or nitride and the cap polished (the cap raises effective k). Problems: adhesion under shear, compressibility (cap cracks, delamination), low thermal conductivity (hot spots); FSG polishes faster. Direct-polish slurries: zirconia (p. 125), manganese oxide (deep scratches 20 → 2 nm)

Sacrificial-layer etchback (notes pp. 110–111; M13 §3.2)

  1. Deposit CVD dielectric over the metal lines.
  2. Spin on SOG or photoresist as a liquid: it fills low regions more than high ones; baked, its surface is nearly flat (> 70 % for features under about 10 µm).
  3. Etch in O₂ or O₂ + CF₄ plasma: fast through the sacrificial layer to the dielectric tops, then at equal rates for the two materials, so the flat profile moves down into the oxide.
  4. Continue until the sacrificial layer is gone; deposit further CVD oxide to restore the thickness over the metal.

Oxide removal cycle (notes p. 120; M13 §6.3)

  1. Plastic deformation: particles held by the pad are pressed into the oxide; more with particle pressure and frictional heat.
  2. Hydration: the deformed oxide takes up water and softens.
  3. Plowing: the sliding particles cut the hydrated layer away; more with pad speed.
  4. Repeat on the fresh oxide (implied, not stated in the notes). Pressure × speed gives Preston's Pv.

Lecture 23's difficulty — how one contact both hydrates and removes — is answered by the two drivers: pressure for step 1, speed for step 3. Lecture 24 defined plowing generically as pad asperities cutting the surface, while the notes and Wolf have the particles plow and the pad grip them (flagged, §6.4).

Metal polishing, Kaufman's model (notes p. 121; M14 §2.1)

  1. Passivation: the oxidizer (Fe(NO₃)₃, H₂O₂) forms a metal oxide on up- and down-areas (W → WO₃).
  2. Abrasion: pad and abrasive remove the softer oxide from the up-areas only, since the pad is only partly flexible.
  3. Regrowth: the slurry re-forms the oxide on the up-areas; the down-areas stay protected. The cycle repeats until the pad reaches the down-areas; then pad hardness and downforce set the planarization.

Tungsten plug (M14 §4.1, §5.1; contact detail from Lecture 24)

  1. Open the contact or via window in the oxide (round, Lecture 24), over n⁺ source or drain for a contact.
  2. Line with Ti and TiN (Wolf: TiN over Ti); anneal: Ti on silicon forms TiSi₂, a low-resistance contact, and does not react on the oxide walls.
  3. Fill with CVD tungsten from WF₆.
  4. CMP the tungsten (first platen, W > 350 nm/min, pH 2–3 slurry).
  5. CMP the TiN/Ti liner off the field (second platen; 3–8 times slower), with overpolish so no Ti or TiN remains.
  6. Oxide buff with silica slurry (third platen): repairs oxide damage and levels the oxide with the recessed plugs.

Copper single damascene (notes p. 129, Lecture 23; M14 §4.2)

  1. Trench: etch the line pattern into the oxide, about 1 µm deep. (Lecture 23: reflow rounds the corners of a doped-glass trench; only possible under the first metal.)
  2. Barrier: sputter Ta, TaN, Ti or TiN over everything, 200–300 Å (Lecture 23; Lecture 24: 100–200 Å) — adhesion, and a diffusion barrier on every side.
  3. Seed: sputter copper, about 0.1 µm — a conducting surface to carry the plating current.
  4. Plate: electroplate copper 1–2 µm, filling and overfilling the trench.
  5. CMP copper off the field (Cu about 350 nm/min, stops on the barrier).
  6. Change slurry; CMP the barrier off the field (near 1:1 Cu:barrier, > 100:1 to oxide); oxide buff as a third step (Wolf).
  7. Cap with silicon nitride, closing the copper in from above (Wolf).

The notes (p. 129) label the liner "seed layer Ta, TiN"; it is the barrier, and the seed is the copper film over it (p. 130; Lecture 23).

Copper dual damascene (notes p. 130; M14 §4.3)

  1. Over the copper line below, deposit Si₃N₄ etch stop, insulator, Si₃N₄ etch stop, insulator, top Si₃N₄.
  2. Two mask-and-etch steps: a narrow via through the lower insulator to the copper below, a wider trench through the upper insulator; the middle nitride sets the trench depth, the bottom one protects the copper until the via is opened.
  3. Sputter a TiN barrier and a copper seed into both openings at once.
  4. Electroplate copper to fill via and trench together.
  5. One CMP removes the copper and barrier overburden.

In-line endpoint loop (notes p. 127; M14 §6.1)

  1. Measure T0 over an area dense in features.
  2. Calculate tf = (T0 − Tf)/RR from calibration data for that pressure, speed and pad state.
  3. Polish a pilot wafer for less than tf (deliberate underpolish: too thin is unrecoverable).
  4. Measure; derive the true rate; polish again for the remainder; repeat until in specification.
  5. Polish the rest of the lot for the time the pilot wafer gave.

6.4 Where the course and the literature differ

Lecture 24 called the during-polish signals "in-line"

Lecture 24 called the signals read during the polish — surface temperature, platen or carrier torque, and a "surface velocity" that the notes give as surface reflectivity — "in-line", and, as the notes do, called the laser through a hole in the pad "real-time". In the notes, in-line is the measure–polish–measure loop between polishes (p. 127), and every signal read during the polish is real-time in-situ detection (p. 128). Answer in the notes' terms (M14 §6.2).

PointThe course's version (use it in answers)The literature or the notes' source§
