6.5 Self-check

Choose one. A process removes the steps between closely spaced metal lines but leaves a step over a wide, isolated pad. Which degree of planarization is it? — (a) smoothing (b) partial planarization (c) complete local planarization (d) complete global planarization

(c). The third degree eliminates steps where the features are close together (Wolf: under about 10 µm apart) while wide, isolated features still show one; global planarization would leave no step anywhere. M13 §2.

Choose one. Copper lines are polished at fixed pattern density, but their width is increased tenfold. The dishing — (a) increases (b) decreases (c) does not change (d) none of the above

(a). The pad sags further into a wider recess and keeps polishing the soft copper in its middle, so dishing grows with width. Erosion, the dielectric loss, depends on pattern density and would not change. M14 §5.3.

Choose one. Which slurry polishes the STI fill oxide down to its nitride with the least nitride loss? — (a) fumed silica in KOH at pH 10 (b) alumina with ferric nitrate at pH 2 (c) ceria at pH 6–7 (d) zirconia

(c). Ceria removes oxide faster than silica and up to 250 times faster than nitride, which is why it was adopted for STI (notes p. 125). Alumina–oxidizer slurries are for metals; zirconia was investigated for low-k. M14 §1.2.

Choose one. In Kaufman's model, what keeps the down-areas of a metal film from being removed? — (a) the pad does not touch them and their passivating oxide blocks the corrosive slurry (b) the abrasive is softer than the metal (c) the slurry pH is above 9 (d) the barrier layer beneath them

(a). The oxidizer passivates both up- and down-areas; the pad, only partly flexible, abrades the oxide from the up-areas alone, and the oxide left on the down-areas keeps the corrosive species away from the metal. M14 §2.1.

Choose one. In a copper damascene polish, which signal falls sharply when the polish breaks through the copper to the tantalum barrier? — (a) carrier motor current (b) the in-line loop's measured thickness (c) surface temperature (d) laser reflectance

(d). Copper reflects strongly and the thin tantalum beneath is semi-transparent, so the reflected laser signal drops at breakthrough; the carrier motor current, at constant speed, rises instead as the tantalum is exposed. The in-line loop is a between-polish measurement, not a signal read during the polish. M14 §6.2.

Choose one. Under Zhao and Shi's law, doubling the downforce at constant pad speed multiplies the oxide removal rate by — (a) 2.00 (b) 1.59 (c) 1.41 (d) 1.00

(b). RR = KpvP2/3, so the factor is 22/3 = 1.59; Preston's linear law would give 2.00. M13 §6.2.

Choose one. After an oxide polish with a silica slurry, the cleaning solution should be — (a) acidic (b) neutral DI water only, by immersion (c) above pH 7 (d) any pH, since brushing does the work

(c). Silica particles and the oxide surface both grow more negative as the pH rises, so above pH 7 they repel and the liquid driven by the PVA brush carries the particles away. Immersion alone does not remove slurry particles. M14 §7.1.

Choose one. Which substitution lowers k by reducing the polarizability of the bond? — (a) Si–CH₃ for Si–O (b) Si–F for Si–O (c) pores replacing solid (d) B₂O₃ in the network

(b). Fluorine holds its valence electrons tightly, so Si–F is less polarizable than Si–O; methyl groups act through reduced ionization and pores through lower density (Wolf). M14 §8.2.

Copper single damascene: how is a line made, step by step, and what is each layer for? Why is the slurry changed partway through the polish?

Copper is difficult to plasma-etch, so the pattern is put in the dielectric instead. (1) A trench about 1 µm deep is etched in the oxide. (2) A barrier of Ta, TaN, Ti or TiN, 200–300 Å (Lecture 23; Lecture 24 said 100–200 Å), is sputtered over everything, trench walls and floor included: copper adheres poorly to oxide, and it diffuses fast and kills devices in trace amounts, so it must be enclosed. (3) A copper seed about 0.1 µm thick is sputtered: the plating needs a conducting surface, and plated copper grows poorly on TiN. (4) Copper is electroplated 1–2 µm, overfilling the trench; sputtering that much is slow and may not fill. (5) CMP removes the copper from the field and stops on the barrier. (6) The slurry is changed, because the copper slurry hardly removes the harder barrier (Cu about 350, Ta about 30 nm/min); left on, it would recess wide lines by tb(SCu:b − 1). The barrier slurry runs near 1:1 copper to barrier and above 100:1 to oxide, and an oxide buff follows (Wolf). (7) A silicon nitride cap seals the copper from above. Dual damascene does the via and trench together over Si₃N₄ etch stops, with one fill and one polish.

Full treatment: M14 §4.2 and §5.2.

Dishing and erosion in copper CMP: what is each, what causes it, what does it cost, and what reduces it? Sketch both.

A damascene polish must run past the point where the copper clears, so that no metal is left on the field to short neighbouring lines, and during that overpolish the oxide is exposed. Dishing is the recess of the copper below the dielectric surface beside it. The pad is not rigid: it sags into a wide recess and presses on its middle, so the soft copper there keeps being removed, and dishing grows with line width. Clearing the barrier with the copper slurry adds a recess tb(SCu:b − 1) in wide lines. Erosion is the loss of dielectric thickness during the metal polish: where the pattern is dense, the copper carries more of the pad's load, clears first and is overpolished longest, so erosion grows with pattern density, not width (Wolf; Lecture 23 placed the density dependence under dishing). The metal left is tm = h − (D + E), and since R = ρL/A, a thinner line has a higher resistance, summed over millions of lines (Lecture 23). Dishing cannot be prevented, only reduced: a two-slurry process (barrier slurry near 1:1), dummy structures that break up wide areas, a harder pad, a relatively non-selective slurry, efficient endpoint detection, slurry-free pads.

Full treatment: M14 §5.3 and §5.4.

How does CMP remove an oxide film, how does the mechanism account for Preston's law, and how does polishing a metal differ?

An oxide film is already oxidized, and the silica abrasive in a KOH or NH₄OH slurry is about as hard as SiO₂, so it cannot cut unreacted oxide; water makes removal possible. The notes give the sequence (p. 120): abrasive particles held by the pad are pressed into the oxide and deform it plastically, more with particle pressure and frictional heat; the deformed oxide takes up water and forms a soft hydrated layer; the particles sliding with the pad plow that layer away, more with pad speed; fresh oxide is exposed and the cycle repeats. SiO₂ dissolution, faster at higher pH, is a co-factor. Pressure sets how much oxide each contact prepares, speed how often it is plowed off, so the rate grows as Pv: Preston's RR = KpPv (1927), empirical, with the sub-linear P2/3 of Zhao and Shi as a refinement. A metal cannot be hydrated: in Kaufman's model an oxidizer converts the surface into a softer metal oxide everywhere, the pad abrades it only from the up-areas, and it regrows there while the oxide on the down-areas protects them. The oxide must be soft enough to abrade but dense enough to stop etching, which fixes the pH (W: pH 2–3, where WO₃ is stable); the larger chemical part gives metal slurries high selectivity to oxide (W:SiO₂ 120:1).

Full treatment: M13 §6.3 and M14 §2.

Multilevel interconnect: what problem does the topography cause, why did reflow and etchback not solve it, and what does CMP do differently?

CVD dielectrics are nearly conformal, so each patterned level adds its thickness to the step beneath: the notes' stack of 0.3, 0.4, 0.5 and 1.0 µm gives steps of 0.3, 0.7, 1.2 and 2.2 µm, and metal-2 is printed over 1.2 µm. A rough surface fails three ways (p. 108): metal thins on high, steep steps (step coverage); stringers survive an anisotropic etch at the foot of steps, where the film is h1 + h2 thick vertically; and the surface exceeds the ±0.5 µm depth of focus of submicron lithography, the condition that makes planarization mandatory. Doped-glass reflow (800–900 °C flow, so pre-metal only), sacrificial resist or SOG etchback (equal etch rates in O₂/CF₄; voids above aspect ratio 0.4, so not below 1.25 µm spacing) and spin-on glass all rely on a liquid levelling itself, which flattens narrow gaps but follows wide ones: local planarization below about 10 µm, inadequate beyond three metal levels. CMP removes the highest points first, because the compliant pad presses hardest on the up-areas, and reaches β = 0.90–0.95 over several millimetres: global planarization.

Full treatment: M13 §1, §3 and §7.1.

Cumulative steps. A process has a field-oxide step of 0.25 µm, polysilicon 0.30 µm, metal-1 0.60 µm and metal-2 0.90 µm, all conformal and unplanarized (illustrative thicknesses). Find the step after each layer, and the step under each metal level when it is patterned, against ±0.5 µm.

Running sum. t¹ = 0.25 µm; t² = 0.25 + 0.30 = 0.55 µm; t³ = 0.55 + 0.60 = 1.15 µm; t⁴ = 1.15 + 0.90 = 2.05 µm.

Under each metal. Metal-1 is patterned over t² = 0.55 µm, 0.05 µm beyond the allowance; metal-2 over t³ = 1.15 µm, 2.3 times the 0.5 µm allowance.

0.25, 0.55, 1.15, 2.05 µm; metal-1 over 0.55 µm and metal-2 over 1.15 µm, both beyond ±0.5 µm

Check. The last step equals the sum of the four thicknesses, 2.05 µm, as it must with no planarization.

Method: M13 §1.1.

Stringer overetch. A 0.6 µm metal film is deposited conformally over a 0.3 µm step and etched anisotropically (illustrative values). Find the vertical thickness at the foot of the step and the overetch, as a percentage of the nominal etch time, that clears it.

Vertical thickness. h1 + h2 = 0.3 + 0.6 = 0.9 µm.

Overetch. The nominal etch clears 0.6 µm; the extra 0.3 µm takes 0.3/0.6 = 0.50 of the nominal time.

0.9 µm at the foot; 50 % overetch

Check. The same 0.9 µm as the module's case (0.4 + 0.5 µm) needs less overetch here because the film is thicker relative to the step: the overetch is h1/h2.

Method: M13 §1.2.

Planarization factor. An IMD step falls from 1.1 µm to 0.08 µm during CMP (illustrative values). Find β and the DOP, and the step that the same β would leave of the 1.15 µm step under metal-2 in the cumulative-steps problem above.

Ratio. tf/ti = 0.08/1.1 = 0.0727.

Factor. β = 1 − 0.0727 = 0.927; DOP = 100β = 92.7 %.

Residual step. (1 − β) × 1.15 = 0.0727 × 1.15 = 0.084 µm.

β = 0.927 (DOP 92.7 %); 0.084 µm left of 1.15 µm

Check. 0.927 lies inside CMP's 0.90–0.95, and 0.084 µm is well within ±0.5 µm.

Method: M13 §2; formula sheet (13.2), (13.4).

Removal rate. A blanket oxide is 1100 nm thick before and 640 nm after 4.0 min of polishing (illustrative values). Find RR in nm/min and Å/min, compare it with Wolf's 100–140 nm/min goal, and estimate the time to remove 500 nm.

Thickness removed. ΔT = 1100 − 640 = 460 nm.

Rate. RR = 460/4.0 = 115 nm/min = 1150 Å/min.

Time for 500 nm. 500/115 = 4.35 min.

RR = 115 nm/min (1150 Å/min), inside 100–140 nm/min; 500 nm in about 4.35 min

Check. 115 nm/min × 4.0 min = 460 nm. On a patterned wafer up-areas polish faster than this blanket rate.

Method: M13 §5.1; formula sheet (13.3).

Within-wafer non-uniformity. Post-CMP oxide thicknesses at five sites on one wafer are 402, 395, 410, 388 and 405 nm (illustrative values). Find the WIWNU and compare it with ±2–5 % (1σ).

Mean. (402 + 395 + 410 + 388 + 405)/5 = 2000/5 = 400 nm.

Deviations and squares. +2, −5, +10, −12, +5 nm; squares 4, 25, 100, 144, 25 nm²; sum 298 nm².

Standard deviation. σ = √(298/4) = √74.5 = 8.63 nm (with n = 5 instead, 7.72 nm: the notes' form uses n − 1).

Non-uniformity. 8.63/400 = 0.0216 = 2.16 %.

WIWNU = 2.16 % (1σ), within the 2–5 % range

Check. The readings span −3.0 % to +2.5 % about the mean; a σ of about 2 % fits that spread.

Method: M13 §5.3; formula sheet (13.5).

Oxide to deposit. Metal lines are 0.6 µm high; 0.6 µm of oxide must remain over them; the CMP process has %EOP = 70 % (illustrative values). Find the oxide to deposit, Tr and Td, and the deposit at 100 %.

Deposit. Ttotal = 0.6/0.70 + 0.6 = 0.857 + 0.6 = 1.457 µm.

Removals. Tr = 1.457 − 0.6 = 0.857 µm; Td = 0.857 − 0.6 = 0.257 µm. By (13.6), (0.857 − 0.257)/0.857 × 100 = 70 %, as assumed.

Ideal. At 100 %, Ttotal = 0.6 + 0.6 = 1.2 µm and Td = 0.

1.46 µm to deposit; Tr = 0.857 µm, Td = 0.257 µm; 1.2 µm at 100 %

Check. The excess over the ideal deposit, 0.257 µm, equals Td: every nanometre lost in the spaces had to be deposited and polished away.

Method: M13 §5.4; formula sheet (13.6), (13.7).

Preston's constant. An oxide polishes at 110 nm/min at 6 psi and 40 ft/min (illustrative values). Find Kp in Pa⁻¹, and the rate Preston's law predicts at 8 psi and 45 ft/min.

Pressure. 6 × 6894.76 = 4.137×10⁴ Pa.

Velocity. 40 × 0.3048 m / 60 s = 0.2032 m/s.

Rate. 110×10⁻⁹ m / 60 s = 1.833×10⁻⁹ m/s.

Preston's constant. Kp = RR/(Pv) = 1.833×10⁻⁹/(4.137×10⁴ × 0.2032) = 2.18×10⁻¹³ Pa⁻¹.

New conditions. RR scales with Pv: (8 × 45)/(6 × 40) = 360/240 = 1.50, so RR = 1.50 × 110 = 165 nm/min.

Kp = 2.18×10⁻¹³ Pa⁻¹; 165 nm/min at 8 psi, 45 ft/min

Check. Kp is close to the module's 2.33×10⁻¹³ Pa⁻¹, as expected for a similar oxide process. Under Zhao and Shi's P2/3 the same change gives × 1.36, 150 nm/min.

Method: M13 §6.1; formula sheet (13.8).

Overpolish loss. A tungsten slurry removes W at 400 nm/min with a selectivity of 80:1 to SiO₂; the overpolish runs 40 s after the field clears (illustrative values). Find the oxide removed.

Oxide rate. RRox = RRW/S = 400/80 = 5.00 nm/min.

Time. 40 s = 0.667 min.

Oxide removed. 5.00 × 0.667 = 3.33 nm.

3.33 nm of oxide

Check. The loss scales as time over selectivity; a 120:1 slurry at the same rate would remove two-thirds as much.

Method: M14 §2.3; formula sheet (14.1).

Barrier-step recess. A 20 nm tantalum barrier is cleared with the copper slurry (Cu 350, Ta 30 nm/min, Wolf's rates; the barrier thickness is illustrative). Find the copper removed from a wide line and the recess it leaves, and the recess with a 1:1 barrier slurry.

Time to clear the barrier. 20/30 = 0.667 min = 40.0 s.

Copper removed. 350 × 0.667 = 233 nm; equivalently 20 × (350/30) = 20 × 11.67.

Recess. Db = 233 − 20 = 20 × (11.67 − 1) = 213 nm.

Barrier slurry, 1:1. 20 nm of copper removed, the same as the field: no recess.

233 nm of copper removed, 213 nm recess; none with a 1:1 slurry

Check. At fixed selectivity the recess is proportional to tb: the module's 25 nm barrier leaves 267 nm, and 20 nm leaves 20/25 of that.

Method: M14 §5.3; formula sheet (14.3).

Line resistance and dishing. A copper line 2 mm long sits in a trench 3.0 µm wide and 0.6 µm deep with a 15 nm barrier; ρCu = 1.7 µΩ·cm. Find R undished, with a mean dishing of 0.06 µm, and for an all-copper section (geometry and dishing illustrative).

Prefactor. ρL = 1.7×10⁻⁶ Ω·cm × 0.2 cm = 3.4×10⁻⁷ Ω·cm².

Undished. Copper section (3.0 − 0.03) × (0.6 − 0.015) = 2.97 × 0.585 = 1.737 µm² = 1.737×10⁻⁸ cm²; R = 3.4×10⁻⁷/1.737×10⁻⁸ = 19.6 Ω.

Dished. Height 0.585 − 0.06 = 0.525 µm; section 2.97 × 0.525 = 1.559 µm²; R = 21.8 Ω. The width cancels in the ratio: 0.585/0.525 = 1.114, +11.4 %.

All copper. 3.0 × 0.6 = 1.8 µm²; R = 18.9 Ω: the barrier takes 3.5 % of the section and raises R by 3.6 %.

19.6 Ω undished; 21.8 Ω dished (+11.4 %); 18.9 Ω all-copper

Check. A 10 % loss of copper height raises R by about 11 %, as found; the barrier's share is small because the line is wide.

Method: M14 §5.4; formula sheet (14.4).

Materials and RC. In the module's geometry (1 mm line, pitch 0.5 µm, A = 2, d = t), compare Al in SiO₂ (2.7 µΩ·cm, k 3.9) with Cu in a polymer at k = 2.6 (the low end of Lecture 24's 2.6–2.8) and with Cu in SiO₂. Then compare the Cu/Al and Ag/Cu ratios from Lecture 24's resistivities and from the handbook's.

Materials factors. Al/SiO₂: 2.7 × 3.9 = 10.53; Cu at 2.6: 1.7 × 2.6 = 4.42; ratio 4.42/10.53 = 0.42. Cu/SiO₂ against Al/SiO₂: 1.7/2.7 = 0.63.

Delays by (14.8). 37.3 ps (Al/SiO₂), 23.5 ps (Cu/SiO₂), 15.7 ps (Cu, k 2.6).

Lecture 24's resistivities. Cu/Al = 1.8/2.3 = 0.783 (Wolf's 1.7/2.7 = 0.63); Ag/Cu = 1.2/1.8 = 0.667, a 33 % gain, against the handbook's 1.59/1.68 = 0.946, a 5.4 % gain.

Cu in k 2.6 has 0.42 of the Al/SiO₂ delay (15.7 against 37.3 ps); Cu alone 0.63. Silver would cut ρ by 33 % on the lecture's values but about 5 % on the handbook's

Check. Each delay ratio equals the ρk ratio, since the geometry is unchanged: 15.7/37.3 = 0.42.

Method: M14 §8.1, §8.3; formula sheet (14.8).

In-line endpoint. A pilot wafer measures T0 = 1.10 µm over dense features; target Tf = 0.70 µm; calibrated rate 140 nm/min; first polish at 85 % of the calculated time; then it measures 0.77 µm (illustrative values). Find the first time, the true rate, the second time, and the time for the rest of the lot.

Nominal time. (1.10 − 0.70) µm = 400 nm; 400/140 = 2.857 min (171 s).

First polish, 85 %. 0.85 × 2.857 = 2.429 min (146 s).

True rate. Removed 1100 − 770 = 330 nm in 2.429 min: 330/2.429 = 136 nm/min, 2.9 % below calibration.

Second polish. 770 − 700 = 70 nm; 70/135.9 = 0.515 min = 30.9 s.

Rest of the lot. 400/135.9 = 2.94 min (177 s).

2.43 min, then 30.9 s; true RR 136 nm/min; the lot is polished for 2.94 min

Check. Had the full 2.857 min been run at the true rate, the film would have been left at 0.712 µm, still thick of target and recoverable; the underpolish protects against a pad faster than calibration.

Method: M14 §6.1; formula sheet (14.5).

7 · Chapter 9: shallow trench isolation

Chapter 9 of the notes (pp. 131–146) replaces the isolation that Chapter 5 built. LOCOS grows its field oxide out of the substrate, so oxidation kinetics, not the mask, set its shape: a bird's beak that takes active width in proportion to the oxide thickness, a step of about half that thickness, thinning in narrow openings and stress defects. Etching the isolation space and filling it with deposited oxide removes the first three and reduces the fourth, but the first such process, BOX, failed on voids, planarity and the trench corner, and STI became practical only with three enabling processes: dry trench etching, gap-filling CVD and CMP. The chapter then follows the new problems the trench brings — a sidewall that a neighbouring well can invert, a top corner that crowds the gate field and recesses into a divot, a fill that voids, and a polish that dishes wide trenches. M15 teaches it from the notes and Wolf's Chapter 9, which the notes follow nearly sentence by sentence; the chapter had not been lectured when M15 was written, beyond the flow Lecture 17 drew as a preview and the ceria remark of Lecture 24. The trench depth is the field threshold of Chapter 5 (M08 eq. 8.6) with the trench as the field oxide, the wells of the baseline flow are the implanted wells of M08 §4, and the polish is the CMP of Chapter 8 (M13, M14).

7.1 Facts and lists

LOCOS and its four limitations (notes p. 131)

ItemThe course's version
LOCOS process
M15 §1.1		Thin pad oxide, silicon nitride patterned over the future active areas, oxidize; nitride is nearly impermeable to the oxidant, so oxide grows only where it was removed. Plummer's example: about 500 nm in H₂O at 1000 °C for 90 min; nitride stripped in hot phosphoric acid (M15, after Plummer)
Silicon consumed
M15 §1.1		0.46 of the oxide thickness below the original surface, 0.54 above: the field oxide is semi-recessed and the active areas sit at the bottom of a step (course ratio; Plummer 0.45, texts 0.44–0.46)
1. Bird's beak
M15 §1.2		"Penetrates the active regions." Oxidant diffuses sideways through the pad oxide and oxidizes under the nitride edge, which the expanding oxide lifts: a tapered wedge (Lecture 16: "formed due to lateral penetration of oxidation"). Active width is narrower than drawn by twice the beak. Encroachment ∝ field-oxide thickness, a fraction of a micrometre for the 0.3–0.5 µm field oxides of submicron LOCOS (Wolf)
2. Non-planar surface
M15 §1.1		Step ≈ 0.54 tox above the active silicon: 270 nm for 500 nm, the first level of M13 §1's topography. CMP reduces the topography but does not remove the encroachment (notes; Wolf)
3. Thinning at narrow spacings
M15 §1.3		A field oxide grown in a narrow opening is thinner than in a wide one, and the charge terms of the field threshold scale with the thickness, so the narrowest spaces leak first. No mechanism in the notes or Wolf; the reporting paper (Mizuno 1987) gives reduced oxidant supply, with compressive stress a proposed second contributor
4. Stress-induced defects
M15 §1.3		Stress between nitride and silicon during the long field oxidation
Shortening the beak
M15 §1.2		Thinner pad oxide (narrower diffusion path) and thicker nitride (resists lifting); both raise the stress. Poly-buffered LOCOS: about 200 nm nitride over about 20 nm oxide, against about 80 nm over about 40 nm in plain LOCOS; the polysilicon absorbs the stress (Plummer)
Channel-stop encroachment
M15 §1.3		A fifth cost (Wolf): channel-stop boron diffuses sideways into the active edge during the field oxidation; with the beak it raises the threshold of a narrow transistor (narrow-channel effect)
Why not grow it thinner
M15 §1.4		The thickness sets the field threshold (M08 §3), so the encroachment stays near a fixed fraction of a micrometre while the printable active width shrinks

What a deposited-oxide trench gains (Wolf pp. 433–434; M15 §1.4)

ItemThe course's version
Six gains
M15 §1.4		(1) No bird's beak and less channel-stop redistribution: smaller spacing. (2) Fully recessed field oxide: completely planar surface. (3) No thinning in narrow spaces (deposited, not grown). (4) VT constant with width: "the narrow-channel effect found in LOCOS" eliminated. (5) Larger transconductance, smaller subthreshold swing. (6) Lower, tighter (rounded) corners where the field lines spread, which raises the corner threshold: the "corner effect"
Price
M15 §1.4		More steps than LOCOS: the baseline has twenty-one
Generations
M15 §1.4		LOCOS the workhorse to 0.35 µm. STI in production in the 166 MHz Pentium (0.35 µm) and PowerPC 603e (0.45 µm); standard for CMOS at about 0.25 µm (notes p. 134; Wolf). Lecture 16: LOCOS no longer used in current technologies

Trench isolation before STI: BOX, its drawbacks and the enablers (notes pp. 131–134)

ItemThe course's version
Four uses of non-LOCOS isolation
M15 §2		(1) Replacing LOCOS between like devices in the same well of a CMOS chip; (2) isolating bipolar transistors; (3) isolating n-channel from p-channel and preventing latch-up in CMOS; (4) the trench capacitor of a DRAM cell (M07b)
BOX
M15 §2.1		Buried-oxide isolation: shallow trenches refilled with CVD SiO₂, etched back to a planar surface. BOX-I 1981, BOX-II 1983 (Wolf). Trenches 0.5–0.8 µm deep, walls ~60°, 20–50 nm passivating thermal oxide; flow in 7.2
Drawback 1. Voids
M15 §2.2		CVD oxide in 0.5 µm deep trenches narrower than about 2 µm left voids
Drawback 2. Sidewall inversion
M15 §2.2		Sidewalls of p-type active regions could still invert, especially with vertical walls; boron segregating into the passivating oxide lowered the sidewall doping (Wolf)
Drawback 3. Non-uniform field oxide
M15 §2.2		The profile after the planarization etch varied from area to area
Drawback 4. Imperfect planarization
M15 §2.2		Even two resist layers were not planar over a wide range of active sizes and spacings: poor global planarity
Drawback 5. Gate-oxide thinning at the edge
M15 §2.2		Degrades reliability, raises the field at the corner, lowers the corner threshold (notes p. 133)
Drawback 6. The overetch
M15 §2.2		About 100 nm, to clear every active area: the field oxide ends below the active surface and exposes the sidewall, so the gate wraps the corner (notes p. 133)
Why BOX did not displace LOCOS
M15 §2.2		Above all the complex processing needed for adequately planar surfaces (Wolf). Notes p. 131 add that STI "is less effective in preventing latchup and isolating n-channel from p-channel in CMOS devices" (no reason given; M15 suggests the wells continue below a shallow trench)
Enabler 1. Dry etching
M15 §2.3		Plasma etches replaced wet ones: controlled slope, rounded bottom corners, easier fill
Enabler 2. Gap-filling CVD
M15 §2.3		LPCVD TEOS (Si(OC₂H₅)₄), ozone–TEOS CVD at atmospheric pressure (notes p. 133; the fill step, p. 137, says sub-atmospheric, and Wolf p. 452 writes "(sub)atmospheric": one process family), HDP-CVD; trench fill thicker than an IMD, so its stress must be lower: < 200 MPa
Enabler 3. CMP
M15 §2.3		Developed at IBM in the late 1980s, introduced to the industry in the early 1990s; removes the oxide from the active areas, leaves a planar surface
Optimum STI structure
M15 §2.3		A gate that runs flat across the isolation: a planar surface before poly deposition, given by an RIE etchback followed by CMP (notes p. 134)

Values of the baseline flow (notes pp. 135–139; flow itself in 7.2)

ItemThe course's version
Pad oxide
M15 §3.1		10–20 nm, thermal; buffers nitride stress. No long field oxidation, so about 10 nm against LOCOS's 25–40 nm (Wolf). Trenches are the first structures on the bare wafer; wells later
Nitride
M15 §3.1		LPCVD Si₃N₄ 100–200 nm. Two roles: trench-etch hard mask and CMP polish stop. Batch furnace, so also on the wafer back (removed at step 16)
Trench etch
M15 §3.1		Four goals: depth; slope 70–85° (node, depth, circuit); rounded bottom corners (less stress in liner and fill); smooth, low-damage walls. HBr/Cl₂/O₂ or HBr/NF₃/He/O₂ (better rounding, Wolf). Hard to control: ARDE (wide trenches etch faster) and no endpoint (blind, timed)
Liner
M15 §3.2		Dry O₂, 40–50 nm; best rounding by RTO at 1050–1100 °C. Three roles: consumes etch damage; passivates with the most stable interface (keeps parasitic thresholds high and stable); rounds the top corner. Converts a thin layer of the nitride surface to oxide. DRAMs may add a thin CVD nitride liner against leakage (notes p. 137)
Fill
M15 §3.3		LPCVD TEOS, SACVD ozone–TEOS or HDP-CVD; blanket: 8000–9000 Å deposited for a 5000 Å trench
Densification
M15 §3.3		About 900 °C, so the fill etches in HF near the thermal-oxide rate. Wolf's text: about 20 % lower wet-etch rate; his Table 9-1 (850 °C): SACVD 5 → 1.3, LPCVD TEOS 2.1 → 1.1, HDP 1.1 unchanged
CMP selectivity (oxide:nitride)
M15 §3.3		About 5:1 for every fill in Wolf's Table 9-1; ceria reaches 250:1 (M14 §1.2). Lecture 24: ceria "can be used for the shallow trench isolation and sometimes also the ILD"
Nitride strip
M15 §3.4		Short HF dip (removes the converted oxide layer), then hot H₃PO₄. Wet because cheap, selective to pad oxide and silicon, and removes the backside nitride at once
Sacrificial oxide
M15 §3.4		About 20 nm: protects the surface during the implants, and consumes silicon nitrided at the active edge (O₂ in the densify ambient + H → H₂O; H₂O + nitride → NH₃; Kooi-like nitriding thins the gate oxide locally; Wolf)
HF dips
M15 §3.4		Steps 15, 17 and 20 also etch the trench fill; little lost as a whole, the significant effect is at the top corner (Wolf): the divot
Wells
M15 §3.4		High-energy implants after the trench: wells, channel stop under the trench, VT-adjust and punch-through implants together; no long drive-in (M08 §4.4)
Final step height
M15 §3.4		Trench oxide ends about 100 nm above the active silicon with a 150 nm nitride, about 50 nm with 100 nm (M15's estimates, upper estimates): the nitride thickness is the main lever

Trench depth (notes p. 139; Wolf pp. 455–457)

ItemThe course's version
Depth
M15 §4		Baseline about 0.5 µm (Wolf's range 0.3–0.5 µm); near the 0.1 µm node expected to "decrease to 0.2 µm" (notes); "< 0.2-µm, at least for some circuit applications" (Wolf)
Two considerations
M15 §4		(1) The field threshold: trench depth = tFOX of M08 eq. 8.6, with the channel stop under it. (2) The capacitance of every poly and M1 line over the field, ∝ 1/depth. Both relax as the supply falls. Third limit (Wolf): severity of dishing
Sidewall angle
M15 §4		Steepens from 70–85° to as much as 88°; steep walls at low pressure, high ion flux, non-depositing chemistries such as Cl₂/Ar (Wolf)
Contamination
M15 §4		Metallic contamination from the etch chamber; one tool maker replaced ceramic chamber parts with quartz (Wolf)

Parasitic devices at the trench (notes p. 134; Wolf pp. 436–446)

ItemThe course's version
Deep trench for CMOS
M15 §5.1		At the n-well/p-well boundary, to shrink n⁺-to-p⁺ spacing; stops the wells' dopants compensating. Deeper than the wells, it replaces the junction at the well sidewall. Considerably more complex than STI, latch-up results below expectations: not widely adopted
Sidewall MOSFET
M15 §5.2		Notes p. 134: a horizontal parasitic MOS device; the positively biased n-well is the gate, the trench oxide the gate oxide, its thickness = the trench width; the p-type sidewall across the trench is the channel. Inverted, it joins source and drain of an nMOSFET at the trench edge regardless of its gate, or joins an n⁺ source to the n-well
Wolf's simulation conditions
M15 §5.2		Sidewall NA 9×10¹⁴ cm⁻³; interface charge 5×10¹⁰ cm⁻²; n-well +3 V; n⁺ 0 V; substrate −1 V. Inversion "can easily occur" when the trench is "narrow (e.g., < 1-µm)"; by eq. 15.3 the sidewall holds 3 V only above about 0.78 µm (n-well 10¹⁷ cm⁻³, M15's illustrative value)
Why p-wells are worse
M15 §5.2		(1) Interface charge is positive: lowers the inversion threshold of p-type silicon. (2) Growing the liner that keeps it low draws boron into the oxide by segregation (M08 §3.2), lowering NA. An n-well sidewall is accumulated by the charge and its phosphorus piles up
Remedies, in Wolf's order
M15 §5.2		(1) Channel-stop doping between devices and well edge. (2) Move devices back, widen the trench (cost: spacing). (3) Low-tilt boron implant at 8°, four times rotated 90° (like devices only). (4) Davari: boron from p⁺ poly or BSG patterned off the n-well wall; trenches to 0.8 µm wide, 0.35 µm process. (5) At 0.25 µm and below, wells implanted after STI: no boron during the liner oxidation, channel stop at the trench bottom, DIBL channel boron keeps walls from inverting. Minimum trench in a 0.18 µm process about 0.4 µm
Corner device
M15 §5.3		Gate over the top corner of the mesa: fields from top and side converge, the corner inverts first, a narrow lower-VT transistor in parallel. A "hump" or "kink" in the subthreshold characteristic, more off-state leakage. Worse with thinned corner gate oxide and a recessed field oxide (wrap-around)
Corner radius
M15 §5.3		50 nm, with a sidewall slope no more than 80°, removes the double hump (device simulations, Wolf p. 451)

Corner rounding, the divot and RELACS (notes pp. 140, 144–146)

ItemThe course's version
Baseline rounding
M15 §6.1		The liner: RTO in dry O₂ at 1050–1100 °C (notes p. 140). Pad-oxide undercut first (step 7) exposes more of the corner
Why high temperature
M15 §6.1		Kao (in Plummer): oxidation retarded at sharp corners, a factor two in thickness for nominally 500 nm oxides, much stronger at low temperature, virtually none at 1200 °C. Mechanisms: range of orientations, 2-D oxidant diffusion, above all compressive stress; at high temperature viscous flow relaxes it
Other rounding methods
M15 §6.1		HCl added to the dry-O₂ RTO; post-CMP oxidation in dry O₂ at 1050–1100 °C (rounds well, reduces active area); "mini-LOCOS" edge (complex; Wolf only); H₂ anneal after the etch, surface migration, radii up to 60 nm (Wolf text 1100 °C; Fig. 9-18 caption 950 °C, 380 Torr, 60 s)
Divot
M15 §6.2		Recess in the trench oxide at the active edge, left by the HF dips of steps 17 and 20 (pad and sacrificial oxide strips) (notes p. 144)
Divot: four effects (notes pp. 144–145)
M15 §6.2		(1) Gate wraps the corner: more subthreshold leakage (the hump). (2) Behaves like a recessed isolation: enhances the reverse narrow-width effect, VT falling as the channel narrows. (3) VT falls as the divot deepens; together, unacceptable variations of ±200 mV. (4) The rounded edge encroaches under the nitride: electrical width lost, a large fraction near 0.1 µm (41.4 nm for a 45 nm liner: 16.6 % of 250 nm, 41.4 % of 100 nm, M15's estimate)
RELACS
M15 §6.3		A resist-shrink technique from Clariant-AZ (Wolf); rounds the corner during the trench etch, after the nitride is opened, before the trench. Result (Wolf): no encroachment under the nitride edge, less poly wrap-around, less wafer-to-wafer VT variation; top-corner radius about 15 nm on 180 and 100 nm trenches (Fig. 9-32). Reverse narrow-channel effect is worse if the trench oxide encroaches beneath the nitride edge (notes p. 146)

Filling the trench (notes pp. 140–142)

ItemThe course's version
Why void-free
M15 §7		A void opened by a later HF dip becomes a seam that polysilicon fills and shorts along (Wolf p. 459)
Five criteria (notes p. 140)
M15 §7.1		(1) Void-free, so HF deglazing opens no seams for poly filaments. (2) Known HF etch rate relative to thermal oxide (deglazing thins the fill, can expose the corners). (3) Stress, shrinkage, composition after densification suit the integration. (4) CMP rate relative to nitride and its uniformity: oxide left in the trench, so the "corner effect". (5) p- and n-channel diode isolation tested electrically
Four candidates (Nag)
M15 §7.1		LPCVD TEOS, HDP-CVD, SACVD ozone–TEOS, HSQ spin-on glass. HDP most suitable: no high-temperature anneal; shrinkage 0–0.8 % at 850 °C (SOG 22–28 %); HF rate 1.1 × thermal as deposited (SACVD 5, LPCVD TEOS 2.1); all 1.1–1.3 once densified. Trenches 0.5 µm deep, 0.22 µm wide filled void-free; two-step SACVD BPSG later filled 0.06 µm wide, 0.35 µm deep
Void mechanism
M15 §7.2		An overhang grows at the trench top and closes before the trench is full (notes p. 141). Cause (Plummer): the top corner's wider view angle receives more reactant. Aspect ratio (depth/width): BOX limit 0.25, Nag's trench 2.27, SACVD BPSG 5.8
HDP-CVD
M15 §7.2		SiH₄, O₂ and Ar (or He); high-density source, wafer bias. O₂ + SiH₄ deposit SiO₂ while Ar⁺ sputters: yield highest at about 45° to the normal, so net deposition higher on the flat field and trench bottom than on the 45° corner facets; the deposition-to-etch ratio keeps the mouth open, fill bottom-up; sputtered bottom material redeposits on the walls
HDP conditions and limits
M15 §7.2		Below 10 mTorr (collisionless sheath: full energy, near-normal ions). Ion heating caps the tolerable sputter rate, hence the deposition rate. Notes' "deposition-to-etch ratio… between 0.14 and 0.33" (read by M15 as etch-to-deposition; see 7.4). Sputtering can clip the nitride corners: He instead of Ar, or Wolf's "decrease the deposition-to-(sputter)-etching ratio" at a cost in gap fill. Ridges and spikes left for CMP

Dishing in the STI polish (notes pp. 143–144)

ItemThe course's version
Cause
M15 §8		The pad is not perfectly rigid, and oxide is removed faster than nitride (notes p. 143): once the nitride is exposed the pad flexes into a wide trench and polishes the oxide concave
Size
M15 §8		Grows with field width: more than 50 nm of field-oxide thinning wherever wider than 100 µm (one process; notes, Wolf Fig. 9-24). Stop as soon as the oxide clears; quality judged by control of trench-oxide and nitride thickness
Remedy 1. RIE/CMP
M15 §8		Reverse-mask etchback first: shorter polish, less dishing, higher throughput; cost: complexity
Remedy 2. Nitride cap
M15 §8		Thin blanket nitride on the fill, low-selectivity slurry: polished off the raised oxide, survives over the recessed trenches as a stop
Remedy 3. Dummy active regions
M15 §8		Unetched silicon islands make wide trenches effectively narrow; also needed around isolated actives (dense regions polish slower). Complicate design and masks, raise defects, unusable in analog or mixed-signal (variable coupling capacitance)
Remedy 4. Patterned nitride cap
M15 §8		As 2, removed from the actives with a reverse block mask: cap only over the trenches
Remedy 5. Direct polish
M15 §8		None of the above: a linear tool, or rotary with a harder pad, efficient endpoint and (Wolf) a relatively non-selective slurry; or a fixed-abrasive pad. Linear: 300–500 ft/min, 3–5 psi, against rotary 34 ft/min, 7 psi; Wolf's linear process used ceria with a nitride-inhibiting additive, ≤ 20 nm oxide removed across a die
MIT test wafer
M15 §8		Density-and-pitch wafer for STI dishing: 4800 Å trenches, 150 Å liner, 1960 Å nitride, 8000 Å TEOS (Wolf)

LOCOS and STI side by side (M15 §9)

FeatureLOCOSSTI
Field oxidegrown thermally, semi-recessed (0.46 below, 0.54 above)deposited (HDP-CVD) over a 40–50 nm thermal liner, fully recessed
Edge of the field oxidebird's beak, a fraction of a micrometre, ∝ oxide thicknessnone; a sharp corner that must be rounded
Surface after isolationa step of about half the oxide thicknessplanar after CMP; trench oxide about 50–100 nm above the active surface (M15 §3.4 example)
Narrow spacingsfield oxide thins; narrow-channel effect raises VTno thinning; reverse narrow-width effect if the corner is sharp or recessed
Pad oxide / nitride25–40 nm / ~80 nm (Plummer: ~40 / ~80)10–20 nm / 100–200 nm
Wellsusually before the field oxidation (diffused); can be implanted afterimplanted after the trench (0.25 µm and below)
Parasitic devicesfield transistor under linesfield transistor, sidewall MOSFET, corner device
Processfewer stepsmore steps, three of them enabling technologies (dry etch, gap fill, CMP)
Generationsuntil 0.35 µmfrom 0.35–0.45 µm in production; standard from about 0.25 µm

7.2 Problems and fixes, and the process flows

LOCOS (M15 §1.1, after Plummer)

  1. Pad oxide grown (25–40 nm, Wolf; ~40 nm, Plummer): buffers the nitride's stress.
  2. Silicon nitride deposited (~80 nm) and patterned over the future active areas: the oxidation mask.
  3. Field oxidation, e.g. ~500 nm in H₂O at 1000 °C for 90 min: oxide grows only where the nitride is open, 0.46 below and 0.54 above the surface, with a beak under each nitride edge.
  4. Nitride stripped in hot phosphoric acid, which leaves the oxide.

BOX-II (notes p. 132; Wolf p. 435; M15 §2.1)

  1. Trenches 0.5–0.8 µm deep, orientation-dependent silicon etch: sidewalls at about 60° (the notes write "isotropically"; see 7.4).
  2. Channel-stop implant: the sloped walls receive enough dose to prevent weak inversion of the sidewall.
  3. Thermal oxide 20–50 nm: passivates the trench walls.
  4. CVD oxide deposited to fill the trenches.
  5. Two resist layers for planarity, then a plasma that erodes resist and SiO₂ at the same rate: oxide left only in the recesses.

Baseline STI, 0.25 µm and below, 21 steps (notes pp. 135–139; Wolf pp. 446–455; M15 §3)

  1. Pad oxide 10–20 nm, thermal, on the cleaned bare wafer: keeps the nitride from stressing the silicon.
  2. LPCVD nitride 100–200 nm: trench-etch hard mask and CMP stop; also on the wafer back.
  3. Mask #1: resist patterned to open the trench regions.
  4. Hard-mask etch: nitride and pad oxide etched anisotropically in a plasma, usually a dielectric etcher.
  5. Trench etch: plasma, HBr/Cl₂/O₂ or HBr/NF₃/He/O₂; slope 70–85°, rounded bottom corners, smooth low-damage walls, ~0.5 µm deep (timed).
  6. Resist strip (the notes' drawing strips it before the silicon etch; see 7.4).
  7. Pad-oxide undercut: short HF dip pulls the pad back under the nitride, so the liner rounds the corner better.
  8. Liner oxidation: dry O₂, 40–50 nm, best as RTO at 1050–1100 °C: removes damage, passivates, rounds the top corner; converts a skin of the nitride to oxide.
  9. Fill: LPCVD TEOS, SACVD O₃–TEOS or HDP-CVD; 8000–9000 Å for a 5000 Å trench (blanket, also over the nitride).
  10. Densify at ~900 °C: HF etch rate brought near thermal oxide's, so the later dips do not remove the fill.
  11. Reverse mask: resist covering the trenches (the reverse of the active pattern), with a planarizing resist spun over it.
  12. Etchback: RIE removes resist and oxide at equal rates, thinning the oxide over the actives: shorter CMP, less dishing.
  13. Resist strip: oxide spikes remain at the active edges (reverse mask drawn slightly small).
  14. CMP to the nitride stop, removing the spikes; wide trenches may dish.
  15. Nitride strip: short HF dip (converted oxide skin, residual oxide), then hot H₃PO₄.
  16. Backside nitride removed by the same H₃PO₄: wet strip is cheap, selective to oxide and silicon.
  17. Pad-oxide strip in HF (etches trench oxide too).
  18. Sacrificial oxide ~20 nm: protects against implant contamination; consumes nitrided silicon at the active edge.
  19. Wells by high-energy implants, with channel stop under the trench, VT-adjust and punch-through implants: no long drive-in.
  20. Sacrificial-oxide strip in HF (etches the trench oxide again; with step 17, the divot).
  21. Gate oxidation.

Lecture 17 drew a shortened preview of this flow: oxidize (by wet oxidation, in that lecture's words), etch the trench through a mask, fill with high-density CVD oxide, polish, then form the wells (M15 §3).

RELACS corner rounding (notes pp. 145–146; Wolf pp. 470–472; M15 §6.3)

  1. Resist exposed and developed as usual (KrF resist in the notes' figure).
  2. RELACS agent coated over the resist pattern.
  3. Bake: the agent crosslinks where it touches the resist, through acid from the resist sidewalls.
  4. Water rinse removes the uncrosslinked agent: a thin layer stays on the sidewalls, every opening slightly narrower than printed.
  5. Nitride opened through the narrowed openings.
  6. Short silicon etch undercuts the nitride edge: rounded top corner.
  7. Rest of the trench etched anisotropically (the notes say isotropic; see 7.4).

HDP-CVD gap fill, as a sequence (notes pp. 141–142; M15 §7.2)

  1. SiH₄, O₂, Ar (or He) at below 10 mTorr; high-density source excites O₂ and Ar; wafer bias draws ions normal to the surface.
  2. O₂ + SiH₄ deposit SiO₂ everywhere; Ar⁺ sputters fastest at ~45°, so 45° facets form at the trench mouth and stay open.
  3. Net growth on the flat field and the trench bottom: the trench fills bottom-up; sputtered material redeposits on the walls.
  4. Ridges and spikes left on the surface are removed by the CMP.

The chapter was not worked at the board: STI had not been lectured when M15 was built, so every problem above is constructed on the notes' and Wolf's values.

7.4 Where the course and the literature differ

PointThe course's version (use it in answers)The literature or the notes' source§
Bird's beak and STILecture 4: "whenever you do the shallow trench isolation you get these sharp corners. These are called bird's beak" — a slip in naming; the same lecture credited STI with removing the beakThe bird's beak is LOCOS's; STI has none, only a ~20 nm liner consumption, and its corner is sharp and must be rounded (Wolf; Plummer)M15 §1.2
BOX trench etchNotes p. 132: "isotropically etched… through orientation dependent Si-etch"Wolf: "orientation-dependent silicon-etch", no "isotropically"; such an etch is anisotropic by definitionM15 §2.1
RELACS trench etchNotes p. 146: the rest of the trench "is etched with an isotropic etch"; M15 teaches anisotropicWolf p. 470: "with an anisotropic etch"M15 §6.3
HDP ratioNotes p. 142 (and Wolf's Fig. 9-20 caption): "deposition-to-etch ratio… between 0.14 and 0.33"; quote the notes' wording, read it as etch-to-depositionA D/E below 1 means net etching. As E/D: 67–86 % of what deposits stays, deposition 3.0–7.1× sputtering. Wolf p. 462: deposition to removal "no larger than 3.0"; the direction of Wolf's ratio is not settled by the source; industry usage fits E/DM15 §7.2
Nitride clippingNot in the notesWolf: "decrease the deposition-to-(sputter)-etching ratio" at a cost in gap fill; physically less sputtering clips less, consistent only with the E/D readingM15 §7.2
Future trench depthNotes p. 139: "decrease to 0.2 µm"Wolf p. 456: "< 0.2-µm, at least for some circuit applications"M15 §4
When the resist is strippedNotes' step list (p. 136): resist stripped after the trench etch (step 6)The notes' own drawing (p. 136), "Remove PR, Si trench etch", and Wolf's preferred two-etcher process strip it after the hard-mask etch, the nitride alone masking the silicon; Plummer allows eitherM15 §3.1
Liner temperatureNotes p. 136: "Temp 1050–1100 °C" on the 40–50 nm baseline linerWolf: 40–50 nm in dry O₂, maximum rounding with an RTO at 1050–1100 °C; Plummer: 10–20 nm at ≈ 1100 °CM15 §3.2, §6.1

Densification effect~900 °C densify so the fill etches near thermal oxide's rate (notes p. 137)Wolf's text: about 20 % lower wet-etch rate; his Table 9-1: 5 → 1.3 (SACVD), 2.1 → 1.1 (LPCVD TEOS), HDP unchanged at 1.1 — film-dependentM15 §3.3
Hydrogen-anneal roundingNot in the notesWolf's text 1100 °C; his Fig. 9-18 caption 950 °C, 380 Torr, 60 sM15 §6.1
End of LOCOSLecture 16: LOCOS no longer used in current technologiesWolf: the workhorse until the 0.35 µm generationM15 §1.4

Which way up is the HDP ratio?

Taken literally, the notes' deposition-to-etch ratio of 0.14–0.33 removes material 3 to 7 times faster than it arrives, so no film would grow. Quote the notes' figures and wording, and explain the fill with the reading under which they make sense: sputtering is 0.14–0.33 of the deposition rate on flat surfaces (M15 §7.2).

7.5 Self-check

Choose one. Which trench-fill technology keeps the trench mouth open by sputtering the deposit at the top corners while it deposits? — (a) LPCVD TEOS (b) HDP-CVD (c) SACVD ozone–TEOS (d) hydrogen-silsesquioxane spin-on glass

(b). Argon ions, drawn normal to the wafer at below 10 mTorr, sputter fastest at about 45°, so the facets at the mouth erode while the flat bottom grows: the trench fills from the bottom up. See M15 §7.2.

Choose one. In the sidewall parasitic MOSFET of STI, what serves as the gate oxide? — (a) the 40–50 nm liner alone (b) the transistor's gate oxide at the corner (c) the trench fill, with a thickness equal to the trench width (d) the pad oxide under the nitride

(c). The positively biased n-well is the gate, the trench oxide the gate oxide, its thickness the trench width (notes p. 134); the p-type sidewall across the trench is the channel. See M15 §5.2.

Choose one. The trench is made shallower at fixed channel-stop doping. The threshold of the field transistor under a polysilicon line — (a) increases (b) decreases (c) does not change (d) none of the above

(b). The trench depth is tFOX in eq. (8.6); both charge terms scale with it: 4.45 V at 0.5 µm against 1.65 V at 0.2 µm, at 10¹⁶ cm⁻³. See M15 §4.

Choose one. The trench between an n-well and a p-type active region is made narrower. The well voltage needed to invert the p-type sidewall — (a) increases (b) decreases (c) does not change (d) none of the above

(b). A narrower trench is a thinner gate oxide for the sidewall device: Ctr rises and both charge terms of (15.3) fall in proportion, 3.86 V at 1.0 µm to 1.47 V at 0.4 µm at Wolf's conditions. See M15 §5.2.

Choose one. Together, the divot's effects produce threshold-voltage variations of about — (a) ±20 mV (b) ±50 mV (c) ±200 mV (d) ±1 V

(c). Wrap-around, the enhanced reverse narrow-width effect and the fall of VT with divot depth give unacceptably large variations of ±200 mV (notes pp. 144–145). See M15 §6.2.

Choose one. Steps 15, 17 and 20 of the baseline flow are HF dips. In Wolf's account their most significant effect on the trench oxide is — (a) a uniform recess of the whole fill by tens of nanometres (b) the loss of oxide near the top corner of the trench (c) an attack on the thermal liner alone (d) none, since HF does not etch densified oxide

(b). Each dip etches the trench fill as well as its intended film; Wolf allows a recess of the whole fill but says little thickness is lost that way, and the effect that matters is at the top corner, where it opens the divot. See M15 §3.4.

Choose one. Dummy active regions are also placed around isolated active areas because — (a) isolated areas polish more slowly than dense ones (b) dense regions polish more slowly, so the nitride over an isolated area would be polished through (c) they lower the coupling capacitance of lines over the trenches (d) they make the trench etch faster

(b). Dense regions polish more slowly than isolated ones, so without dummies the nitride over an isolated active area is polished through. Within wide trenches the dummies also make the trenches effectively narrower. (c) is backwards: dummies make that capacitance variable. See M15 §8.

Choose one. A LOCOS field oxide 600 nm thick stands how high above the active silicon after the nitride strip? — (a) 162 nm (b) 276 nm (c) 324 nm (d) 600 nm

(c). By (15.1), 0.54 × 600 = 324 nm lies above the original surface; 276 nm is the silicon consumed below it. See M15 §1.1.

Compare LOCOS and STI at the edge of the field oxide, at the surface and at narrow spacings, and say where the difficulty of isolation moved — with a sketch of each structure.

LOCOS grows the field oxide by oxidizing the silicon through openings in a nitride mask over a pad oxide. Two consequences of growing it could not be scaled away. (1) The bird's beak: the oxidant diffuses sideways through the pad oxide and oxidizes under the nitride edge, lifting it, so a tapered wedge of field oxide takes active width from each side; the encroachment is proportional to the field-oxide thickness, a fraction of a micrometre for 0.3–0.5 µm oxides, and the oxide cannot be thinned because its thickness sets the field threshold. (2) Non-planarity: 0.46 of the oxide lies below the original surface and 0.54 above, a step of 270 nm for 500 nm. The notes add field-oxide thinning in narrow openings and stress-induced defects, and Wolf channel-stop encroachment. STI etches a trench through a nitride hard mask, grows only a 40–50 nm liner, fills it with deposited oxide (HDP-CVD) and polishes it flat by CMP stopping on the nitride: nothing is grown under the mask except the liner, so there is no beak; the oxide is fully recessed, so the surface is planar; and a deposited oxide does not thin in narrow spaces. Its own costs are more steps and new problems at the sidewall and the sharp top corner. So the difficulty moved: LOCOS's problems came from growing the oxide, STI's from the geometry of an etched hole — filling it, planarizing it, and the corner and sidewall where a gate or a well meets it.

Full treatment: M15 §1 and §9.

How is shallow trench isolation made in the baseline process, step by step?
  1. Grow a 10–20 nm pad oxide on the bare wafer (stress buffer); deposit 100–200 nm of LPCVD nitride (hard mask and polish stop).
  2. Pattern mask #1; etch nitride and pad anisotropically; plasma-etch the silicon trench (HBr-based, 70–85°, rounded bottom, ~0.5 µm, timed); strip the resist.
  3. Undercut the pad oxide with a short HF dip; grow a 40–50 nm dry-O₂ liner, best by RTO at 1050–1100 °C: it removes etch damage, passivates the walls and rounds the top corner.
  4. Fill with LPCVD TEOS, SACVD ozone–TEOS or HDP-CVD oxide, 8000–9000 Å for a 5000 Å trench; densify at ~900 °C so the fill resists the later HF dips.
  5. Reverse mask plus planarizing resist; RIE etchback of the oxide over the actives; strip the resist (spikes remain at the active edges).
  6. CMP to the nitride stop, removing the spikes; wide trenches may dish.
  7. Short HF dip (oxide skin converted on the nitride by the liner oxidation), then hot H₃PO₄ for the nitride, front and back.
  8. Strip the pad oxide in HF; grow a ~20 nm sacrificial oxide; implant the wells, the channel stop under the trench, the VT-adjust and punch-through layers at high energy; strip the sacrificial oxide in HF; grow the gate oxide.

Full treatment: M15 §3.

The sidewall MOSFET and the corner device: what each is, why the sidewall is worse in p-wells, and the fixes — with a sketch of the sidewall device.

The sidewall MOSFET is a horizontal parasitic transistor across the trench (notes p. 134): the n-well at +VDD is the gate, the trench oxide the gate oxide with a thickness equal to the trench width, and the p-type sidewall facing it the channel. Inverted, it joins the source and drain of an n-channel transistor at the trench edge whatever that transistor's gate does, raising its off-state leakage, or joins an n⁺ source to the n-well. Its threshold is eq. (15.3), linear in the trench width, so narrow trenches invert: at Wolf's simulated conditions a 3 V well inverts the sidewall below about 0.8 µm. In a p-well two effects lower the threshold: the positive interface charge favours inversion of p-type silicon, and growing the liner that keeps that charge low draws boron into the oxide by segregation, lowering NA. Fixes: channel-stop doping near the well edge; moving devices back or widening the trench (both cost spacing); a low-tilt boron sidewall implant at 8°, four rotations; Davari's selective diffusion from p⁺ poly or BSG; and, at 0.25 µm and below, wells implanted after the STI, so no boron is present during the liner oxidation and the p-well implant leaves channel-stop boron under the trench. The corner device is the top corner of the active mesa under the gate: fields from the top and the side converge, the corner inverts first and acts as a narrow, lower-threshold transistor in parallel, seen as a subthreshold hump. Thinned corner gate oxide and a recessed (divoted) field oxide make it worse; a 50 nm corner radius with a slope of at most 80° removes it.

Full treatment: M15 §5.2 and §5.3.

What is the divot, what does it do to the transistor, and how do corner rounding and RELACS bear on it?

The divot is a recess in the trench oxide at the top corner, where the fill meets the active edge, left by the HF dips that strip the pad oxide and the sacrificial oxide (steps 17 and 20), which etch the fill as well. The notes give four effects: the gate wraps around the corner and raises the subthreshold leakage (the hump); the transistor behaves as one with recessed isolation, which enhances the reverse narrow-width effect, the fall of VT as the channel narrows; VT falls as the divot deepens, and together these give unacceptable variations of ±200 mV; and the rounded edge encroaches under the nitride and reduces the electrical width, a large fraction near 0.1 µm. The baseline rounds the corner with the liner, an RTO in dry O₂ at 1050–1100 °C, because at high temperature the oxide flows and relaxes the compressive stress that otherwise retards oxidation at the apex; but the liner consumes 0.46 of its thickness from the wall under the nitride, 41.4 nm of width for a 45 nm liner. RELACS rounds the corner during the etch instead: a crosslinked layer narrows the resist openings, the nitride is opened narrower than printed, and a short silicon etch rounds the corner under the nitride inside that margin before the anisotropic trench etch. Wolf reports no encroachment, less poly wrap-around and less wafer-to-wafer VT variation, with a corner radius of about 15 nm.

Full treatment: M15 §6.

A thinner LOCOS oxide. A LOCOS process grows a 450 nm field oxide (an illustrative value inside Wolf's 0.3–0.5 µm). How far does it extend below and above the original surface, and how high is the step at the active edge once the nitride is stripped?

Silicon consumed. By (15.1), 0.46 × 450 nm = 207 nm below the original surface.

Oxide above. 450 − 207 = 243 nm (0.54 × 450 nm).

207 nm below, 243 nm above: a 243 nm step at the active edge

Check. 207 + 243 = 450 nm. Against 500 nm, the step falls by 27 nm, in proportion to the oxide — and so would the bird's beak, which is why LOCOS could not trade one for the field threshold.

Method: M15 §1.1; formula sheet (15.1).

A thinner liner in a tighter trench. Illustrative values inside the notes' ranges: pad oxide 10 nm, nitride 120 nm, a trench 350 nm wide and 400 nm deep, liner 40 nm. Find the silicon consumed per sidewall, the active width lost and its share of a 200 nm active area, and the width, depth and aspect ratio of the gap the fill must close.

Per sidewall. 0.46 × 40 = 18.4 nm consumed; 40 − 18.4 = 21.6 nm grows into the trench.

Active width lost. 2 × 18.4 = 36.8 nm under the nitride; 36.8/200 = 18.4 % of a 200 nm active area.

Gap width. 350 + 36.8 − 2 × 40 = 306.8 nm.

Gap depth. From the nitride top, 120 + 10 + 400 = 530 nm, plus 18.4 nm consumed at the bottom, less the 40 nm liner: 508.4 nm.

Aspect ratio. 508.4/306.8 = 1.66, against 400/350 = 1.14 for the bare trench and 530/350 = 1.51 with the mask stack.

18.4 nm per wall; 36.8 nm of active width (18.4 % at 200 nm); gap 307 × 508 nm, aspect ratio 1.66

Check. M15's 45 nm liner in a 400 × 500 nm trench gives 20.7 nm, 41.4 nm and 1.82: a thinner liner takes less width, and a narrower, shallower trench with a thinner stack ends at a lower aspect ratio. The loss is fixed in nanometres, so its share grows as the active area shrinks.

Method: M15 §3.2; formula sheet (15.1).

Overpolish with a thicker overburden. Illustrative values: the polish must clear 700 nm of oxide from the nitride and runs 25 % beyond the clearing time. Find the nitride lost with a slurry of selectivity 4:1 and with a ceria slurry at 200:1, the nitride left of 120 nm at 4:1, and the loss at 4:1 if an etchback first leaves 300 nm over the actives.

Overpolish. 0.25 × 700 = 175 nm of oxide-equivalent removal.

Nitride lost. By (15.2), 175/4 = 43.8 nm at 4:1; 175/200 = 0.875 nm with ceria.

Nitride left. 120 − 43.8 = 76.2 nm at 4:1.

With the etchback. 0.25 × 300/4 = 18.8 nm.

43.8 nm at 4:1 (76.2 nm left), 0.875 nm with ceria, 18.8 nm after an etchback to 300 nm

Check. The loss is proportional to tclear/S: the selectivity cuts it 50-fold, the etchback by 700/300 = 2.33. Only the etchback also shortens the time the wide trenches spend under the pad, so only it reduces dishing (M15's 850 nm case: 33.3, 0.68 and 9.8 nm).

Method: M15 §3.3; formula sheet (15.2).

A 0.3 µm trench. An n⁺ polysilicon line crosses trench oxide 0.3 µm deep (illustrative, between the baseline 0.5 µm and the projected 0.2 µm); p-type silicon under it at 10¹⁶ cm⁻³, or 5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³ with channel-stop boron; Ni = 10¹¹ cm⁻²; M08's constants. Find the field threshold in each case, whether it clears a 3.3 V and a 1.8 V supply, and the capacitance of a line 0.18 µm wide and 20 µm long.

Capacitance per area. CFOX = 3.9 × 8.85×10⁻¹⁴/(0.3×10⁻⁴) = 1.15×10⁻⁸ F/cm².

Charge terms. They scale with the depth, 0.6 of M15's 0.5 µm values: Qi/CFOX = 1.39 V; |Qd|/CFOX = 4.19 V at 10¹⁶ cm⁻³ and 9.92 V at 5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³.

At 10¹⁶ cm⁻³. φF = 0.347 V, φMS = −0.907 V: VT,field = −0.907 − 1.39 + 4.19 + 0.695 = 2.59 V.

At 5×10¹⁶ cm⁻³. φF = 0.389 V, φMS = −0.949 V: −0.949 − 1.392 + 9.916 + 0.778 = 8.35 V.

Line capacitance. 1.15×10⁻⁸ F/cm² = 0.115 fF/µm²; × 0.18 × 20 = 3.6 µm² gives 0.414 fF.

2.59 V without a channel stop (clears 1.8 V, not 3.3 V); 8.35 V with it (clears both); 0.414 fF per line

Check. The results lie between M15's 0.5 µm (4.45 V, 14.0 V, 0.249 fF) and 0.2 µm (1.65 V, 5.51 V, 0.621 fF) cases; the capacitance is 0.5/0.3 = 1.67 times the 0.5 µm value.

Method: M15 §4; formula sheet (8.6).

How wide must the trench be? Wolf's simulated conditions as in M15 (sidewall NA 9×10¹⁴ cm⁻³, 5×10¹⁰ cm⁻², n-well +3 V, VSB = 1 V; n-well 10¹⁷ cm⁻³, illustrative). Find VT,sw for trenches 0.6 and 0.8 µm wide (illustrative) and the narrowest trench that holds 3 V.

Width-independent terms. φMS = −0.692 V; 2φFp = 0.570 V (M15 §5.2).

Charge terms. At 1.0 µm, 2.32 V and 6.30 V; they scale with the width: at 0.6 µm 1.39 V and 3.78 V; at 0.8 µm 1.86 V and 5.04 V.

Thresholds. 0.6 µm: −0.692 − 1.39 + 3.78 + 0.570 = 2.27 V. 0.8 µm: −0.692 − 1.86 + 5.04 + 0.570 = 3.06 V.

Width for 3 V. VT,sw rises 3.98 V/µm; from 1.47 V at 0.4 µm, 0.4 + (3 − 1.469)/3.978 = 0.785 µm.

2.27 V at 0.6 µm (inverts at 3 V), 3.06 V at 0.8 µm (holds); the sidewall holds 3 V above about 0.785 µm

Check. The threshold is linear in the width between M15's 1.47 V at 0.4 µm and 3.86 V at 1.0 µm, and the crossing matches M15's ~0.78 µm. Widening costs spacing, which is why the fix is sidewall doping or wells implanted after STI.

Method: M15 §5.2; formula sheet (15.3).

Linear against rotary, new tool settings. Illustrative settings: a linear polisher at 400 ft/min and 4 psi, a rotary polisher at 30 ft/min and 6 psi. Find the ratio of the Preston products Pv, and the ratios of pressure and velocity, linear to rotary.

Preston products. Linear 4 × 400 = 1600 psi·ft/min; rotary 6 × 30 = 180 psi·ft/min.

Ratio. 1600/180 = 8.89.

Pressure and velocity. 4/6 = 0.667; 400/30 = 13.3.

The linear tool removes oxide 8.89 times as fast at 0.667 of the pressure

Check. Inside M15's 3.8–10.5 range for Wolf's settings. The velocity speeds the recessed trench oxide as much as the overburden, so it does not by itself reduce dishing; the lower pressure, which the flexing pad transmits into the trench, does. Preston's law is not exact (M13 §6).

Method: M15 §8; formula sheet (13.8).

Summary

  • Chapter 4: high-k means k above Si₃N₄'s 7 in the notes. EOT = thigh-k(3.9/k), and in a stack the SiO₂ interlayer counts at full thickness, so the lowest-k layer sets the floor. Tunnelling falls with physical thickness, but the larger k comes with a smaller band gap and offset; HfO₂ and its silicates won over Ta₂O₅ and ZrO₂, and a metal gate removes polysilicon depletion at a cost in threshold voltage. The DRAM cell stays near 30 fF: three-dimensional cells, thinner dielectrics and HSG, then Ta₂O₅ (k 25) and BST (k 90 in films), with platinum-group electrodes; FeRAM uses ferroelectric PZT and SBT.
  • Chapter 5, wells: diffused wells (50–100 keV, 12–24 h above 1100 °C, 3–4 µm deep, lateral spread 0.7 times the depth) gave way to retrograde wells (300 keV–1 MeV, peak near 1 µm, short anneal), implanted after the field oxide in a chain from the highest energy down: well, punch-through/channel stop, threshold. The bottom-heavy well suppresses latch-up and vertical punch-through and lowers junction capacitance.
  • Chapter 5, channel and source/drain: below about 0.13 µm a uniform channel cannot satisfy both the 5×10¹⁷ cm⁻³ punch-through floor and the surface mobility ceiling; indium and arsenic or antimony give a super-steep retrograde profile, and tilted pocket implants put the doping at the channel ends. The source/drain is a shallow extension plus a deep silicided contact; its four resistances must stay near 10 % of the channel's; sub-keV implants and spike anneals make the junction shallow; the elevated source/drain moves the silicide above it.
  • Chapters 6–7: F = 0.5λ/NA and DOF = 0.6λ/NA²; the wavelength fell from 436 and 365 nm (mercury lamp) to 248, 193 and 157 nm (excimer lasers), each step needing a new resist (DNQ/novolac, then chemically amplified resists); ARCs suppress reflections; phase-shift masks, off-axis illumination and OPC lower k₁; below the optical limit come EUV at 13.5 nm on Mo/Si mirrors, X-ray proximity printing and electron beams, which write the masks.
  • Chapter 8: CMP gives global planarization (β 0.90–0.95), measured by RR, DOP, non-uniformity and %EOP and described by Preston's RR = KpPv. Oxide is hydrated and plowed off; a metal is oxidized, abraded from the up-areas and re-passivated. Copper is inlaid by damascene (barrier, seed, plate, polish copper, change slurry, polish barrier), which dishes wide lines and erodes dense arrays. Copper and low-k insulators cut the RC delay through the product ρk.
  • Chapter 9: LOCOS's bird's beak, step, thinning and stress ended it near 0.35 µm. Shallow trench isolation etches a trench through a pad-oxide/nitride mask, lines it with 40–50 nm of thermal oxide, fills it by HDP-CVD and polishes it to the nitride; its problems are the sidewall and corner devices, the divot, void-free fill and dishing in wide trenches.
